**5.1.1**  Then he, the crusher of foes, resolved to seek the track of Sītā who had been carried off by Rāvaṇa, on the path frequented by the Cāraṇas.

**5.1.2**  Then the mighty, steadfast one roamed at ease over the verdant meadows colored like vaidūrya gems, which were as level as water.

**5.1.3**  Terrifying the birds, smashing trees with his chest, slaying many deer — he was like a fully grown lion.

**5.1.4**  The mountain was adorned with variegated mineral ores of their natural hues — blue, red, crimson, lotus-pink, white, and black.

**5.1.5**  It was thronged constantly, along with their retinues, by Yakṣas, Kiṃnaras, Gandharvas, and serpent-beings resembling gods, all assuming forms at will.

**5.1.6**  Standing at the foot of that foremost mountain, surrounded by lordly elephants, the best of monkeys shone there like an elephant in a lake.

**5.1.7**  Making añjali to Sūrya, to Mahendra, to Pavana, to Svayambhū, and to all beings, he made up his mind to depart.

**5.1.8**  Facing east, making añjali to Pavana, his own progenitor, he then grew in order to proceed south — the capable one toward the southern quarter.

**5.1.9**  Seen by the foremost of leaping monkeys, resolved upon the crossing, he expanded for the benefit of Rāma's cause, as the ocean swells at the full moon.

**5.1.10**  His body boundless, intent on leaping across the ocean, he pressed the mountain down with his arms and with his feet.

**5.1.11**  The lovely mountain, pressed by the monkey, trembled for a moment and shook all the blossoms from the flowering treetops.

**5.1.12**  Covered on all sides by that fragrant flood of flowers shed by the trees, the mountain shone as though it were made entirely of blossoms.

**5.1.13**  Pressed by that one of supreme valor, the mountain oozed forth water, like an elephant in rut exuding ichor.

**5.1.14**  Pressed by that mighty one, mount Mahendra gave forth streams of gold, antimony, and silver, and the mountain discharged great slabs of rock along with slabs of soapstone.

**5.1.15**  As the mountain was compressed, the creatures dwelling in its caves, pressed on all sides, cried out with strange and terrible sounds.

**5.1.16**  That great tumult of beings, arising from the mountain's compression, filled the earth and the directions and the groves on every side.

**5.1.17**  Serpents with wide, clearly marked svastika-signs on their heads, vomiting terrible fire, bit the rocks with their fangs.

**5.1.18**  Those great slabs of rock, bitten by the enraged, venomous serpents, blazed up kindled with fire and split into thousands of fragments.

**5.1.19**  Even the masses of herbs born on that mountain, though antidotes to serpent-venom, could not quell the poison of those Nāgas.

**5.1.20**  Thinking "the mountain is being shattered by these beings," the ascetic Vidyādharas, frightened, flew up from it together with their bands of women.

**5.1.21**  Abandoning the golden vessels and seats left behind on the drinking grounds, the precious drinking cups, and the golden water-pots,

**5.1.22**  leaving behind lickable and various edible delicacies, different kinds of meats, bull-hides, and swords with golden hilts,

**5.1.23**  adorned with garlands around their necks, intoxicated, wearing red garlands and unguents, with red eyes and lotus eyes, they took to the sky.

**5.1.24**  The women, wearing necklaces, anklets, armlets, and ornaments, astonished and smiling, stood in the sky together with their lovers.

**5.1.25**  The Vidyādharas and great sages, displaying their great powers, stood together in the sky and gazed upon the mountain.

**5.1.26**  They also heard the words of the self-perfected sages, of the Cāraṇas, and of the Siddhas who stood in the clear sky.

**5.1.27**  "This is Hanūmān, son of Māruta, mountain-like, who wishes to leap across the mighty, swift ocean, the abode of sea-monsters.

**5.1.28**  Wishing to accomplish a most difficult deed for the sake of Rāma and the monkeys, he desires to reach the far shore of the ocean — a shore most difficult to reach."

**5.1.29**  He shook his body hair, and trembled though mountain-like, and let out a great roar like a vast thundercloud.

**5.1.30**  About to leap, he flung his tail — round and covered with hair from base to tip — like the king of birds flinging a serpent.

**5.1.31**  His tail, whirling behind him as he sped at great velocity, looked like a great serpent being carried off by Garuḍa.

**5.1.32**  He braced his arms, which resembled great iron bars; the monkey crouched at the haunches and tensed his feet.

**5.1.33**  Drawing in his arms and likewise his neck, the glorious and valiant one channeled into himself his brilliance, vitality, and heroic energy.

**5.1.34**  Gazing far ahead at the path, his eyes directed upward, gazing at the sky, he held the breath in his heart.

**5.1.35**  Having planted his feet firmly, and drawing in his ears, the mighty Hanūmān — foremost elephant among monkeys, about to spring — spoke these words to the monkeys, the best of monkeys:

**5.1.36**  "As an arrow released by Rāghava, with the force of the wind, would fly, so shall I fly to Laṅkā ruled by Rāvaṇa.

**5.1.37**  If I do not find Janaka's daughter in Laṅkā, I shall go with the same speed to the abode of the gods.

**5.1.38**  Or if I do not find Sītā in the triple heaven after all my efforts, I shall bind the king of the Rākṣasas and bring Rāvaṇa back.

**5.1.39**  In any case I shall return having accomplished my mission — either with Sītā, or I shall uproot Laṅkā together with Rāvaṇa and bring it here."

**5.1.40**  Having spoken thus to the monkeys, Hanūmān, foremost among monkeys and swift of movement, then leapt forth with great speed, without hesitation.

**5.1.41**  As he leapt upward with speed, the trees that clung to the mountain, drawing in all their branches, sprang up on every side.

**5.1.42**  Carrying along the flowering trees — including the intoxicated koyaṣṭibhaka — by the force of his thighs, he moved through the clear sky.

**5.1.43**  The trees borne aloft by the force of his thighs followed the monkey for a moment on his long journey, like kinsmen following a dear one setting out.

**5.1.44**  The śāla trees and other foremost trees, churned up by the force of his thighs, followed Hanūmān like soldiers following a king.

**5.1.45**  Attended by the many trees with their fully-blossomed tops, the monkey Hanūmān, mountain-like, became wondrous to behold.

**5.1.46**  Then the sturdy trees among them sank into the salt water, as if mountains in fear of Mahendra had submerged into the abode of Varuṇa.

**5.1.47**  The monkey, strewn with various blossoms together with their sprouts and buds, cloud-like, shone like a mountain lit with fireflies.

**5.1.48**  Released by his speed, those trees, having shed their blossoms, fell back into the water like friends who have escorted one as far as they can and then returned.

**5.1.49**  That varied, motley mass of flowers from the trees, stirred by the monkey-wind, fell lightly and beautifully upon the ocean.

**5.1.50**  Trailed by the flood of multi-colored blossoms, the monkey shone like a rising cloud adorned with clusters of lightning.

**5.1.51**  The water, scattered with blossoms arising from his speed, looked like the sky adorned with lovely, risen stars.

**5.1.52**  His two arms, stretched out as he moved through the sky, appeared to issue from the mountain's peak like two five-headed serpents.

**5.1.53**  He appeared as if drinking the great ocean with its wave-mass, and the great monkey seemed as if thirsting to consume the sky.

**5.1.54**  His two eyes, gleaming with the brilliance of lightning as he sped along the path of the wind, blazed like two fires on a mountain.

**5.1.55**  The two large, round, tawny eyes of that foremost tawny-eyed one shone like the moon and the sun standing together.

**5.1.56**  His face, reddened by his copper-hued nose, shone like the orb of the sun touched by twilight.

**5.1.57**  The tail of Vāyu's son, whirling as he leapt, blazed in the sky like the banner of Śakra raised aloft.

**5.1.58**  The great, white-toothed son of Anila, most wise, shone with the wheel of his curling tail like the sun with its halo.

**5.1.59**  The great monkey, with his copper-red haunches, blazed like a mountain with a great vein of red ochre exposed.

**5.1.60**  As this lion among monkeys leapt across the ocean, the wind trapped in his armpits roared like a thundercloud.

**5.1.61**  As a meteor issuing from the northern end falls and is seen trailing its wake through the sky, so was that elephant among monkeys.

**5.1.62**  Resembling a flying bird in his darting motion, the extended monkey shone like a great elephant being girthed with a belly-band.

**5.1.63**  With his body above and his deep shadow below on the ocean, the monkey was then like a boat wind-driven upon the sea.

**5.1.64**  Whatever region of the ocean the great monkey traversed, that region appeared as if agitated to frenzy by the force of his body.

**5.1.65**  Striking with his chest the wave-masses of the ocean that were mountain-high, the great monkey sped across at tremendous velocity.

**5.1.66**  The powerful wind created by the monkey and the wind issuing from the clouds greatly shook the ocean with its terrible roar.

**5.1.67**  Dragging the great wave-masses in the salt sea, he surpassed them at great speed, as if counting the waves as he went.

**5.1.68**  Seeing that tiger among monkeys leaping through the sky, the serpents dwelling in the ocean took him for Suparṇa — Garuḍa.

**5.1.69**  The shadow of that lion among monkeys, ten leagues wide and thirty leagues long, became most beautiful upon the waters.

**5.1.70**  His shadow, spread out upon the salt water and following in the wake of Vāyu's son, shone like a dense bank of white clouds.

**5.1.71**  Seeing that monkey leaping swiftly then, the gods, Gandharvas, and Dānavas rained down showers of flowers.

**5.1.72**  The sun did not scorch that lord of monkeys as he leapt; and the wind attended upon him for the accomplishment of Rāma's purpose.

**5.1.73**  The sages praised him as he flew through the heavens, and gods and Gandharvas sang, extolling him of mighty splendor.

**5.1.74**  Nāgas and Yakṣas, Rākṣasas, gods, and birds praised him, suddenly seeing the foremost monkey flying through the sky, free from fatigue.

**5.1.75**  While that tiger among monkeys, Hanūmān, was leaping, the ocean, concerned for the honor of the Ikṣvāku lineage, pondered.

**5.1.76**  "If I do not render assistance to Hanūmān, that king among monkeys, I shall become the subject of reproach from all those who wish to speak.

**5.1.77**  I was enlarged by Sagara, lord of the Ikṣvākus; and this one serves the Ikṣvākus — he ought not to sink in exhaustion.

**5.1.78**  I must arrange matters so that the monkey may rest; and having rested upon me, he shall fly the remainder of the way with ease."

**5.1.79**  Having made this wholesome resolve, the ocean spoke to Maināka of the golden peak, that foremost of mountains, which was submerged in the water.

**5.1.80**  "O foremost of mountains, you were placed here as a bolt-lock by the king of the gods against the hordes of Asuras dwelling in the floor of Pātāla.

**5.1.81**  You stand blocking the door to the unfathomable Pātāla, preventing those whose strength is known from flying up again.

**5.1.82**  You have the power to grow sideways, upward, and downward; therefore I urge you — rise up, O best of mountains.

**5.1.83**  This tiger among monkeys, the valiant and terrible-in-deed Hanūmān, who has leapt into the sky for the purpose of Rāma's mission, passes over you.

**5.1.84**  I must render assistance to him who acts for the Ikṣvāku line. The Ikṣvākus are revered by me, but supremely revered by you.

**5.1.85**  Render us this service; let not our duty go unperformed. A task that ought to be done but is left undone would rouse the indignation of the good.

**5.1.86**  Rise up from the water, let this monkey stand upon you; he is both our guest and worthy of honor — the foremost of those who leap.

**5.1.87**  O Maināka of the great golden peak, attended upon by gods and Gandharvas, once Hanūmān has rested upon you he shall then proceed for the remainder of the way."

**5.1.88**  Considering the gentleness of Kākutstha, the banishment of Maithilī, and the exertion of the lord of leaping monkeys, you ought to rise up.

**5.1.89**  Golden-peaked Maināka, hearing this from the salt sea, quickly sprang up from the water, together with great trees and creepers.

**5.1.90**  He rose exceedingly high, bursting through the sea's water, as the sun with blazing rays bursts through a cloud.

**5.1.91**  With his golden peaks inhabited by Kiṃnaras and great serpents, gleaming like the sunrise, seemingly scratching the sky,

**5.1.92**  as the mountain's golden peaks rose up, the sky became resplendent, gleaming like a golden weapon.

**5.1.93**  With its peaks of pure gold, self-luminous and blazing, that best of mountains became as resplendent as a hundred suns.

**5.1.94**  Hanūmān, seeing it rise suddenly and stand before him in the middle of the salt water, was certain: "This is an obstacle."

**5.1.95**  That great monkey of tremendous speed struck it down with his chest as it stood immensely high — as the wind strikes down a thundercloud.

**5.1.96**  That foremost of mountains, having been dislodged by the monkey, perceived his speed, and thereupon rejoiced and was gladdened.

**5.1.97**  Pleased and with a glad heart, the mountain, assuming a human form and standing upon its own summit, addressed the hero who was moving through the sky.

**5.1.98**  "O best of monkeys, you have accomplished a most difficult deed. Having alighted upon my peaks, rest here at your ease."

**5.1.99**  "The ocean was nurtured by those born in the lineage of Rāghava; therefore that ocean now honours you, who are engaged in Rāma's service."

**5.1.100**  "To return a favour when a favour has been done — this is the eternal law. Thus the ocean, desiring to repay that debt, deserves respect from you."

**5.1.101**  "Urged by deep regard on your account, the ocean has prompted me. This monkey is leaping a hundred yojanas. Let him rest upon your slopes and then proceed with the rest of the journey."

**5.1.102**  "Stay, O tiger among monkeys; rest upon me and then proceed. Here are fragrant, sweet roots and fruits in abundance — taste them, O best of monkeys, and you will depart refreshed."

**5.1.103**  "There is indeed a bond between us as well, O chief of monkeys — a connection renowned throughout the three worlds, and attended by great virtues."

**5.1.104**  "Among all the swift-leaping monkeys, O son of the wind, O elephant among monkeys, I regard you as the foremost."

**5.1.105**  "A guest, even an ordinary one, is worthy of honour by one who understands dharma and seeks to learn it — how much more so one such as yourself!"

**5.1.106**  "For you are the son of Māruta, the greatest of the gods, the noble one; and equal to him in speed, O elephant among monkeys."

**5.1.107**  "When you, O knower of dharma, are honoured, Māruta receives honour thereby. For this reason you are worthy of my reverence — and hear also another reason for it."

**5.1.108**  "Long ago, dear one, in the Kṛtayuga, mountains had wings; they too moved in all directions with the speed of Garuḍa and the wind."

**5.1.109**  "As they roamed about, the hosts of gods together with the seers, and all beings, were seized with fear lest the mountains fall upon them."

**5.1.110**  "Then Sahasrākṣa, Śatakratu, enraged, cut off the wings of the mountains in thousands upon thousands of places with his thunderbolt."

**5.1.111**  "The lord of the gods approached me in fury, raising his thunderbolt; but then I was swiftly hurled away by the great-souled wind-god."

**5.1.112**  "I was cast into these salt waters, O best of monkeys, and there protected, wings concealed and body intact, by your father."

**5.1.113**  "Thus I honour you, for Māruta is honoured by me — and through you, O chief of monkeys, I have this bond, so full of great virtue."

**5.1.114**  "In this matter that concerns both the ocean and myself, you, O great monkey, with a glad heart, ought to gratify us both."

**5.1.115**  "Shake off your fatigue, accept my honour, O best of monkeys, and deem our affection well-placed — I am delighted by your sight."

**5.1.116**  Thus addressed, the best of monkeys replied to that foremost mountain: "I am pleased; hospitality has been rendered — let your displeasure be put aside."

**5.1.117**  "The time for my task presses; the day is advancing; and I have given a vow — I must not halt here in between."

**5.1.118**  Having said this, the bull among monkeys, full of valour, touched the mountain with his hand and entered the sky, as if laughing as he went.

**5.1.119**  The son of the wind-god was gazed upon with deep respect by both the mountain and the ocean, and was honoured with fitting benedictions.

**5.1.120**  Then, soaring upward into the distance, leaving behind the mountain and the great ocean, he took to his father's path and moved through the clear sky.

**5.1.121**  Again gaining an upward course and glancing back at that mountain, the son of Vāyu moved through the clear, unsupported sky.

**5.1.122**  Seeing that second most difficult feat accomplished by Hanūmān, all the gods, the Siddhas, and the great seers extolled him.

**5.1.123**  The divine beings gathered there were filled with joy at his act; and Sahasrākṣa Vāsava himself, along with the golden Sunābha, was pleased.

**5.1.124**  The wise lord of Śacī himself, Śatakratu, with his voice choked by satisfaction, spoke words to Sunābha, the best of mountains.

**5.1.125**  "O Hiraṇyanābha, O lord of mountains, I am deeply gratified with you; I grant you freedom from fear — remain here, good one, at your ease."

**5.1.126**  "You have rendered very great assistance to the valiant Hanūmān as he strides his hundred yojanas, offering fearlessness where danger existed."

**5.1.127**  "This monkey goes as the emissary of Rāma, son of Daśaratha; by rendering him due honour, you have truly pleased me greatly."

**5.1.128**  Then that foremost of mountains experienced immense delight, seeing the well-pleased Śatakratu, lord of the gods.

**5.1.129**  That mountain, thus granted the boon, remained in place; and Hanūmān in a moment had traversed the ocean.

**5.1.130**  Then the gods, together with the Gandharvas, the Siddhas, and the great seers, addressed Surasā, the mother of the Nāgas, who was radiant as the sun.

**5.1.131**  "This glorious son of the wind, named Hanūmān, is flying over the ocean; for a moment, place an obstacle before him."

**5.1.132**  "Assuming a most dreadful rākṣasa form, vast as a mountain, with fangs fearsome and tawny eyes, opening a mouth that touches the sky —"

**5.1.133**  "— we wish to learn yet more of his strength and valour: whether he will overcome you by stratagems or sink into despair."

**5.1.134**  Thus addressed by the gods who had duly honoured her, that goddess Surasā, assuming a rākṣasa form, appeared in the middle of the ocean.

**5.1.135**  Distorted, hideous, and terrifying to all, she intercepted Hanūmān as he flew and spoke these words.

**5.1.136**  "O bull among monkeys, you have been appointed as my food by the lords; I shall devour you — enter this mouth of mine."

**5.1.137**  Thus addressed by Surasā, the glorious bull among monkeys, with joyful countenance and palms joined, spoke these words.

**5.1.138**  "One named Rāma, son of Daśaratha, entered the Daṇḍaka forest together with his brother Lakṣmaṇa and his wife, the Vaideha princess."

**5.1.139**  "Of this one, thus engaged in his duty and having earned the enmity of the rākṣasas, the glorious Sītā, his wife, has been abducted by Rāvaṇa."

**5.1.140**  "I am going, by Rāma's command, as a messenger to her; O you who dwell in this domain, you ought to render Rāma assistance."

**5.1.141**  "Or else, having seen the Maithilī princess and Rāma of unwearied action, I shall come and enter your mouth — I solemnly promise you this."

**5.1.142**  Thus addressed by Hanūmān, the shape-shifting Surasā replied: "None may pass beyond me — this is my boon."

**5.1.143**  Thus addressed by Surasā, the enraged bull among monkeys replied: "Then make your mouth large enough to take me in."

**5.1.144**  Having spoken thus to Surasā, Hanūmān in his fury became ten yojanas in length and ten yojanas in breadth.

**5.1.145**  Seeing him cloud-dark and ten yojanas in extent, Surasā also opened her mouth to twenty yojanas.

**5.1.146**  Then Hanūmān in his fury grew to thirty yojanas; and Surasā made her mouth forty yojanas high.

**5.1.147**  The heroic Hanūmān became fifty yojanas in height; Surasā made her mouth sixty yojanas across.

**5.1.148**  Likewise the heroic Hanūmān rose to seventy yojanas; Surasā made her mouth eighty yojanas wide.

**5.1.149**  Hanūmān, like a motionless mountain, grew to ninety yojanas; Surasā made her mouth a hundred yojanas in extent.

**5.1.150**  Seeing that gaping mouth of Surasā, terrifyingly wide and long-tongued, resembling the entrance to the netherworld, the wise son of Vāyu —

**5.1.151**  — instantly contracted his body; like the wind within a cloud, Hanūmān at that moment became no larger than a thumb.

**5.1.152**  He swiftly darted into that mouth, then with great speed shot out of it, and standing in the sky, the glorious one spoke these words.

**5.1.153**  "O daughter of Dakṣa, I have entered your mouth — homage to you. I shall go to where the Vaideha princess is; and let your words prove true."

**5.1.154**  Seeing him freed from her mouth as the moon from the mouth of Rāhu, the goddess Surasā, in her own form, addressed the monkey.

**5.1.155**  "For the success of your purpose, O best of monkeys, go, gentle one, at your ease; and reunite the Vaideha princess with the great-souled Rāghava."

**5.1.156**  Seeing that third most difficult feat of Hanūmān, all living beings thereupon praised the monkey with cries of "Well done! Well done!"

**5.1.157**  He had overcome the irresistible ocean, the abode of Varuṇa, and entered the sky, moving with speed like Garuḍa.

**5.1.158**  He traversed the sky frequented by cloud-bearers and birds, visited by celestial bards, and coursed by Airāvata.

**5.1.159**  The sky was adorned with shining aerial cars flying about, carrying those borne by lions, elephants, tigers, birds, and serpents.

**5.1.160**  The sky was beautified by fires blazing like the crash of thunderbolts, and adorned by the meritorious and fortunate who had conquered heaven.

**5.1.161**  The sky was frequented by the many-rayed fire bearing oblations without end, and was adorned with planets, constellations, the moon, the sun, and hosts of stars.

**5.1.162**  The sky was filled with bands of great seers, Gandharvas, Nāgas, and Yakṣas; it was clear and pure and wide, frequented by Viśvāvasu.

**5.1.163**  He moved through the sky traversed by the elephant of the king of gods, along the path of the moon and the sun, the auspicious canopy of the world of the living, spread out and fashioned by Brahmā.

**5.1.164**  Frequented on all sides by heroes and by noble hosts of Vidyādharas, great clouds blazed as the monkey drew them in his wake.

**5.1.165**  Darting into masses of clouds and bursting out again and again, he shone like the moon in the rainy season, emerging and entering in turn.

**5.1.166**  But seeing him flying, a rākṣasī named Siṃhikā, enormous and able to assume any form at will, thought within her mind:

**5.1.167**  "Today, after a long time, I shall eat my fill; this great creature has come within my power after so long."

**5.1.168**  Having thought thus in her mind, she seized his shadow. As his shadow was seized, the monkey thought to himself:

**5.1.169**  "I have been suddenly dragged back with my momentum arrested, like a great ship in the ocean struck by a contrary wind."

**5.1.170**  Looking about him to the sides, above, and below, the monkey then saw a massive creature rising from the salt sea.

**5.1.171**  "This is the creature of marvellous appearance, the shadow-seizer of great power, about which the monkey king spoke — there is no doubt."

**5.1.172**  Having truly understood her nature, the wise monkey grew immensely large in body, like a rain-cloud of the wet season.

**5.1.173**  Gazing up at the growing body of that great monkey, she stretched open her mouth to the measure of the sky and the netherworld.

**5.1.174**  Then the wise great monkey saw her hideously vast mouth, and measured her body, and discerned her vital spots.

**5.1.175**  Into her wide-open mouth the monkey, firm as a thunderbolt, abruptly contracted himself and burst out — that one of mighty strength.

**5.1.176**  The Siddhas and Cāraṇas saw him plunging into her mouth as Rāhu swallows the full moon on a lunar day.

**5.1.177**  Then with his sharp nails the monkey tore out her vital organs, and with speed swift as thought he leapt out of her.

**5.1.178**  Seeing Siṃhikā swiftly slain by the monkey and falling, the sky-ranging beings spoke to that bull among monkeys:

**5.1.179**  "A terrible deed has been done today; you have slain a mighty creature. Now accomplish your desired purpose — safe journey, O best of the leapers!"

**5.1.180**  "He who possesses these four qualities as you do, O lord of monkeys — fortitude, foresight, intelligence, and dexterity — does not falter in his undertakings."

**5.1.181**  Honoured and revered by them, his purpose well in hand, the monkey entered the sky like the devourer of serpents — Garuḍa.

**5.1.182**  Having nearly reached the far shore, glancing about in all directions, he saw a line of forest at the end of the hundred yojanas.

**5.1.183**  And even as he flew, that best of tree-branch-dwellers saw an island adorned with varied trees, and the groves of the Malaya range.

**5.1.184**  He beheld the ocean, the ocean shores, the trees growing on those shores, and even the faces of the ocean's consorts.

**5.1.185**  The wise one, reflecting upon himself — vast as a great cloud, as if blocking the sky — resolved within his mind:

**5.1.186**  "The rākṣasas, upon seeing my great size and speed, will take an undue interest in me" — so the great monkey reasoned.

**5.1.187**  Thereupon he contracted that mountain-like body and returned to his natural form, like one free of delusion — composed and self-possessed.

**5.1.188**  He who could assume many beautiful forms, having reached the far shore of the ocean, wore a form that none of his enemies could anticipate — he had reflected upon himself and surveyed his purpose.

**5.1.189**  Then that great soul, rivalling the peak of a mighty mountain, alighted upon the richly adorned, variegated summit of Mount Lamba, with its ketakī, uddālaka, and nālikera trees.

**5.1.190**  Having by his power traversed that ocean filled with Dānavas and Nāgas, garlanded with great waves, and having alighted upon the shore of the great sea, he then beheld Laṅkā, like Amarāvatī.

**5.2.1**  Having crossed the irresistible ocean, that mighty one beheld Laṅkā standing on the peak of Trikūṭa, and stood at ease.

**5.2.2**  Then, as he stood there, the valiant one was showered by a rain of flowers shed from the trees, and shone as though made of blossoms.

**5.2.3**  Though the glorious ape of supreme prowess had crossed a hundred yojanas, he did not breathe hard and felt no fatigue there.

**5.2.4**  "I could stride many hundreds of yojanas — how much less this ocean whose end is measured at a hundred yojanas!"

**5.2.5**  He who is the best among the mighty and the foremost of leapers, having vaulted the great ocean, swiftly came to Laṅkā.

**5.2.6**  He passed through dark-green meadows, fragrant forests, groves with prominent peaks, and mountain-bearing tracts.

**5.2.7**  The lustrous Hanūmān, the bull among leapers, passed over hills covered with trees and flowering forest-rows.

**5.2.8**  Standing on that mountain, the son of the wind god beheld forests and groves; and from the mountain's peak he saw Laṅkā.

**5.2.9**  He saw sāra pines, karṇikāra trees, date palms in full bloom, priyāla and muculinda trees, kuṭaja shrubs, and ketaka plants.

**5.2.10**  He saw priyaṅgu vines full of fragrance, nīpa trees, saptacchadā trees, asana trees, kovidāras, and karavīra shrubs in bloom.

**5.2.11**  He saw trees weighed down with loads of flowers, some still in bud, covered with birds, their crowns tossed by the wind.

**5.2.12**  He beheld ponds thronged with haṃsa and kāraṇḍava birds, adorned with lotuses and blue water-lilies, many delightful pleasure-groves, and various reservoirs.

**5.2.13**  The bull among apes saw gardens densely planted with various trees that bore fruit and flowers in every season.

**5.2.14**  Approaching it, the glorious one beheld Laṅkā, guarded by Rāvaṇa, adorned with moats filled with lotuses and blue water-lilies.

**5.2.15**  It was well-guarded by Rāvaṇa on account of his abduction of Sītā, and patrolled on all sides by rākṣasas carrying fierce bows.

**5.2.16**  The great city was encircled by a golden rampart, beautiful and covered with hundreds of watchtowers, garlanded with banners and standards.

**5.2.17**  Hanūmān saw Laṅkā with its divine golden gateways embellished with rows of creepers — like a city of the gods in heaven.

**5.2.18**  The glorious ape beheld Laṅkā set on the mountain's summit, with white and beautiful mansions — like a city floating in the sky.

**5.2.19**  Hanūmān saw the city protected by the lord of rākṣasas, built by Viśvakarman, appearing as though it floated in the sky.

**5.2.20**  Filled with terrible rākṣasas and serpents like Bhogavatī, inconceivable, well-built, splendid — formerly inhabited by Kubera himself.

**5.2.21**  It was guarded by many fanged warriors, terrible rākṣasas holding spears and battle-axes in their hands, like a cave of venomous serpents.

**5.2.22**  Its hips were the earthen rampart and walls; its robe was the great mass of water; its locks were tipped with śataghnī engines and spears; its ornament was the row of watchtowers.

**5.2.23**  Approaching the northern gate, the monkey reflected — it was like the peak of Kailāsa, as if scratching the sky, as if holding the firmament aloft with its towering mansions.

**5.2.24**  Having surveyed its mighty defenses and the ocean, and reflecting upon Rāvaṇa the terrible enemy, the monkey fell into thought.

**5.2.25**  "Even if the monkeys came here, they would be in vain. Laṅkā cannot be conquered in battle even by the gods."

**5.2.26**  "Even having reached this inaccessible fortress, Laṅkā protected by Rāvaṇa — what will the long-armed Rāghava do?"

**5.2.27**  "No scope for conciliation is found among the rākṣasas; no scope for bribery or sowing dissension is visible; nor even for battle."

**5.2.28**  "The passage belongs only to four of the great-souled monkeys: the son of Vāli, Nīla, myself, and the wise king."

**5.2.29**  "Until I learn of Vaidehī — whether she lives or not — I shall deliberate only after seeing Janaka's daughter."

**5.2.30**  Then the bull among apes, devoted to Rāma's welfare, stood for a moment on that mountain peak, deep in thought.

**5.2.31**  "In this form, I cannot enter the city of the rākṣasas, guarded as it is by cruel and powerful rākṣasas."

**5.2.32**  "All these rākṣasas of fierce energy, great valor, and great strength must be deceived by me in my search for Jānakī."

**5.2.33**  "In a form barely visible, at night, I should enter the city of Laṅkā — the time is ripe for me to accomplish this great task."

**5.2.34**  Seeing that city thus — inaccessible to gods and asuras — Hanūmān reflected, sighing again and again.

**5.2.35**  "By what means might I see Maithilī, Janaka's daughter, without being seen by Rāvaṇa the evil-souled lord of rākṣasas?"

**5.2.36**  "How should the mission of the self-knowing Rāma not be ruined? And how should I, alone, see Janaka's daughter alone and in private?"

**5.2.37**  "Accomplished purposes collapse when contrary to place and time, and when they meet with a confused envoy — as darkness collapses at sunrise."

**5.2.38**  "Even a settled judgment does not shine at the boundary between advantage and disadvantage; for envoys who fancy themselves wise destroy missions."

**5.2.39**  "How should the mission not be ruined? How should confusion not arise? And how should my crossing of the ocean not be in vain?"

**5.2.40**  "If the rākṣasas see me, this mission of the self-knowing Rāma, who wishes ill to Rāvaṇa, would become fruitless."

**5.2.41**  "It is impossible to remain anywhere without being recognized by the rākṣasas — even in rākṣasa form, much less in any other form."

**5.2.42**  "My belief is that not even the wind moves here unrecognized; for there is nothing unknown to these mighty rākṣasas."

**5.2.43**  "If I remain here disguised in my own form, I shall meet destruction, and my master's cause will suffer."

**5.2.44**  "Therefore, having taken a diminutive form from my own nature, I shall fly into Laṅkā by night, for the accomplishment of Rāghava's purpose."

**5.2.45**  "Entering by night this city of Rāvaṇa so difficult to assault, searching every mansion, I shall find Janaka's daughter."

**5.2.46**  Having thus deliberated, the heroic Hanūmān awaited the setting of the sun, eager to see Vaidehī, and having made himself the size of a mere gnat, he became wondrous to behold.

**5.2.47**  At dusk, the valiant Hanūmān swiftly sprang up and entered the beautiful city with its broad, well-laid avenues.

**5.2.48**  It was spread with rows of palaces, with golden and silver pillars, with lattices of pure gold — resembling the city of the gandharvas.

**5.2.49**  He saw the great city with its seven- and eight-storied buildings, with floors filled with crystal and adorned with pure gold.

**5.2.50**  The mansions of the rākṣasas there shone with floors decorated with vaidūrya gems and adorned with nets of pearls.

**5.2.51**  The golden and variegated gateways of the rākṣasas lit up Laṅkā, which was adorned and embellished on all sides.

**5.2.52**  Beholding Laṅkā, inconceivable and of wondrous form, the great monkey was at once downcast and elated, eager to see Vaidehī.

**5.2.53**  He gazed at it — garlanded with white, soaring aerial pavilions, with gateways latticed with precious jāmbūnada gold, protected by the arms of the glorious Rāvaṇa, surrounded by night-roaming beings of terrible power.

**5.2.54**  And the moon, as though rendering service to Laṅkā, rising resplendent surrounded by hosts of stars, spreading a canopy of moonlight over the world, rose with its thousands upon thousands of rays.

**5.2.55**  That foremost of monkeys saw the moon rising and shining — bright as a conch, white as milk and lotus-fiber — gliding like a haṃsa upon a lake.

**5.3.1**  On a lofty peak, high and resembling a hanging cloud, the wise Hanūmān, son of the wind, composed himself with courage.

**5.3.2**  At night, Hanūmān the great-souled bull among apes entered Laṅkā — the city protected by Rāvaṇa, rich with beautiful groves and waters.

**5.3.3**  It was beautified by mansions resembling autumnal clouds; its roar was like the ocean; it was fanned by the ocean wind.

**5.3.4**  Guarded by a well-nourished army — like Viṭapāvatī herself — with beautiful gateway-arches and white-pillared portals.

**5.3.5**  Frequented by serpentine beings and well-guarded, beautiful — like Bhogavatī — covered with clouds shot through with lightning, and traversed by pathways of luminaries.

**5.3.6**  Resounding with fierce winds like Indra's Amarāvatī, surrounded by a great rampart of pure gold.

**5.3.7**  Adorned with banners ringing with clusters of tiny bells, he approached suddenly, joyful, and reached the rampart.

**5.3.8**  His heart seized with wonder, surveying the city on all sides — with gateways made of jāmbūnada gold and raised platforms fashioned of vaidūrya gems —

**5.3.9**  — adorned with gem-inlaid floors of sapphire, crystal, and pearl; with projecting cornices of refined gold; gleaming silver-white and spotless —

**5.3.10**  — with staircases of vaidūrya, with crystal dust between the steps, fitted with beautiful running-courses, those auspicious mansions seemed to soar into the sky —

**5.3.11**  — resounding everywhere with the cries of krauñca birds and peacocks, frequented by royal haṃsas, reverberating with the sounds of musical instruments and ornaments —

**5.3.12**  Surveying that city matchless as Vasvokasārā, Laṅkā appearing to leap into the sky, the monkey Hanūmān rejoiced.

**5.3.13**  Surveying that city Laṅkā of the lord of rākṣasas — beautiful, unrivaled, full of prosperity — the valiant one fell into thought.

**5.3.14**  "This city cannot be assaulted by force by anyone else, guarded as it is by the armies of Rāvaṇa with weapons raised and ready."

**5.3.15**  "This ground might be within the reach of Kumuda and Aṅgada, of the great monkey Suṣeṇa, and also of Mainda and Dvivida."

**5.3.16**  "It might be within reach of Vivasvat's son, of the monkey Kuśaparvan, of Ṛkṣa and Ketumāla, and of myself as well."

**5.3.17**  Yet reflecting on the valor of the long-armed Rāghava and on Lakṣmaṇa's prowess, the monkey became confident and glad.

**5.3.18**  He saw the city adorned with jewels and garments, crowned with treasure-houses, her breast the arsenal — wealthy, like a great lady adorned with ornaments.

**5.3.19**  The great monkey saw the city of the lord of rākṣasas — its darkness dispelled by lamps and the luminous great mansions.

**5.3.20**  Having entered by night, the son of the wind, full of courage, took the great road adorned and shining with scattered flowers.

**5.3.21**  With sounds of laughter and uproar attended by music, with householders' dwellings resembling thunderbolts and hooks, adorned with lattices of lightning — the beautiful city shone like the sky with storm-clouds.

**5.3.22**  Laṅkā blazed then with the beautiful homes of the rākṣasa hosts — white as clouds, adorned with lotus and svastika patterns, and with expanding mansions eloquently fashioned on all sides.

**5.3.23**  Roaming for Rāghava's sake, the glorious one who works for the welfare of the monkey-king saw that city adorned with varied garlands and ornaments, and was delighted.

**5.3.24**  He heard sweet song adorned with tones from the three registers — from women intoxicated with wine, like the apsarases in heaven.

**5.3.25**  He heard the tinkling of girdles and the ringing of anklets, the sound of footsteps on staircases in the mansions, and here and there the sounds of clapping and hissing.

**5.3.26**  He saw yātudhānas engaged in recitation of the Vedas, and rākṣasas roaring praises to Rāvaṇa.

**5.3.27**  He saw a great body of rākṣasa troops blocking the royal road, and in the central guard-post many scouts of the rākṣasa.

**5.3.28**  He saw some who had taken vows, some matted-haired, some shaven — clothed in cowhides and goatskins, armed with fistfuls of kuśa grass, and others with fire-pots as weapons.

**5.3.29**  He saw those holding crooked clubs, those bearing staff-weapons, one-eyed ones, many-eared ones, and those with swinging, pendulous breasts.

**5.3.30**  He saw terrible ones, disfigured ones, monstrous and dwarfish ones, archers, swordsmen, those armed with śataghnī engines and clubs, those holding great iron bars in their hands — all gleaming in varied armor.

**5.3.31**  He saw those who were neither too stout nor too lean, neither too tall nor too short — some ill-shaped, some of many forms, and some of handsome form and bright complexion.

**5.3.32**  The great monkey also saw those armed with śakti-spears and tree-trunks, those bearing battle-axes and thunderbolts, and those holding slings and nooses in their hands.

**5.3.33**  He saw those wearing garlands and anointed with unguents, adorned with excellent ornaments, those bearing sharp tridents, and those carrying the vajra — all of great power.

**5.3.34**  The great monkey saw the central guard of one hundred thousand, unwavering, completely surrounding the rampart.

**5.3.35**  It was like the divine Triviṣṭapa, filled with divine sounds, resounding with the neighing of horses, reverberating with the music of ornaments.

**5.3.36**  There were chariots, vehicles, aerial cars, auspicious elephants and horses, and four-tusked elephants resembling masses of white cloud.

**5.3.37**  Adorned and with a beautiful gateway, with intoxicated animals and birds — guarded as the mansion of the lord of rākṣasas — the monkey entered.

**5.4.1**  Then that wise one beheld in the sky the Moon, who had reached the zenith, pouring out a great flood of moonlight, blazing with radiance, roaming like a rutting bull in a cattle-pen.

**5.4.2**  He beheld the cool-rayed Moon advancing — destroying the sins of the world, swelling the great ocean, and illuminating all living beings.

**5.4.3**  The beauty that shines on earth when set upon Mandara, and at nightfall when resting upon the ocean, and likewise when dwelling in lotus-pools — that same splendour now blazed, abiding in the lovely Moon.

**5.4.4**  As a swan resting in a silver cage, as a lion couched in a cave of Mandara, as a hero mounted on a proud elephant — so too the Moon blazed, stationed in the sky.

**5.4.5**  Like a great mountain with a sharp summit, like a white peak rising high, like an elephant with its tusks bound in gold — so the Moon shone with its disc perfectly full.

**5.4.6**  The blessed nightfall had its faults dispelled by the bright rising of the Moon; the fault of the flesh-eating Rākṣasas grown mighty; the fault of minds stirred by longing for lovely Rāma — yet it shone like heaven.

**5.4.7**  Sweet sounds of lutes and strings, pleasing to the ear, arose; women of good conduct slept beside their husbands; and the night-wanderers too stirred forth to sport — those of most wondrous and dread disposition.

**5.4.8**  That wise monkey beheld households in tumult with the intoxicated and the heedless, filled with chariots, horses, and fine seats, and thronged with the glory of heroes.

**5.4.9**  They hurled boastful taunts at one another, flinging their stout arms about, uttering the babbling speech of the intoxicated, casting challenges at each other in their drunkenness.

**5.4.10**  Rākṣasas throwing out their broad chests, casting their limbs upon their beloved women — he saw some conversing with their sweethearts, and others there sleeping.

**5.4.11**  It resounded with great elephants trumpeting, with well-worshipped and excellent horses, with heroes heaving sighs — gleaming like a lake breathing with serpents.

**5.4.12**  In that city he saw Yātudhānas — the demon-sorcerers — foremost in intellect, bearing lovely names, revered and regarded as chiefs of the world, of many varieties and with pleasing appellations.

**5.4.13**  He rejoiced to see those of fair form, endowed with varied qualities befitting their own natures, blazing with splendour — those of beautiful form he saw; and then again some others of hideous form.

**5.4.14**  Then he saw the wives of those Rākṣasas — women worthy of the best, with hearts utterly pure, of great majesty, devoted in spirit to their beloved drinks, shining like stars of brilliant radiance.

**5.4.15**  Blazing with beauty, veiled by modesty, in the midnight hour clasped by their lovers — he saw some women embraced by their beloved mates, like birds nestled amid blossoms.

**5.4.16**  Others again were seated on the floors of their mansions, comfortably settled in the laps of their beloved husbands — devoted to virtue, dear to their lords, well-settled in their place — and that wise one saw them possessed of a dignity that confers honour.

**5.4.17**  Some were unclothed, of golden-streak complexion; some were most precious ones of burnished-gold hue; others again were of lunar-spot colouring; and some, deserted by their lovers, were of lovely limb-colour.

**5.4.18**  Then, having obtained their beloved ones who delight the mind, filled with deep joy, looking upon those Rāmas — the lord of monkeys beheld those women, joyful in their homes and supremely beautiful.

**5.4.19**  He saw garlands of faces bright as moonlight, garlands of lovely eyes with curving lashes, garlands of ornaments — like the fair garland of the hundred-flash lightning.

**5.4.20**  But Sītā, of the highest birth, born into the royal line that walks the righteous path, slender as a blossoming vine of noble growth — born of the mind itself — he did not find her.

**5.4.21**  Her who is established in the eternal path, that one worthy to be seen by Rāma, seized by longing, who has entered the glorious mind of her husband — ever distinguished above all excellent women — he did not see.

**5.4.22**  Afflicted by heat, her throat flowing with streaming tears, her throat once adorned with the finest and most precious jewelled pendants, with well-formed lashes, her throat flushed — wandering in the forest like a blue-throated bird — he did not see her.

**5.4.23**  Like the crescent of the moon with its mark obscured; like a line of gold smeared with dust; like the track of an arrow-wound grown over again; like a streak of cloud rent asunder by the wind.

**5.4.24**  Not finding Sītā — wife of Rāma, lord of men, best among the eloquent — the monkey became struck with grief, growing slow and sluggish after long exertion, like a fire robbed of its vigour.

**5.5.1**  Roaming at will among the bending trees, the monkey, assuming whatever form he desired, explored Laṅkā with swiftness and ease.

**5.5.2**  Then that glorious one came upon the palace of the lord of Rākṣasas, entirely enclosed by a wall of sun-like colour and radiant splendour.

**5.5.3**  Guarded by terrible Rākṣasas as a great forest is guarded by lions — surveying that mansion, the elephant among monkeys shone forth with purpose.

**5.5.4**  It was adorned with variegated gateways fitted with silver finials and decorated with gold, surrounded by elaborate courtyards and beautiful doors.

**5.5.5**  It was attended by great elephant-drivers mounted on their elephants, by tireless heroes, by irresistible horses and chariot-riders.

**5.5.6**  It was constantly traversed by chariots with armour made of lion and tiger hide, wrought of ivory, gold, and silver, resonant with noise and richly adorned.

**5.5.7**  It was strewn with many precious gems, filled with vessels of the most valuable seats, the dwelling-place of great warriors, furnished with great thrones for great car-warriors.

**5.5.8**  It was filled on all sides with various most excellent spectacles, and with various beasts and birds in their thousands upon thousands.

**5.5.9**  It was well-guarded by well-trained gate-keepers and Rākṣasa guards, and filled everywhere with foremost women of the finest quality.

**5.5.10**  That jewel of a palace of the lord of Rākṣasas, filled with joyful women, resounded with the tinkling of exquisite ornaments like the roar of the ocean.

**5.5.11**  That palace, endowed with every royal excellence, perfumed with the finest sandalwood, resounded with the beat of kettledrums and mṛdaṅgas, and rang with the blast of conch-shells.

**5.5.12**  Perpetually worshipped, offered oblations at every festival, constantly honoured by the Rākṣasas — deep as the ocean, and resonant as the ocean.

**5.5.13**  That great monkey of mighty soul beheld the vast dwelling, furnished with most precious appurtenances, thronged with great multitudes of people.

**5.5.14**  Blazing with splendour in its form, crowded with elephants, horses, and chariots — that great monkey reckoned it to be the very ornament of Laṅkā itself.

**5.5.15**  Moving from house to house among the Rākṣasas, surveying the gardens and the palace-towers without any fear, that monkey roamed about.

**5.5.16**  Leaping with great speed into the mansion of Prahasta, that mighty one then bounded to another dwelling — the mansion of Mahāpārśva.

**5.5.17**  Then that great monkey leaped into the dwelling of Kumbhakarṇa, resembling a thundercloud, and likewise into that of Vibhīṣaṇa.

**5.5.18**  And likewise into the mansion of Mahodara, and of Virūpākṣa, into the dwelling of Vidyujjihvā, and likewise that of Vidyunmālin, and the dwelling of Vajradaṃṣṭra — so that great monkey leaped.

**5.5.19**  With great speed, the leader of the monkey-horde went to the mansion of Śuka, and of the wise Sāraṇa, and likewise to the dwelling of Indrajit.

**5.5.20**  The leader of the monkey-horde went to the mansions of Jambumālin and Sumālin, and likewise to the dwelling of Raśmiketu and Sūryaśatru.

**5.5.21**  The son of the Wind visited the mansion of Dhūmrākṣa and Saṃpātin, of the dreadful Vidyudrūpa, of Ghana and of Vighana.

**5.5.22**  He visited the mansions of Śukanābha, of Vakra, of Śaṭha and Vikaṭa, of Hrasvakarṇa, of Daṃṣṭra, and of the Rākṣasa Romaśa.

**5.5.23**  He visited those of Yuddhonmatta, of Matta, of Dhvajagrīva, of Nādin, of Vidyujjihvā and Indrajihva, and likewise of Hastimukha.

**5.5.24**  And of Karāla, of Piśāca, and of Śoṇitākṣa — advancing step by step in due order, Hanūmān, son of the Wind, moved on.

**5.5.25**  In each of those most magnificent mansions, that great monkey of great glory beheld the opulence of those opulent ones.

**5.5.26**  Having passed through all the mansions on every side, that glorious one then arrived at the palace of the lord of Rākṣasas.

**5.5.27**  That foremost of monkeys, that tiger among monkeys, roaming about, beheld the female Rākṣasas who lay in attendance upon Rāvaṇa — those of distorted eyes, bearing tridents and cudgels in their hands, armed with śakti-spears and bearing tomaras.

**5.5.28**  He beheld the various guard-posts in the dwelling of that lord of Rākṣasas.

**5.5.29**  Red ones, white ones, and pale ones — and tawny ones of great speed, of noble lineage, endowed with fine form — elephants that break the elephants of enemies.

**5.5.30**  He saw in that dwelling those trained in the peak of elephant-craft, the equals of Airāvata in battle, the slayers of enemy armies.

**5.5.31**  Dripping like rain-clouds, oozing like mountains weeping — producing a roar like the thunder of clouds, unconquerable by enemies in battle.

**5.5.32**  A thousand companies of troops were there, adorned with gold of the Jambū river, unbroken by nets of gold, resembling the young sun.

**5.5.33**  That monkey, the son of the Wind, beheld in the palace of Rāvaṇa, the lord of Rākṣasas, palanquins of various shapes and forms.

**5.5.34**  Lovely bower-houses of creepers, and painted hall-chambers, and other pleasure-houses, and also wooden model mountains.

**5.5.35**  The charming house of pleasures, and also the daytime-chamber — these he beheld in the palace of Rāvaṇa, the lord of Rākṣasas.

**5.5.36**  He beheld that most excellent of mansions — resembling the surface of Mount Mandara, crowded with peacock-grounds, and thickly planted with flag-poles.

**5.5.37**  With a mass of infinite gems, a treasury on every side, with all work-tasks executed steadily and surely — like the palace of the lord of all beings.

**5.5.38**  And with the flames of gemstones and the splendour of Rāvaṇa himself — that mansion then blazed forth like the sun radiating its rays.

**5.5.39**  Couches and seats made entirely of Jāmbūnada gold, and bright, gleaming vessels — these the leader of the monkey-host beheld.

**5.5.40**  Moist with the residue of honey-wine, crowded with jewelled vessels, delightful and unencumbered — like the palace of Kubera himself.

**5.5.41**  It resounded with the jingling of anklets, with the tinkling of golden girdles, and rang out loudly with the sound of mṛdaṅga-drums and clapping hands.

**5.5.42**  Provided with an assemblage of palace-towers, thronged with hundreds of jewel-like women, with its courtyards well-arranged — Hanūmān entered that great mansion.

**5.6.1**  That mighty one beheld a network of dwelling-chambers, interlaced with a lattice of vaidūrya gems and gold — like a great canopy of clouds in the rainy season, bound together by lightning and interwoven with flights of birds.

**5.6.2**  Various halls of the palatial residences — halls foremost with conches, weapons, and bows — and also charming and spacious moonlit terraces on the palace-mountains: these he beheld.

**5.6.3**  Mansions glorified by many kinds of jewels, well-honoured even by gods and asuras, free from all faults — the monkey beheld those acquired by his own prowess.

**5.6.4**  Those mansions brought together with great care, built as if by Maya himself in person, surpassing all in every excellence on the face of the earth — he beheld those homes of the lord of Laṅkā.

**5.6.5**  Then he beheld the most excellent of mansions — towering in the form of a great cloud, charming and beautiful with golden appearance, commensurate with the personal might of the lord of Rākṣasas, matchless in form and without equal.

**5.6.6**  Like heaven spread out upon the earth, blazing with glory, strewn with many gems, strewn with blossoms of manifold trees — like the summit of a mountain sprinkled with pollen.

**5.6.7**  Blazing as if with the finest of women; worshipped like a rain-cloud by lightning-flashes; carried as if by the finest of swans — that heavenly vimāna-palace shone in the sky, endowed with glory, the dwelling of those who have performed great merit.

**5.6.8**  As the peak of a mountain is variegated with many minerals, as the sky is variegated with planets and the moon — he beheld that jewel of a vimāna, variegated with artfully fashioned clouds, variegated with many precious stones.

**5.6.9**  The earth was fashioned full of ranges of mountains; the mountains were fashioned full of spreading canopies of trees; the trees were fashioned full of spreading canopies of flowers; the flowers were fashioned full of filaments and petals.

**5.6.10**  And white mansions were fashioned, and lotus-pools in full bloom; and again lotuses with their stamens, auspicious and variegated, and likewise wonderful forests.

**5.6.11**  Named Puṣpāhvaya — the Flower-named — blazing splendidly, growing ever greater with the radiance of precious gems, rising even above the most excellent of mansions — that great vimāna the great monkey beheld there.

**5.6.12**  Birds were fashioned of vaidūrya, and likewise birds of silver and coral; serpents were variegated with many kinds of gems; and horses of fine breeds with beautiful limbs were fashioned.

**5.6.13**  With wings of coral, Jāmbūnada gold, and flowers; with bent, curving wings gracefully inclined — their wings shine as if Kāma himself were manifest; birds were fashioned with beautiful faces and lovely wings.

**5.6.14**  And elephants being yoked, with graceful trunks, bearing lotus-leaves as their trunks — and also the goddess Lakṣmī was fashioned with lovely hands, holding a lotus in the lotus-pool.

**5.6.15**  Having approached that beautiful mansion — full of wonder, like a lovely, beautiful mountain — and again that supremely fragrant, lovely one — like a mountain with a beautiful cave at the end of the snowy season.

**5.6.16**  Then that monkey, having traversed that revered city guarded by the arms of the ten-faced Rāvaṇa, not finding that daughter of Janaka — so greatly honoured, so deeply afflicted, overwhelmed by the power of her husband's virtues.

**5.6.17**  Then at that time, the mind of that great-souled one — whose spirit had been cultivated in many ways, whose self was disciplined, who walked the right path, who roamed with clear vision — became exceedingly grieved at not finding the daughter of Janaka.

**5.7.1**  In the midst of that most excellent abode, vast and spacious, Hanūmān, the son of the wind-god, beheld the finest of mansions.

**5.7.2**  That palace of the lord of rākṣasas was half a yojana in breadth and a full yojana in length, crowded with many turrets.

**5.7.3**  Seeking Vaidehī Sītā of the large eyes, Hanūmān the destroyer of enemies wandered in every direction.

**5.7.4**  It was surrounded without obstruction by elephants bearing four tusks and likewise by those bearing three tusks, and guarded by soldiers with upraised weapons.

**5.7.5**  The dwelling of Rāvaṇa was enclosed by his rākṣasī wives and by princesses who had been carried off by force.

**5.7.6**  It teemed with crocodiles and sea-monsters, was filled with timiṅgila fish, and was stirred by the speed of the wind — like the ocean teeming with serpents.

**5.7.7**  Whatever wealth and splendor belong to Vaiśravaṇa and to Indra who rides the bay horses — all that was permanently and unfailingly present in Rāvaṇa's abode.

**5.7.8**  Such prosperity as belongs to King Kubera, to Yama, and to Varuṇa — or even surpassing that — was present here in the dwellings of the rākṣasas.

**5.7.9**  Standing in the midst of that palace, the son of the wind-god saw another exquisitely constructed mansion, dense with many projecting balconies.

**5.7.10**  In heaven, made by Viśvakarman at the behest of Brahmā, stood the divine aerial car called Puṣpaka, adorned with every kind of jewel.

**5.7.11**  That which Kubera had obtained from the Grandfather Brahmā through supreme austerity — that very thing the lord of rākṣasas had obtained by vanquishing Kubera by force.

**5.7.12**  It was filled with golden pillars fashioned with longing-deer designs, worked in gold of fitting note, and blazed as if ablaze with radiance.

**5.7.13**  It was adorned on all sides with peaked chambers of beautiful form, resembling Meru and Mandara, scratching the sky as it were.

**5.7.14**  Wrought by Viśvakarman, it shone like blazing sun, furnished with golden stairways and graceful, excellent terraces.

**5.7.15**  Fitted with latticed windows, of gold and crystal, with terraces of indranīla and mahānīla gems — the great monkey mounted the divine aerial car Puṣpaka.

**5.7.16**  Standing there, he inhaled a divine fragrance arising from drink, food, and cooked dishes — concentrated, like a wind that had taken form.

**5.7.17**  That fragrance drew the mighty-souled one as if a kinsman summoning a kinsman — "Come here" — toward the very place where Rāvaṇa was.

**5.7.18**  Then, proceeding in that direction, he saw a great and beautiful hall — beloved of Rāvaṇa's heart, like a lovely noble woman.

**5.7.19**  It was adorned with steps of jewels, brilliant with a lattice of gold, its floor sheathed in crystal, its panels inlaid with ivory.

**5.7.20**  It was embellished with pearls and coral, with silver and gold, and graced with a great multitude of gem-studded pillars.

**5.7.21**  With even, straight, exceedingly tall and well-adorned pillars on all sides, like immensely lofty wings, it seemed to be setting out for heaven.

**5.7.22**  With a great carpet bearing auspicious earthly marks, it was as vast as the earth itself, garlanded with all the kingdoms and their houses.

**5.7.23**  Resounding with intoxicated birds, pervaded by divine fragrances, furnished with the most excellent couches, and frequented by the lord of rākṣasas.

**5.7.24**  Smoky with agaru incense, spotless and white as a swan, variegated with offerings of flowers — gleaming like a dappled creature.

**5.7.25**  She who was a delight to the mind, who gladdens even one's complexion, that divine hall that destroys sorrow — like the very birthplace of Śrī.

**5.7.26**  The five senses were then gratified by the five excellent sense-objects in that place governed by Rāvaṇa — like a mother gratifying her children.

**5.7.27**  "Is this heaven? Is this the world of the gods? Could this be Indra's city? Or might this be the highest perfection?" — so Māruti reflected.

**5.7.28**  As if lost in thought, he saw the golden lamps there — like cheats defeated by great cheats at gambling.

**5.7.29**  By the radiance of the lamps, the splendor of Rāvaṇa, and the flames of the ornaments — it appeared to be ablaze, so he thought.

**5.7.30**  Then he saw resting on couches a thousand noble women — wearing garments and garlands of many hues, adorned in varied attire.

**5.7.31**  When midnight had come round, having fallen under the sway of wine-sleep, having ceased from sport during the night, he slept deeply.

**5.7.32**  That assembly sleeping — with ornaments stilled, with geese and bees hushed — shone like a great lotus pond at night.

**5.7.33**  The son of the wind saw the faces of those lovely women — teeth hidden, eyes closed, fragrant as lotuses.

**5.7.34**  Like lotuses that had bloomed at the end of darkness and then closed their petals again, so they appeared that night.

**5.7.35**  "Surely the intoxicated bees must seek these lotus-faces again and again, just as they seek blooming water-lilies."

**5.7.36**  So the glorious great monkey reflected by way of analogy — for he judged them equal in quality to water-born lotuses.

**5.7.37**  That hall, graced by those women, shone like the clear autumnal sky adorned with stars.

**5.7.38**  And the lord of rākṣasas, surrounded by them, shone just as the glorious lord of the constellations is surrounded by stars.

**5.7.39**  "These here must be all those stars that have fallen from the sky, enveloped in the remnant of their merit, gathered together" — so the monkey thought then.

**5.7.40**  Indeed like the great stars of auspicious radiance, the women there shone in the clarity of their luster and complexion.

**5.7.41**  Their heavy and full garlands had slipped aside, their fine ornaments were scattered, their senses carried away by sleep during hours of drinking and exertion.

**5.7.42**  Some had their brow-marks displaced, some had their anklets fallen awry, some had their pearl-necklaces slipped to the side — those most excellent women.

**5.7.43**  Others had their faces wrapped in garlands; some had their garments slipped; their girdle-strings loosened — like fillies being led away.

**5.7.44**  Others wore lovely earrings, their garlands crushed and broken — like flowering creepers in a great forest, trampled by a lordly elephant.

**5.7.45**  The brilliant necklaces of some women, shining like moonbeams, lay between their breasts like sleeping swans.

**5.7.46**  The vaidūrya gems of others appeared like kādamba birds; the golden threads of still others appeared like cakravāka geese.

**5.7.47**  Filled with geese and kāraṇḍava ducks and adorned with cakravāka birds, they glittered like rivers — their hips like sandbanks.

**5.7.48**  With nets of small bells like lotuses in broad gold, responsive to feeling, renowned as their banks — they lay like sleeping rivers.

**5.7.49**  On the soft limbs of some, and resting on the tips of their breasts, the lovely rows of ornaments themselves seemed to be ornaments.

**5.7.50**  The fine garment-ends of some, blown by the breath from their mouths, waved again and again over their faces.

**5.7.51**  Those cloth-ends, like flags raised aloft, shining with beautiful radiance at the base of the wives' faces, gleamed in the many colors of gold.

**5.7.52**  The earrings of some of those lovely women, touched by the breath from their mouths, swayed softly, softly, their light auspicious.

**5.7.53**  The breath from their mouths — naturally fragrant and sweet, with the scent of sugarcane-liquor — attended on Rāvaṇa then.

**5.7.54**  And some of Rāvaṇa's wives, mistaking them for Rāvaṇa's face, kissed the faces of their co-wives again and again.

**5.7.55**  Those noble women, their minds utterly devoted to Rāvaṇa, without independence, acted even toward their co-wives as if doing what was dear to him.

**5.7.56**  Placing their arms as pillows, adorned with their ornaments, and lovely garments about them, the women lay there sleeping.

**5.7.57**  One lay upon another's breast; another upon her arm; still another upon that one's lap; yet another upon her two arms.

**5.7.58**  Leaning on each other's thighs, flanks, hips, and backs, their bodies interlocked with each other's, led on by the influence of intoxication and love.

**5.7.59**  Delighting in the mutual touch of their limbs, the slender-waisted women all slept there, their arms merged into one.

**5.7.60**  Strung together by the cord of each other's arms, that garland of women was like a garland threaded on a string, adorned with intoxicated bees.

**5.7.61**  Like creepers in the month of Mādhava blooming and fanned by the wind — their flower clusters clinging together, interwoven in mutual garlands.

**5.7.62**  With necks closely entwined, stirred by bees seeking one another, Rāvaṇa's grove of women was like a forest swaying in the wind.

**5.7.63**  Even among those who were used to such things, it was clearly not possible to make any distinction among the women's ornaments, bodies, garments, and garlands.

**5.7.64**  With Rāvaṇa comfortably asleep, those women of varied radiance — and the blazing golden lamps — seemed to gaze upon him without blinking.

**5.7.65**  The daughters of royal sages, of manes, of daityas, of gandharvas, and of rākṣasas had all come under his power of desire.

**5.7.66**  Not one woman there had been won by force — all had been obtained by valor and virtue; none longed for another man, nor had been previously another's — save for Janaka's daughter, who was worthy of the most excellent.

**5.7.67**  None was of low birth, none of inferior beauty, none lacking in accomplishments, none without proper conduct; none became his wife who was deficient in virtue, or who was not beloved, or who was not desirable.

**5.7.68**  Then the thought arose in the lord of monkeys: "If the lawfully wedded wife of Rāghava were like these wives of the rākṣasa king — how well-born this Rāvaṇa must be!" — for so a good-natured man thinks.

**5.7.69**  And then he reflected again in anguish: "Surely Sītā must exceed these in virtue; and yet this great-souled lord of Laṅkā has done a cruel, ignoble deed against her."

**5.8.1**  There, looking about, Hanūmān saw a foremost couch divine in appearance, made of crystal and adorned with jewels.

**5.8.2**  In one part of it he saw a white canopy adorned with the finest garlands, resembling the lord of the stars.

**5.8.3**  It was being fanned on all sides by women holding yak-tail fly-whisks; pervaded by various fragrances; fumigated with excellent incense.

**5.8.4**  Spread with a most excellent coverlet, covered with woolen cloth and skins, beautified on all sides with strings of the finest garlands.

**5.8.5**  Upon it lay one like a rain-cloud in hue, with brilliant, excellent earrings, red-eyed, long-armed, wearing garments of great silver.

**5.8.6**  His limbs anointed with red sandalpaste of sweet fragrance — like a rain-cloud in the sky reddened at twilight, set with rows of lightning.

**5.8.7**  Encircled by divine ornaments, handsome, able to take on any form — like Mount Mandara asleep, abundant with trees, forests, and shrubs.

**5.8.8**  Having ceased from sport during the night, adorned with excellent ornaments, dear to the rākṣasa maidens, the cause of delight to the rākṣasas.

**5.8.9**  The great monkey saw that hero, the lord of rākṣasas — having drunk and then ceased — sleeping on a couch that glowed like the sun.

**5.8.10**  Approaching Rāvaṇa as he breathed like a serpent, the best of monkeys was greatly agitated and drew back as if in great fear.

**5.8.11**  Then, approaching the ascent and taking shelter at the edge of a terrace, the great monkey observed the sleeping tiger among rākṣasas.

**5.8.12**  The excellent couch of the lord of rākṣasas as he slept shone like a great spring when a rutting elephant has settled in it.

**5.8.13**  He saw the two outstretched arms of that great-souled lord of rākṣasas, bound with golden armlets, comparable to Indra's banner-poles.

**5.8.14**  They bore wounds inflicted by the tusk-tips of Airāvata; their broad shoulders were gashed by Vajra's bolt; they had been struck by Viṣṇu's discus.

**5.8.15**  Thick, evenly and well-formed at the shoulders, joined together, endowed with strength, their thumbs and nails well-marked with auspicious signs, the palms and fingers bearing good marks.

**5.8.16**  Firm, shaped like iron bars, rounded, comparable to elephant's trunks — flung out on the bright couch like two five-headed serpents.

**5.8.17**  Well-anointed and well-adorned with the most excellent sandalwood paste — like fresh blood — cool and fragrant.

**5.8.18**  Caressed by the finest women, attended by the best of fragrances, resounding with yakṣas, serpents, gandharvas, gods, and dānavas.

**5.8.19**  The monkey saw those two arms of his, laid on the couch — like two great serpents sleeping in the caverns of Mandara, as if in anger.

**5.8.20**  With those two full arms, the lord of rākṣasas shone like a mountain — like Mandara with its two peaks.

**5.8.21**  Fragrant with mango and puṃnāga trees, mingled with the finest bakula flowers, joined with the juice of fine food, preceded by the scent of drink.

**5.8.22**  From the mouth of that lion among rākṣasas as he lay sleeping issued a mighty breath — as if filling that entire dwelling.

**5.8.23**  His face was lit by his earrings, his crown — dazzling gold variegated with pearl-jewels — having slipped to one side.

**5.8.24**  And adorned with a necklace anointed with red sandalwood, brilliant with his full, long, and broad chest.

**5.8.25**  His eyes blood-red, a white silk cloth flung aside, neatly wrapped in yellow, most excellent upper garments.

**5.8.26**  Dark as a heap of black beans, breathing like a serpent — like an elephant plunged into the great Gaṅgā's deep water.

**5.8.27**  Illuminated in all four directions by four blazing golden lamps, his entire body made bright — like a cloud surrounded by masses of lightning.

**5.8.28**  And at his feet he saw the wives of that most great-souled one, who was fond of his wives, in the house of that lord of rākṣasas.

**5.8.29**  Faces shining like the moon, adorned with excellent earrings, their garlands and ornaments unfaded — so the lord of the monkey-troop saw them.

**5.8.30**  Skilled in dancing and playing instruments, resting in the embrace of the lord of rākṣasas, wearing excellent ornaments — the monkey saw them seated.

**5.8.31**  At the ears of the women he saw earrings and armlets of refined gold, set with diamonds and vaidūrya gems.

**5.8.32**  With their moon-like faces and beautiful, graceful earrings, that aerial palace shone like the sky adorned with multitudes of stars.

**5.8.33**  Wearied by intoxication and exertion, the slender-waisted wives of the lord of rākṣasas lay sleeping in various spaces.

**5.8.34**  One, embracing a vīṇā and asleep, appeared bright — like a lotus plant in a great river, clinging to a young sprout.

**5.8.35**  Another dark-eyed woman, with a small drum at her side, lay sleeping and shone like a fond mother with her young child.

**5.8.36**  One of beautiful limbs and auspicious breasts lay pressing a kettledrum — like a passionate woman embracing a long-desired lover.

**5.8.37**  One lotus-eyed woman lay asleep, embracing a drum-stand, having fallen under the power of sleep — like a beauty with her beloved.

**5.8.38**  Another, with intoxicated eyes, pressed a mṛdaṅga drum with her soft, full, beautiful limbs shining like gold, and lay asleep.

**5.8.39**  A slender-waisted, blameless woman lay asleep, wearied by intoxication, a small drum resting between her arm and side.

**5.8.40**  Another, having grasped a double-drum and still clinging to it, lay asleep — like a beautiful woman embracing a young calf.

**5.8.41**  One lotus-petal-eyed woman, having pressed a great drum with her embrace of arms, lay asleep, stupefied by intoxication.

**5.8.42**  Another, having set aside a water-jar, lay asleep and shone — like a garland in spring, streaked with flowers, freshly arranged.

**5.8.43**  One woman lay asleep, overcome by the force of sleep, clasping her breasts — resembling golden water-pots — with both hands.

**5.8.44**  Another, lotus-petal-eyed, her face like the full moon, fine-hipped, lay asleep embracing another woman, bewildered by intoxication.

**5.8.45**  Those noble and amorous women lay asleep embracing various musical instruments and pressing them with their breasts — as if embracing their lovers.

**5.8.46**  Apart from them, lying on an auspicious couch in a secluded place, the monkey saw another woman possessed of great beauty.

**5.8.47**  Well-adorned with ornaments set with pearls and gems — as if adorning the excellent palace itself with her own radiance.

**5.8.48**  The monkey saw there, lying and lovely in form — fair, golden in hue, beloved, mistress of the inner apartments — Mandodarī.

**5.8.49**  The long-armed son of the wind, seeing her adorned thus, guessed her to be Sītā by her wealth of beauty and youth; filled with great joy, the lord of the monkey-troop rejoiced.

**5.8.50**  He slapped his arms, kissed his tail, rejoiced, frolicked, sang, went about, climbed the pillars, fell to the ground — displaying the very nature of monkeys.

**5.9.1**  Then shaking off that thought he became still, and the great monkey turned to another reflection concerning Sītā.

**5.9.2**  "That noble woman, separated from Rāma, cannot be sleeping, or eating, or adorning herself, or drinking.

**5.9.3**  She cannot be attending on another man, even if he were lord of the gods — for there exists no one equal to Rāma, not even among the thirty gods." Having thus concluded that this was another woman, he wandered through the drinking hall.

**5.9.4**  Some were wearied by play, others by singing; still others were wearied by dancing; others were overwhelmed by drink.

**5.9.5**  Some women were lying on muraja-drums, on mṛdaṅgas, and on stools; others had settled onto the finest of couches and spreads.

**5.9.6**  With a thousand women adorned with ornaments, skilled in beauty and conversation, apt in speaking the meaning of songs,

**5.9.7**  suited to time and place, speaking fitting words — the lord of the monkey-troop saw him sleeping, sated with love after love.

**5.9.8**  In their midst the long-armed lord of rākṣasas shone — like a bull in a great pen, amid the finest cows.

**5.9.9**  That lord of rākṣasas himself, surrounded by them, shone — like a great elephant in a forest, encircled by she-elephants.

**5.9.10**  The tiger among monkeys saw the drinking hall of that great-souled one, provided with every pleasure, in the house of that lord of rākṣasas.

**5.9.11**  He saw there, in the drinking hall, portions of the flesh of deer, buffalo, and boar laid out.

**5.9.12**  In large golden vessels the tiger among monkeys saw peacocks and cocks, half-eaten.

**5.9.13**  Hanūmān observed boar and dried-meat preparations mixed with curd and black salt, roasted meats, and flesh of deer and peacock.

**5.9.14**  Various cooked partridges, cakor birds half-eaten, whole-skewered buffalo, and goats well-prepared; lickable items both high and low, drinks, and various foods.

**5.9.15**  Along with various sour and salt condiments and colored sweet-and-sour sauces; necklaces, anklets, and armlets of great value strewn about.

**5.9.16**  With drinking vessels scattered about and various fruits as well, and floral offerings made — the floor bloomed with surpassing splendor.

**5.9.17**  With couches and seats well-arranged and placed here and there, the drinking hall appeared to be ablaze even without fire.

**5.9.18**  With meats of many varied kinds, prepared with excellent seasoning and skillfully combined, placed separately in the drinking hall.

**5.9.19**  Divine, clear, varied wines and distilled spirits as well — sugarcane-spirits, mead, flower-spirits and fruit-spirits — each purified with its particular perfumed powder, separately displayed.

**5.9.20**  The floor was continuously beautiful with many garlands laid about it, covered over with golden water-vessels, crystal cups, and yet other drinking vessels of Jāmbūnada-gold.

**5.9.21**  In silver pitchers and in those of Jāmbūnada-gold, the monkey then and there saw an abundance of the finest drink.

**5.9.22**  The great monkey saw vessels of pure gold, of jewels, and of silver, all brimming full with fermented liquor.

**5.9.23**  In some places he saw drinks with half remaining, in others entirely consumed, in still others not yet drunk at all.

**5.9.24**  Seeing various eatables in one place, portions of drink in another, and remnants of food in yet another, he wandered about.

**5.9.25**  In some places with broken drinking cups, in others with overturned pitchers, in yet others water and fruits mixed with garlands.

**5.9.26**  The women's couches here were empty in many places; some noble women lay sleeping, embracing one another.

**5.9.27**  One woman had taken another's garment, wrapped it around herself and embraced it, and lay sleeping, overcome by the force of sleep.

**5.9.28**  The garments and body-garlands of those women, moved by the breeze of their breathing, stirred gently, like a light wind barely reaching them.

**5.9.29**  Of cool sandalwood and sweet fermented liquor, of various garlands, and of various flowers —

**5.9.30**  — in that place the wind bore aloft many varied fragrances; made dense with the scents of bathing substances, sandalwoods, and incense, a fragrant perfume blew through the aerial car Puṣpaka then.

**5.9.31**  Some women there were dark-complexioned and fair; some were wholly dark, those noble ladies; some had limbs of golden hue — those beauties in the rākṣasa dwelling.

**5.9.32**  Indeed, by virtue of their being under the sway of sleep and swooning with love, they had the appearance of sleeping lotus plants.

**5.9.33**  Thus the monkey of immense brilliance surveyed the entirety of Rāvaṇa's inner apartments without exception — and yet did not see Jānakī.

**5.9.34**  And then, having looked upon those women, the great monkey fell into deep reflection, troubled by apprehension about his duty.

**5.9.35**  "Surely this act of gazing upon the inner apartments — upon another man's wives while they sleep — will cause me a great violation of dharma.

**5.9.36**  My gaze has not been directed toward another man's wives with sensual intent; and yet what I have seen here is another man's wife's domain.

**5.9.37**  Then another thought arose for that resolute and steadfast one of fixed single mind — a thought that discerned the certainty of the task.

**5.9.38**  "Though I have indeed seen all of Rāvaṇa's wives with confidence, yet not the slightest perturbation has come upon my mind.

**5.9.39**  For the mind is the cause of all the senses' activity in auspicious and inauspicious conditions — and mine is perfectly controlled.

**5.9.40**  For nowhere else could Vaidehī have been sought by me — for women are always to be looked for among women when searching.

**5.9.41**  A creature is to be sought in its own kind of abode — a lost woman cannot be sought among does.

**5.9.42**  So this has been searched by me with a pure mind — the entire inner apartments of Rāvaṇa are seen, yet Jānakī is not seen.

**5.9.43**  The valiant Hanūmān, looking about even among the daughters of gods, gandharvas, and nāga maidens, did not see Jānakī.

**5.9.44**  Not seeing her there, but seeing the other noble women, that heroic monkey withdrew and began to reflect deeply.
