**5.10.1**  That monkey moved through the inner chambers of his palace — the bower-houses, the painted halls, the night-chambers — eager to behold Sītā, yet nowhere did he see her, she of lovely form.

**5.10.2**  Then that great monkey, not finding her who is dear to the son of Raghu, reflected: "Surely Sītā must be dead, since Maithilī does not come within my sight however thoroughly I search."

**5.10.3**  "That young woman, devoted entirely to guarding her own virtue, a faithful wife, standing firm on the noble path — she must surely have been slain by this wicked evildoer."

**5.10.4**  "Having looked upon the wives of the king of rākṣasas — misshapen, deformed, sallow, great-mouthed, tall and hideous in appearance — the daughter of the lord of Janaka may have perished from sheer terror."

**5.10.5**  "Having failed to find Sītā and having accomplished no heroic deed, after spending so long a time with the monkeys, there is no way for me to return to Sugrīva's presence — he is powerful and swift in punishment."

**5.10.6**  "The entire inner palace has been searched; I have seen Rāvaṇa's women; yet the virtuous Sītā is nowhere to be found. My effort has been in vain."

**5.10.7**  "What will all the assembled monkeys say to me when I return? They will ask: 'Tell us, hero, what did you accomplish when you went there?'"

**5.10.8**  "What shall I say, not having found the daughter of Janaka? When time runs out, they will surely undertake a fast unto death."

**5.10.9**  "What will old Jāmbavān say, and Aṅgada, and the assembled monkeys who have crossed to the far shore of the ocean?"

**5.10.10**  "Perseverance is the root of prosperity; perseverance is the highest happiness. I shall search again those places not yet searched."

**5.10.11**  "For perseverance ever drives a being forward in all endeavors and renders fruitful whatever action that being undertakes."

**5.10.12**  "Therefore I shall exert the best effort born of perseverance, and search those quarters of Rāvaṇa's realm not yet examined."

**5.10.13**  "The drinking halls have been searched, and the flower-houses too; the picture galleries have been examined as well as the pleasure-chambers."

**5.10.14**  "The inner lanes of the groves, the aerial palaces — all have been scrutinized." Having thus reflected, he set about searching again.

**5.10.15**  Underground chambers, shrines, dwellings and smaller outbuildings — leaping up and dropping down, standing still and moving on, turning in every direction,

**5.10.16**  — throwing open doors, pushing at panels, entering and issuing forth, diving down and springing up — that great monkey explored every single space.

**5.10.17**  There was not even a four-finger-wide space in that inner palace of Rāvaṇa which the monkey did not enter.

**5.10.18**  The lanes within the ramparts, the altars and their shrines, the chasms and the lotus pools — all were surveyed by him.

**5.10.19**  Rākṣasī women of every shape, misshapen and deformed, were seen by Hanūmat there — but not that daughter of Janaka.

**5.10.20**  Vidyādhara women of unrivalled beauty in the world were seen by Hanūmat there — but not she who is the beloved of Rāghava.

**5.10.21**  Nāga maidens of noble bearing, their faces like the full moon, were seen by Hanūmat there — but not slender-waisted Sītā.

**5.10.22**  Nāga maidens seized by force by the lord of rākṣasas and held in subjugation were seen by Hanūmat there — but not that daughter of Janaka.

**5.10.23**  Not finding her, yet seeing all those other noble women — the mighty-armed Hanūmat, son of the Wind, fell into despondency.

**5.10.24**  Seeing the great effort of the monkey chiefs and the crossing of the ocean rendered fruitless, the son of the Wind fell once more into anxious thought.

**5.10.25**  Hanūmat, son of the Wind, descended from the aerial palace and fell into reflection, his mind stricken with grief.

**5.11.1**  Crossing easily from the aerial palace to the rampart, Hanūmat, swift lord of the monkey host, was like lightning lodged within a cloud.

**5.11.2**  Having roamed all about Rāvaṇa's dwelling and not found Jānakī Sītā, the monkey spoke these words:

**5.11.3**  "Laṅkā has been searched most thoroughly in service of Rāma, yet I do not see Vaidehī Sītā, she who is beautiful in every limb."

**5.11.4**  "Pools, lakes, ponds and rivers, streams with marshy groves along their banks, mountain fastnesses — the whole earth has been searched, yet I do not see Jānakī."

**5.11.5**  "Here in Rāvaṇa's dwelling Sītā was pointed out by Saṃpāti, king of vultures, yet I do not find her."

**5.11.6**  "Would Sītā — Vaidehī, Maithilī, daughter of Janaka — ever willingly submit to Rāvaṇa, that wicked-natured one?"

**5.11.7**  "I think the rākṣasa, fleeing swiftly in terror of Rāma's arrows while carrying Sītā, may have let her fall midway."

**5.11.8**  "Or else, while being carried along the path frequented by siddhas, the noble lady's heart may have failed upon beholding the ocean below."

**5.11.9**  "Crushed by the great speed of Rāvaṇa and pressed by his arms, I think that wide-eyed noble lady may have surrendered her life."

**5.11.10**  "Or perhaps, as Rāvaṇa traversed higher and higher over the ocean, the daughter of Janaka, writhing in his grasp, fell into the sea."

**5.11.11**  "Or perhaps, while guarding her own chastity from this wretched creature, helpless Sītā, that ascetic woman, was devoured by Rāvaṇa."

**5.11.12**  "Or she of dark eyes, though herself guiltless, may have been devoured by the wives of the rākṣasa king, those women of evil intent."

**5.11.13**  "Meditating on Rāma's face — like the full moon, with eyes resembling lotus petals — that wretched woman may have perished."

**5.11.14**  "Crying out again and again 'Oh Rāma! Oh Lakṣmaṇa!' and 'Oh Ayodhyā!', Maithilī Vaidehī may have laid down her body."

**5.11.15**  "Or perhaps she is confined somewhere in Rāvaṇa's dwelling, piteously murmuring like a myna bird in a cage."

**5.11.16**  "Born in Janaka's lineage, Rāma's wife, slender-waisted, with eyes like lotus petals — how could she ever submit to Rāvaṇa's will?"

**5.11.17**  "Whether the daughter of Janaka is destroyed, disappeared, or dead — it is not proper to report such news to Rāma, who loved her as his wife."

**5.11.18**  "There would be harm in telling him; there would be harm in not telling him. What truly must be done seems difficult to discern."

**5.11.19**  "With matters thus arrived at this pass, what would be timely and proper?" Hanūmān deliberated thus at length.

**5.11.20**  "If I return from here to the city of the monkey king without having found Sītā, what human purpose will have been achieved by me?"

**5.11.21**  "This crossing of the ocean on my part will have been fruitless, as will my entry into Laṅkā and my seeing the rākṣasas."

**5.11.22**  "What will Sugrīva say, or the assembled monkeys, or those two sons of Daśaratha, when I reach Kiṣkindhā?"

**5.11.23**  "If I go and report to Kākutstha the most grievous news — that Sītā has not been found by me — he will abandon his life."

**5.11.24**  "Upon hearing those harsh, terrible, cruel, keen, sense-tormenting, ill words occasioned by Sītā, he will cease to exist."

**5.11.25**  "Seeing him plunged in anguish, his heart dissolved in death, Lakṣmaṇa — devoted to him most deeply, though wise — will not survive."

**5.11.26**  "Upon hearing that both brothers are dead, Bharata too will die; and seeing Bharata dead, Śatrughna will not survive."

**5.11.27**  "Looking upon their dead sons, the mothers — Kausalyā, Sumitrā and Kaikeyī — will not survive either; of that there is no doubt."

**5.11.28**  "Sugrīva, lord of the monkeys, grateful and true to his word, will abandon his life upon seeing Rāma thus departed."

**5.11.29**  "Dejected, distressed, wretched, joyless, tormented by grief for her husband, Rumā will relinquish her life."

**5.11.30**  "Afflicted by the sorrow caused by Vālin's son and worn by grief, Tārā too will not survive the king's death."

**5.11.31**  "With the loss of both his parents and Sugrīva's calamity upon him, how will prince Aṅgada maintain his life?"

**5.11.32**  "Overwhelmed by grief arising from their master's death, the forest-dwellers will beat their heads with palms and fists."

**5.11.33**  "The monkeys, who were cherished by the glorious monkey king with conciliation, gifts and honor, will cast away their lives."

**5.11.34**  "The lordly elephants among monkeys, gathered together, will sport no more in forests, on mountains, nor in groves."

**5.11.35**  "With their sons and wives and ministers, tormented by the calamity that befell their master, they will hurl themselves from mountain peaks and precipices."

**5.11.36**  "The monkeys will resort to poison, hanging, entering fire, starvation, or the sword."

**5.11.37**  "I think, if I depart without success, there will be terrible weeping, and the ruin of the Ikṣvāku lineage together with the destruction of the forest-dwellers."

**5.11.38**  "Therefore I shall not return from here to the city of Kiṣkindhā, for I cannot bear to look upon Sugrīva without Maithilī."

**5.11.39**  "If I remain here without returning, those two great warriors of righteous soul, and the spirited monkeys, will sustain themselves on hope."

**5.11.40**  "Taking food only by hand or by mouth, restrained, dwelling at the root of a tree, I shall become a forest ascetic without having found the daughter of Janaka."

**5.11.41**  "In a place beside the ocean, rich in roots and fruit, I shall build a funeral pyre and enter the fire kindled from the araṇī wood."

**5.11.42**  "Or, sitting still and properly observing the rites of one who bears the marks of renunciation, crows and beasts of prey will devour my body."

**5.11.43**  "This too is a departure approved by the seers, I believe: I shall properly enter the waters, if I do not find Jānakī."

**5.11.44**  "Well-rooted, fortunate, wreathed in glory and fame — this long-standing honor of mine is now shattered, I who have not found Sītā."

**5.11.45**  "I shall become an ascetic, restrained, dwelling at the root of a tree — I shall not return from here without finding her of dark eyes."

**5.11.46**  "If I return from here without obtaining Sītā, Aṅgada together with all the monkeys will not survive."

**5.11.47**  "In dying there are many faults; in living one attains what is good. Therefore I shall preserve my life — while one lives, reunion is certain."

**5.11.48**  "Thus repeatedly bearing many forms of anguish in his mind, that lordly elephant among monkeys could find no end to his grief."

**5.11.49**  "Or I shall slay Rāvaṇa, the mighty ten-necked one; let Sītā have been carried off — it shall be requited."

**5.11.50**  "Or else I shall seize him and carry him above the ocean to Rāma as a sacrificial beast to the lord of sacrificial beasts."

**5.11.51**  Thus the monkey, having entered upon these deliberations, not having found Sītā, his soul overwhelmed by meditative grief, continued to reflect.

**5.11.52**  "As long as I do not find Sītā, the glorious wife of Rāma, I shall search this city of Laṅkā again and again."

**5.11.53**  "And if I bring Rāma here on account of Saṃpāti's word, Rāghava, not finding his wife, would burn all the monkeys."

**5.11.54**  "I shall remain right here with measured food and restrained senses; let not all those men and monkeys perish on my account."

**5.11.55**  "There is also this great Aśoka grove with its mighty trees — I shall approach it, for it has not been searched by me."

**5.11.56**  "Paying homage to the Vasus, the Rudras, the Ādityas, the Aśvins and the Maruts, I shall go there as one who augments the grief of the rākṣasas."

**5.11.57**  "Having conquered the rākṣasas, I shall deliver the queen, delight of the Ikṣvāku lineage, to Rāma the ascetic, proportionate to success."

**5.11.58**  Having reflected for a moment, his senses knotted with care, the mighty-armed Hanūmān, son of the Wind, arose.

**5.11.59**  "Homage to Rāma together with Lakṣmaṇa; homage to that goddess, the daughter of Janaka; homage to Rudra, Indra, Yama and the Wind; homage to the Moon, the Sun and the hosts of Maruts."

**5.11.60**  Having paid those homages and to Sugrīva as well, the son of the Wind surveyed all the directions and turned toward the Aśoka grove.

**5.11.61**  Going first in his mind to the lovely Aśoka grove, the monkey, son of the Wind, then reflected further:

**5.11.62**  "Surely the Aśoka grove, well-tended with every cultivation, will be full of rākṣasas and crowded with forest creatures."

**5.11.63**  "Guards are surely appointed there to protect the trees; even the omnipresent lord of all does not blow there with excessive disturbance."

**5.11.64**  "This form of mine has been contracted for Rāma's sake and for Rāvaṇa's undoing; the gods together with the host of sages will arrange my success here."

**5.11.65**  "May Brahmā the self-born, the blessed Lord, and the gods grant me success — Agni, Vāyu, and Puruhūta the wielder of the thunderbolt."

**5.11.66**  "May Varuṇa with his noose, Soma and the Ādityas, the two great-souled Aśvins, and all the Maruts grant me success."

**5.11.67**  "May all beings grant me success, and he who is the lord of all beings, and whatever others, unseen, range along my path."

**5.11.68**  "When shall I behold that noble face — with its upturned nose, white teeth, unblemished, with a pure smile, with eyes like lotus petals, appearing like the bright full moon?"

**5.11.69**  "By that wretched, sinful, cruel-acting one, wearing a most dreadful adornment and garb — overwhelmed by force, that helpless ascetic woman: when will she come within my sight today?"

**5.12.1**  Having reflected for a moment and reaching it first in his mind, the greatly resplendent one leaped down to the enclosure wall of that mansion.

**5.12.2**  Standing on the enclosure wall, every limb thrilled with joy, that great monkey beheld in the early spring various trees flowering at their crests.

**5.12.3**  Śāla trees, aśoka trees, bhavya trees, beautifully blossoming campaka trees, uddālaka trees, nāga trees, mango trees, and kapimukha trees as well —

**5.12.4**  — he leaped into that tree-garden dense with mango groves and entwined with hundreds of creepers, like an iron arrow loosed from a bowstring.

**5.12.5**  Entering that wondrous place resounding with birds, surrounded on all sides by silver and golden trees,

**5.12.6**  — vivid with birds and herds of deer, a grove of marvellous colors, blazing like the risen sun — Hanūmān the monkey beheld it.

**5.12.7**  Surrounded by trees of every kind laden with flowers and fruit, always frequented by intoxicated kokilas and king-crows —

**5.12.8**  — in a season delightful to the human heart, teeming with deer and birds, resounding with drunken peacocks, filled with many flocks of birds —

**5.12.9**  — while searching for that blameless princess of noble bearing, the monkey awakened birds sleeping in comfort.

**5.12.10**  Struck by the wings of the flocks of birds as they took flight, the śāla trees of many hues released showers of blossoms of every variety.

**5.12.11**  Strewn with blossoms, Hanūmān son of the Wind shone in the midst of the Aśoka grove like a mountain composed of flowers.

**5.12.12**  Seeing the monkey moving through the thickets of trees in all directions, all living beings took him for the season of spring.

**5.12.13**  Strewn with blossoms of every variety fallen from the trees, the earth there blazed like a young woman adorned.

**5.12.14**  Those trees, shaken with speed by that swift monkey, then shed their many-colored flowers.

**5.12.15**  With their crests stripped of leaves, their flowers and fruits shed, they were like rogues stripped of their garments and ornaments in defeat.

**5.12.16**  Those finest of trees, shaken by the swift Hanūmān, quickly shed their flowers, leaves and fruits — those trees so full of blossoms.

**5.12.17**  Stripped of their flocks of birds, those trees with only trunks remaining became utterly inaccessible, as if shaken by a storm-wind.

**5.12.18**  Like a young woman with her hair disheveled, her cosmetics smeared, her beautiful lips and teeth drained of color, scratched by nails and teeth —

**5.12.19**  — so the Aśoka grove, trampled by tail, hands and feet, with its fine trees broken, appeared.

**5.12.20**  The monkey swiftly scattered the garlands of great creepers as the wind scatters masses of cloud from the Vindhyas in the rainy season.

**5.12.21**  Roaming there, the monkey beheld ground paved with jewels, charming grounds paved with silver, and grounds paved with gold.

**5.12.22**  Pools of various shapes filled with the finest water, furnished here and there with stairways of precious gems —

**5.12.23**  — their sands of pearl and coral, their floors inlaid with crystal, adorned by varied golden trees growing along their banks —

**5.12.24**  — with groves of blooming lotuses and blue water-lilies, filled with the murmur of cakravāka birds, resounding with the calls of nātyūha birds, echoing with the cries of geese and cranes —

**5.12.25**  — adorned all around by long streams lined with trees whose water resembled nectar, auspicious streams —

**5.12.26**  — canopied by hundreds of creepers, covered with santānaka trees, their groves clothed in various shrubs, with spaces made by karavīra plants —

**5.12.27**  — then he beheld a mountain resembling a rain-cloud, with high peaks, with varied summits, surrounded on all sides by crests —

**5.12.28**  — canopied by caves in the rock, covered with many kinds of trees: that tiger among monkeys beheld the loveliest mountain in the world.

**5.12.29**  The monkey also beheld a river fallen from that mountain — like a beloved woman who has sprung from the lap of her beloved and fallen away.

**5.12.30**  Adorned by trees with their tops plunged into the water, she was like an angry young woman being restrained by her dear kinsmen.

**5.12.31**  That great monkey beheld her with water turning back again, like a beloved who, now clear-hearted, returns to her beloved once more.

**5.12.32**  Not far from her, that tiger among monkeys, Hanūmān son of the Wind, beheld lotus ponds filled with many flocks of birds.

**5.12.33**  He also beheld an artificial pool filled with cool water, its stairways of finest gems, adorned with sands of pearl.

**5.12.34**  Vivid with various herds of deer, with wonderful colorful groves, adorned all around by grand palaces built by Viśvakarman and by artificial woodlands —

**5.12.35**  — whatever trees were there bearing flowers and fruit, all were well-furnished with canopies and parasols, all with golden platforms.

**5.12.36**  Entwined with many spreading creepers and covered with many leaves, that great monkey beheld a single golden śiṃśupā tree.

**5.12.37**  He saw stretches of ground and gullies with springs; he saw other golden trees resembling peacocks.

**5.12.38**  By the radiance of those trees, that heroic monkey, great among monkeys, then imagined, as if by the light of Meru, that he himself had become golden.

**5.12.39**  Seeing that grove fanned by the breeze among its company of golden trees, resounding with hundreds of tinkling bells, he was filled with wonder.

**5.12.40**  The greatly swift one climbed that śiṃśupā tree — beautiful with fully flowered crest, with tender shoots and sprouts, covered with foliage —

**5.12.41**  — thinking: "From here I shall see Vaidehī, longing to behold Rāma, wandering here and there, tormented by grief, coming about by chance."

**5.12.42**  "And this Aśoka grove of the evil-souled one is truly lovely, adorned with campaka, sandalwood and bakula trees."

**5.12.43**  "And this charming lotus pool, frequented by flocks of birds — Jānakī, the great queen of Rāma, will surely come to this."

**5.12.44**  "She — Rāma's great queen, always dear to Rāghava, well-versed in roaming through forests — Jānakī will surely come here."

**5.12.45**  "Or that woman with eyes like a young deer's, knowing this forest well, she who is worn thin by thoughts of Rāma, will come to this grove."

**5.12.46**  "That goddess of lovely eyes, ever delighting in forest-dwelling, she who ever roams the forest — scorched by grief for Rāma, she will come here."

**5.12.47**  "Formerly she who is the beloved wife of Rāma, the faithful daughter of Janaka, always yearned for those who roam in the forest."

**5.12.48**  "At the time of twilight, dark-complexioned Jānakī, of noble hue, will certainly come to this auspicious river for the evening rite."

**5.12.49**  "And this auspicious Aśoka grove befits her — she who is the consort of the lord of the earth's sons and the match of Rāma."

**5.12.50**  "If that goddess lives — she whose face resembles the lord of the stars — she will certainly come to this auspicious river."

**5.12.51**  Thus reasoning, the great-souled Hanūmān, waiting and watching for the wife of the lord of men, concealed in the dense foliage of the fully blossoming tree, surveyed everything.

**5.13.1**  Standing there, searching for the daughter of Mithilā, he gazed about and surveyed all the ground around him.

**5.13.2**  He beheld it adorned with Santāna and Kalata trees, endowed with divine fragrance and flavor, beautified on every side.

**5.13.3**  It resembled Nandana, that grove — thronged with deer and birds, dense with mansions and palaces, filled with the clamorous song of cuckoos.

**5.13.4**  It was beautified by pools bearing golden lotuses and blue water-lilies, furnished with many seats and carpets, and filled with many-storied houses.

**5.13.5**  Lovely with flowers of every season and fruit-laden trees, it blazed with the splendor of blooming Aśoka trees like the light of sunrise.

**5.13.6**  Standing there, the son of Māruta gazed upon it as if blazing — its branches stripped of leaves, made so again and again by birds flying forth in hundreds, wearing blossoms as their garlands.

**5.13.7**  Aśoka trees — destroyers of sorrow — laden with blossoms from root to crown, bowed down by the weight of their flowers as if touching the earth.

**5.13.8**  With Karṇikāra trees in bloom and Kiṃśuka trees well flowering, that place seemed lit up everywhere by their radiance.

**5.13.9**  Puṃnāga, Saptaparṇa, Campaka, and Uddālaka trees — many and deep-rooted — shone beautifully, their flowers in full bloom.

**5.13.10**  Some of the Aśoka trees there, thousands of them, resembled burnished gold, some were like tongues of flame, and others were like dark collyrium.

**5.13.11**  Like Nandana, like the variegated garden of Citraratha, it surpassed even the inconceivable — divine, charming, and wrapped in beauty.

**5.13.12**  It was like a second sky filled with clusters of floral luminaries, variegated with hundreds of floral gems — like a fifth ocean.

**5.13.13**  The grove was filled with trees of sweet fragrance flowering in all seasons, charming with diverse calls of herds of deer and birds.

**5.13.14**  With currents of many fragrances, sacred in its scent, enchanting to the mind — rich in perfume like Gandhamādana, a second king of mountains.

**5.13.15**  Within that Aśoka grove, that bull among monkeys saw, not far away, a grand sanctuary-palace.

**5.13.16**  It stood in the center supported by a thousand pillars, white as Kailāsa, its stairways made of coral, its platform of molten gold.

**5.13.17**  It seemed to steal away the eyes, blazing with splendor, pure and lofty, as if scratching the sky with its height.

**5.13.18**  Then he saw her — clad in soiled garments, surrounded by rākṣasī women, emaciated by fasting, wretched, sighing again and again — pure as the crescent moon at the start of the bright fortnight.

**5.13.19**  Her beauty, though still alluring, was only faintly visible, like the flame of Agni veiled and obscured by a shroud of smoke.

**5.13.20**  Clad in a single soiled yellow garment, mud-stained and unadorned — like a lotus pond drained of its lotuses.

**5.13.21**  Ashamed, tormented by grief, withered, forlorn — like Rohiṇī afflicted by the planet Aṅgāraka.

**5.13.22**  Her face full of tears, wretched, emaciated by lack of food, absorbed in grief-stricken meditation, pitiable, constantly immersed in sorrow.

**5.13.23**  Seeing no beloved person, only crowds of rākṣasī women — like a doe separated from her own herd, surrounded by a pack of dogs.

**5.13.24**  A single braid of hair, dark as a black serpent, fallen along her hip — she who deserved only happiness, burned by grief, the very embodiment of calamity.

**5.13.25**  Observing her — wide-eyed, exceedingly soiled, emaciated — he surmised her to be Sītā, confirming it by the signs he recognized.

**5.13.26**  For this woman appeared as she had been seen when carried off by that shape-shifting rākṣasa — the very same form.

**5.13.27**  Her face was like the full moon, her brows lovely, her breasts beautifully rounded — this goddess whose radiance dispelled darkness in all directions.

**5.13.28**  Dark-haired, with lips red as Bimba fruit, slender-waisted, well-proportioned — Sītā with eyes like lotus petals, like Rati to Manmatha.

**5.13.29**  Beloved of all the world, radiant as the full moon — seated on the earth, slender, as composed as an ascetic.

**5.13.30**  Breathing heavy sighs, timid, like the wife of a great serpent-king — not shining, ensnared in a great web of grief.

**5.13.31**  Like the flame of fire obscured by a net of smoke — like a memory grown uncertain, like a prosperity that has fallen away.

**5.13.32**  Like faith that has been struck down, like hope that has been thwarted, like a siddhi beset by obstacles, like an intellect grown turbid.

**5.13.33**  Like a fame fallen by false slander — afflicted by the anguish of separation from Rāma, wasted by the abduction of the rākṣasa.

**5.13.34**  Helpless, with eyes like a young deer's, gazing here and there — her dark face with its downcast lashes filled with the water of tears, sighing again and again.

**5.13.35**  Bearing dirt and grime, wretched, worthy of adornment yet unadorned — like the light of the moon-king veiled by dark clouds.

**5.13.36**  His mind wavered repeatedly as he gazed upon her — like a sacred science grown slack through disuse.

**5.13.37**  With difficulty Hanūmān recognized Sītā unadorned — like a word devoid of proper formation that has drifted to another meaning.

**5.13.38**  Observing that wide-eyed, blameless princess, he concluded it was Sītā, establishing it by means of the signs.

**5.13.39**  The ornaments on her limbs that Rāma had described one by one — he now recognized those adornments that beautified her body.

**5.13.40**  The beautifully crafted ear-ornaments, the Śvadaṃṣṭra jewels well set, and the ornaments on her hands variegated with gems and coral.

**5.13.41**  Darkened now through long wearing, yet retaining their shape — these must be the very ones Rāma described, I believe.

**5.13.42**  Those which are absent I do not see; those which have not been lost — these are they, without doubt.

**5.13.43**  That auspicious yellow upper garment, bright as a sheet of gold, slipped off and caught in a tree — the one the monkeys had seen at the time.

**5.13.44**  And the chief ornaments seen on the ground — cast away by her very self, sounding loud and large.

**5.13.45**  This garment, more worn from long wearing, is nonetheless still of that color — as splendid as the other one.

**5.13.46**  This golden-limbed beloved queen of Rāma — even though lost, she does not perish from his mind.

**5.13.47**  She is the one for whose sake Rāma burns with four fires: compassion, tenderness, grief, and longing.

**5.13.48**  Compassion because a woman is lost; tenderness because she was his dependent; grief because his wife is gone; longing because she is his beloved.

**5.13.49**  The beauty of this goddess, the perfection of each of her limbs, and the form of Rāma — she of dark eyes is indeed worthy of him.

**5.13.50**  This goddess's mind is fixed on him, and his mind is fixed on her — it is for that reason she and that righteous-souled one each live, even for a moment.

**5.13.51**  Great-armed Rāma does what is difficult indeed — he lives even for a moment without this Sītā whose glance is intoxicating.

**5.13.52**  Having thus beheld Sītā, the son of Vāyu was overjoyed; in his mind he went to Rāma and praised that lord.

**5.14.1**  Having praised Sītā who is worthy of all praise, and Rāma who is charming with virtues, that bull among monkeys fell once again into deep reflection.

**5.14.2**  Having meditated for a moment, his eyes overflowing with tears, the splendid Hanūmān, overcome by his regard for Sītā, lamented aloud.

**5.14.3**  She who is venerable, dear to the elders, honored even by Lakṣmaṇa who is trained by his elders — if even Sītā suffers such grief, truly time is insurmountable.

**5.14.4**  The goddess, knowing the resolve of Rāma and the wise Lakṣmaṇa, is not excessively disturbed — like the Gaṅgā at the onset of rains.

**5.14.5**  Equal in character, age, and conduct, equal in lineage and mark — the Rāghava deserves the daughter of Videha, and this dark-eyed lady deserves him.

**5.14.6**  Seeing her, resplendent as fresh gold, like Śrī beloved of the world, he went to Rāma in his mind and spoke these words.

**5.14.7**  For the sake of her, this wide-eyed one, the mighty Vālī was slain and Kabandha — equal to Rāvaṇa in prowess — was struck down.

**5.14.8**  And Virādha, the rākṣasa of terrible might, was slain in battle — by Rāma striding forward in the forest as great Indra slew Śambara.

**5.14.9**  Fourteen thousand rākṣasas of terrible deeds were slain at Janasthāna with arrows blazing like tongues of fire.

**5.14.10**  Khara was slain in battle, Triśiras struck down, and the greatly brilliant Dūṣaṇa — by Rāma, the self-realized one.

**5.14.11**  The sovereignty of the Vānaras, difficult to obtain, guarded by Vālī — for her sake Sugrīva attained it, honored by all the world.

**5.14.12**  The illustrious ocean, lord of rivers and streams, was crossed by me, and this city was reconnoitered — all for the sake of her, this wide-eyed one.

**5.14.13**  Even if Rāma were to overturn the entire earth to its ocean-boundary for her sake, the world too would be right — such is my conviction.

**5.14.14**  Kingship over the three worlds or Sītā, daughter of Janaka — the whole sovereignty of the three worlds does not match even a fraction of Sītā's worth.

**5.14.15**  She is the daughter of the virtuous, great-souled king Janaka of Mithilā — Sītā, steadfast in her vow to her husband.

**5.14.16**  She arose from the earth, from a field furrowed by the plowshare — strewn with auspicious field-dust resembling lotus pollen.

**5.14.17**  She is the eldest and glorious daughter-in-law of King Daśaratha — who was valiant, noble in character, and never turned back in battle.

**5.14.18**  She is the beloved wife of Rāma — who knows dharma, is ever grateful, and is self-realized — now fallen into the power of a rākṣasa.

**5.14.19**  Forsaking all pleasures, driven by the strength of her love for her husband, giving no thought to hardships, she entered the uninhabited forest.

**5.14.20**  Contented with fruits and roots, devoted to serving her husband — she who found the highest joy even in the forest as if in her own home.

**5.14.21**  She, this golden-limbed one who always speaks with a sweet smile — now endures this torment, unlucky in suffering these misfortunes.

**5.14.22**  Rāghava yearns to see this virtuous woman, afflicted by Rāvaṇa — as a thirsty man yearns for a drinking place.

**5.14.23**  Surely at regaining her, the Rāghava will find joy — like a king fallen from his kingdom who regains the earth.

**5.14.24**  Abandoned by sensuous pleasures, bereft of her kinsmen, she sustains her body longing for reunion with him.

**5.14.25**  She does not see these rākṣasī women, she does not see these trees with their flowers and fruits — her heart fixed in one place, she sees only Rāma.

**5.14.26**  A husband is indeed the supreme ornament of a woman, greater than all ornaments — and deprived of him, she who deserves to shine does not shine.

**5.14.27**  Rāma, the lord, does what is difficult indeed — bereft of her, he sustains his body and does not sink under grief.

**5.14.28**  Seeing her — with dark ends to her hair, eyes like a hundred-petalled lotus, deserving only of happiness yet suffering — even my mind is pained.

**5.14.29**  Patient as the earth, with eyes resembling a blue lotus, she who was once protected by Rāghava and Lakṣmaṇa — is now guarded at the foot of a tree by rākṣasī women with grotesque eyes.

**5.14.30**  Like a lotus-plant struck by frost, its beauty gone, oppressed by a succession of misfortunes, like a Cakravākī separated from her mate — the daughter of Janaka has fallen into a pitiable state.

**5.14.31**  Indeed, the Aśoka trees whose tips are bent with blossoms evoke a deep sorrow in her; and with winter's departure the sun has risen, his beams no longer mild but blazing in thousands.

**5.14.32**  Having pondered the matter thus, the monkey's mind settled into the certainty that this was indeed Sītā; that mighty, swift bull of the monkeys settled himself in that tree.

**5.15.1**  Then the moon — pure himself, resembling a cluster of Kumuda lotuses — moved through the clear sky, as a swan moves through dark water.

**5.15.2**  As if rendering assistance, the moon with pure luster, by his cool rays, served the son of Vāyu.

**5.15.3**  Then he saw Sītā, whose face resembled the full moon, cast down as if by the weight of her grief, like a boat weighed down upon the water.

**5.15.4**  As Hanūmān, son of Māruta, was eager to behold the daughter of Videha, he saw, not far away, rākṣasī women of terrible aspect.

**5.15.5**  One-eyed and one-eared, and others whose ears served as coverings; some with no ears, others with ears like pegs; some with nostrils set on top of the head.

**5.15.6**  Some with enormous bodies and large heads, others with thin and long necks; some with disheveled hair, some bald, others wearing a blanket of hair.

**5.15.7**  Some with pendulous ears and forehead, pendulous belly and breasts, pendulous lips or chin-like lip, pendulous face and pendulous knees.

**5.15.8**  Short and tall, hunchbacked and deformed, dwarfish and frightful, crooked in garment, tawny-eyed, with disfigured faces.

**5.15.9**  Misshapen, tawny, dark-complexioned, wrathful, fond of quarrel — bearing great iron spears, clubs, and maces.

**5.15.10**  Some with faces of boar, deer, tiger, buffalo, goat, and jackal; others with the feet of elephant, camel, and horse; some with sunken heads.

**5.15.11**  One-handed and one-footed, some with ears like a donkey's or a horse's, others with ears like a cow's or an elephant's, and yet others with ears like a lion's.

**5.15.12**  Some noseless, others with oversized noses, some with sideways noses, others entirely without nostrils; some with elephant-like noses, some with nostrils on their foreheads.

**5.15.13**  Some with elephant's feet, some with great feet, some with cow's feet, some with feet like tufts; some with excessively large heads and necks, others with excessively large breasts and belly.

**5.15.14**  Some with excessively large mouths and eyes, with long tongues and nails — some with faces of a goat, elephant, cow, or sow.

**5.15.15**  Rākṣasī women of terrible appearance with faces of horse, camel, and ass — bearing spears and clubs in their hands, wrathful, delighting in strife.

**5.15.16**  Terrible and smoke-haired rākṣasī women with disfigured faces, constantly drinking, always fond of flesh and wine.

**5.15.17**  Their bodies smeared with flesh and blood, feeding on flesh and blood — those were the ones that best of monkeys saw, dreadful to behold, causing the hair to stand on end.

**5.15.18**  Seated around a great shouldering forest tree, encircling it — and beneath it that blameless goddess and princess.

**5.15.19**  The auspicious Hanūmān fixed his gaze on the daughter of Janaka — lustrous no longer, scorched by grief, her hair matted and filthy.

**5.15.20**  As if a star fallen to earth with exhausted merit — rich in the renown of her virtue yet reduced to misery by separation from her husband's sight.

**5.15.21**  Deprived of her finest ornaments, yet adorned by her husband's love — confined by the lord of rākṣasas, separated from all her kin.

**5.15.22**  Separated from her herd, hemmed in by a lion, like an elephant cow in bondage — like the crescent of the moon veiled at the edge of clouds by autumn clouds.

**5.15.23**  Her beauty worn away from lack of touch, like a lute neglected and unplayed — Sītā, devoted to her husband's welfare, unfitting to be under the dominion of rākṣasas.

**5.15.24**  In the midst of the Aśoka grove, plunged in an ocean of grief, surrounded by those rākṣasī women — like Rohiṇī seized by a planet — Hanūmān saw the goddess, like a creeper stripped of its flowers.

**5.15.25**  Her body smeared with grime, yet adorned by natural beauty — like a lotus-stalk besmeared with mud, she both shines and does not shine.

**5.15.26**  The monkey Hanūmān saw that radiant one, doe-eyed, wrapped in a soiled and worn garment.

**5.15.27**  That goddess with a wretched face, yet inwardly undaunted, protected by her husband's glory and by her own virtue — Sītā of dark eyes.

**5.15.28**  Seeing Sītā, with eyes resembling those of a young deer, frightened like a young doe, gazing in all directions —

**5.15.29**  — as if scorching with her sighs the trees bearing new shoots, like a mass of sorrows embodied, like a wave of anguish arisen —

**5.15.30**  — emaciated, yet with well-proportioned limbs, beautiful even without ornaments —

**5.15.31**  — the son of Māruta felt an incomparable joy on beholding the daughter of Mithilā; and seeing her of intoxicating eyes, Hanūmān shed tears born of joy, and there he bowed to the Rāghava.

**5.15.32**  And having bowed to Rāma and to Lakṣmaṇa, that mighty Hanūmān, exhilarated at the sight of Sītā, composed himself.

**5.16.1**  As he gazed about the grove with its flowering trees and searched for Vaidehī, only a small portion of the night remained.

**5.16.2**  In the deep of night he heard the Vedic chanting of Brahmā-rākṣasas — those versed in the six ancillaries of the Veda and sacrificers at the foremost rites.

**5.16.3**  Then, with the auspicious sounds of musical instruments pleasing to ear and mind, the mighty-armed, greatly powerful Daśagrīva was awakened.

**5.16.4**  Awakening at the proper hour, the lord of rākṣasas yawned and stretched; his garlands and garments dishevelled, he turned his mind to Vaidehī.

**5.16.5**  Mightily goaded by the god of love and overcome with passion for her, that rākṣasa could not conceal the desire within himself.

**5.16.6**  Adorned with every ornament and bearing an unrivalled splendour, he set out toward that garden frequented by various trees laden with every fruit and flower,

**5.16.7**  surrounded by lotus-ponds adorned with manifold blossoms, filled with perpetually intoxicated birds — a garden wondrous and supremely marvellous.

**5.16.8**  Encircled by diverse creatures delightful to the eye, and beholding the avenues with their archways of jewels and gold,

**5.16.9**  he entered the Aśoka grove — densely wooded, thronged with herds of various animals, and carpeted with fallen fruits.

**5.16.10**  A full hundred women followed him as he walked — that descendant of Pulastya — just as the women of gods and gandharvas follow great Indra.

**5.16.11**  Some women there took up golden lamps, others held young fly-whisks in their hands, and yet others carried palm-leaf fans.

**5.16.12**  Some carried water in golden pitchers and went before him; others took up broad-edged swords and followed behind.

**5.16.13**  One fair lady then took up with her right hand a jewel-studded bowl filled with drink.

**5.16.14**  Another took up a parasol resembling a royal swan, bright as the full moon, with a golden staff, and went behind him.

**5.16.15**  Rāvaṇa's noble wives, their eyes heavy with sleep and intoxication, followed their heroic lord as lightning-creepers follow a storm-cloud.

**5.16.16**  Then the monkey, son of the Wind, heard the jingling of girdles and the tinkling of anklets of those foremost women.

**5.16.17**  The monkey Hanūmān saw him — that one of matchless deeds and inconceivable strength and valour — as he reached the gateway.

**5.16.18**  He was illuminated on all sides by numerous lamps soaked in fragrant oil and held aloft before him.

**5.16.19**  His eyes oblique, coppery, and wide — suffused with desire, pride, and passion — he appeared like Kāmadeva himself who had cast aside his bow.

**5.16.20**  His finest garment — white as the foam of churned nectar and free of dust — trailed gracefully behind him, caught loosely on his armlet.

**5.16.21**  Concealed in the foliage of a branch, hidden by the dense mass of leaves and flowers, Hanūmān began to observe him as he drew near.

**5.16.22**  As he looked on, that elephant among monkeys saw Rāvaṇa's beautiful women, endowed with beauty and youth.

**5.16.23**  The king of great renown, encircled by those lovely women, entered the pleasure-grove resounding with the calls of deer and birds.

**5.16.24**  Intoxicated, adorned with variegated ornaments, with sharp ears, and greatly powerful — this son of Viśravas, the lord of rākṣasas, was thus seen.

**5.16.25**  Surrounded by those foremost women as the moon is by stars, the greatly resplendent great monkey beheld him — that one of great splendour.

**5.16.26**  "This is the mighty-armed Rāvaṇa" — thinking thus, the greatly resplendent Hanūmān, son of the Wind, leapt down.

**5.16.27**  Yet though himself of fierce energy, Hanūmān was overwhelmed by Rāvaṇa's radiance and remained hidden, clinging concealed within the shelter of a leafy hollow.

**5.16.28**  Rāvaṇa turned and drew near, wishing to behold her — she of dark tresses, beautiful hips, full breasts, and dark-cornered eyes.

**5.17.1**  At that very moment the blameless princess — she who is endowed with beauty and youth — saw him adorned with the finest of ornaments:

**5.17.2**  upon seeing Rāvaṇa, the lord of rākṣasas, Vaidehī — she of noble stature — trembled like a plantain tree in the wind.

**5.17.3**  Covering her belly with her thighs and her breasts with her arms, the wide-eyed, fair-complexioned woman sat weeping.

**5.17.4**  Daśagrīva beheld Vaidehī, guarded by the throng of rākṣasī women — wretched, tormented by grief, like a sinking boat upon the ocean.

**5.17.5**  Seated on the bare ground, firm in her vow, her limbs smeared with dirt in lieu of adornment — she who deserved adornment yet wore none — like a broken branch fallen to the earth from a forest tree.

**5.17.6**  She seemed to travel on the horses of resolve and longing toward the presence of Rāma — that lion among kings, the knower of the self.

**5.17.7**  Wasting away, weeping, alone, absorbed in meditation and grief, seeing no end to her sorrow — she who was utterly devoted to Rāma.

**5.17.8**  Writhing and afflicted — like the wife of the serpent-lord in his coils — tormented as Rohiṇī is by a comet in the grip of a planet.

**5.17.9**  Born of a family of noble conduct, in a lineage virtuous and righteous — yet brought to such refinement as though born anew in a low family.

**5.17.10**  Like great fame that has faded, like faith that has been dishonoured, like wisdom that has dwindled, like hope that has been thwarted.

**5.17.11**  Like a prosperous future destroyed, like a command countermanded, like a direction blazing bright whose time has passed, like an act of worship stolen away.

**5.17.12**  Like a lotus-pond laid waste, like an army whose heroes have been slain, like a radiance undone by ascetic's curse, like a river running thin.

**5.17.13**  Like a sacrificial altar defiled, like a flame of fire extinguished, like the full-moon night whose lunar disc is seized by Rāhu.

**5.17.14**  Like a lotus-pond thrown into confusion — its lotus leaves plucked out, its birds frightened away, disturbed by an elephant's trunk.

**5.17.15**  Tormented by grief for her husband, withered — like a river drained dry, bereft of all luster, like the night of the dark fortnight.

**5.17.16**  Delicate, with perfectly formed limbs, fit to dwell in a gem-studded inner chamber — yet scorched as a lotus-stalk only recently uprooted burns in the heat.

**5.17.17**  Seized and chained to a post, separated from the lord of the herd — sighing, deeply tormented by grief — like the wife of the elephant-king.

**5.17.18**  Beautiful without effort, adorned by a single long dark braid — like the earth at the passing of the rain-clouds, clothed in a kingdom of forest.

**5.17.19**  Wasted away by fasting, grief, meditation, and fear — emaciated, wretched, eating little, rich only in austerity.

**5.17.20**  Afflicted with grief, hands folded like a deity imploring — she prayed with all her being for Rāma, the foremost of the Raghus, to vanquish Daśagrīva.

**5.17.21**  Gazing and weeping, blameless, her eyes fringed with fine lashes — coppery, wide, and white — utterly devoted to Rāma: Maithilī thus inflamed Rāvaṇa exceedingly, to his own destruction.

**5.18.1**  Rāvaṇa addressed her — who was surrounded, wretched, joyless, and afflicted — with words that were sweet and full of expressive gestures.

**5.18.2**  "Upon seeing me, you cover your thighs and belly with your arms, as if wishing out of fear to render yourself invisible."

**5.18.3**  "I desire you, wide-eyed one; hold me in high regard, my dear — you who are endowed with perfection of every limb, enchanting to all the worlds."

**5.18.4**  "There are no humans here, nor rākṣasas who take any shape they will. Let the fear that has arisen in you from me depart, O Sītā."

**5.18.5**  "Among rākṣasas, O timid one, this is in every way their proper law — beyond doubt: either going to the wives of others or carrying them away by force."

**5.18.6**  "Yet being such, I will not touch you against your will, O Maithilī; let desire play freely in my body as it pleases — but I will not act."

**5.18.7**  "Lady, there is nothing to fear here; place your trust in me, my dear, and give me your love truly — do not thus languish in sorrow."

**5.18.8**  "A single braid, a bed upon the ground, meditation, soiled garments, and fasting even without purpose — these are not fitting for you."

**5.18.9**  "Colourful garlands, sandalwood paste, aloe-wood, garments of many kinds, and divine ornaments —"

**5.18.10**  "— the most precious drinks, conveyances, beds, song, dance, and music: obtain all these by accepting me, O Maithilī."

**5.18.11**  "You are a jewel among women — do not be thus; adorn your limbs with ornaments. How could you be unworthy, you of such beauty, having attained me?"

**5.18.12**  "This your youth — so exquisitely arisen — is passing away; that which is past never returns, like the swift current of waters."

**5.18.13**  "I believe that having made you, the creator of form — the maker of the universe — rested from his labour; for no other exists equal to you in beauty, O fair to behold."

**5.18.14**  "Having encountered you, O Vaidehī — resplendent with beauty and youth — what man could remain unmoved? Even the Grandfather himself, Brahmā, could not."

**5.18.15**  "On each and every part of your body that I behold — O you whose face is like the cool-rayed moon, O broad-hipped one — my gaze becomes fixed."

**5.18.16**  "Become my wife, O Maithilī — cast aside this folly; become the chief queen among the many foremost women I possess."

**5.18.17**  "All the jewels that have been seized and brought by me from the worlds — all of these are yours, O timid one; this kingdom is yours, and I am yours."

**5.18.18**  "Having conquered the entire earth, garlanded with its many cities, I shall give it to Janaka for your sake, O graceful one."

**5.18.19**  "I see no other in this world who could be my equal in strength; behold my exceedingly great valour — without adversary in battle."

**5.18.20**  "Again and again in battle, gods and asuras alike have been routed by me, their banners crushed — unable to stand against my forces."

**5.18.21**  "Desire me; let the finest adornment be done to you today; let the radiant ornaments be fastened upon your limbs — I see your beauty perfectly suited to such adorning."

**5.18.22**  "Adorned with all manner of adornment, gracious in bearing, O fair-faced one — enjoy pleasures as you wish, drink, take delight, O timid one, and bestow as you please — even the earth and its wealth."

**5.18.23**  "Revel in me with confidence and boldly command me; and through my power, as you flourish, let your kinsmen also flourish."

**5.18.24**  "Behold my prosperity, my glory, and my renown, O fortunate one; what will you do with Rāma, O beautiful one — Rāma who wears bark-cloth?"

**5.18.25**  "Rāma has renounced his victory, his glory is gone, he wanders the forest, bound by a vow, sleeping on the bare ground — I doubt whether he even lives."

**5.18.26**  "Rāma will neither see you nor obtain you, O Vaidehī — as a swarm of dark cranes before the clouds conceals the moonlight."

**5.18.27**  "Nor does Rāghava deserve to recover you from my hands — as Hiraṇyakaśipu could not recover the glory that had passed into Indra's hands."

**5.18.28**  "O you of lovely smile, lovely teeth, lovely eyes — O graceful one — you steal my mind, O timid one, as Garuḍa seizes a serpent."

**5.18.29**  "Having seen you — slender, unadorned, clad in a soiled silk garment — I find no pleasure in my own wives."

**5.18.30**  "However many women dwell in my inner apartments, endowed with every virtue — exercise sovereignty over all of them, O Jānakī."

**5.18.31**  "For among my dark-tressed women are the foremost ladies of the three worlds; they shall wait upon you as the Apsarases attend upon Śrī."

**5.18.32**  "The jewels and wealth that belong to Vaiśravaṇa — those, and the worlds themselves, and me, O lovely-browed, broad-hipped one — enjoy all at your pleasure."

**5.18.33**  "Rāma is not my equal, O lady — not in asceticism, not in strength, not in valour, not in wealth, not in energy, nor in glory."

**5.18.34**  "Drink, roam freely, take delight, enjoy pleasures; I give you wealth in abundance and the earth itself — revel in me at your pleasure, O graceful one, and let your kinsmen gather around you and revel in you."

**5.18.35**  "Your limbs adorned with necklaces of pure gold, roam with me through the forests along the seashore — forests draped in networks of flowering trees, humming with bees, O timid one."

**5.19.1**  Having heard the words of that fierce rākṣasa, Sītā, afflicted and with a pitiful voice, softly spoke words of distress in reply.

**5.19.2**  Sītā, tormented by grief, weeping, trembling, that afflicted woman of noble bearing — devoted wife that she was — thought only of her husband.

**5.19.3**  Placing a blade of grass between herself and him, she of pure smile replied: "Turn your mind away from me; fix your mind instead upon your own people."

**5.19.4**  "You are no more fit to desire me than a sinner is fit to attain liberation. I shall not do what must not be done — I am a woman of one husband; having reached a censurable family, born myself in a great and virtuous lineage."

**5.19.5**  Having spoken thus, Vaidehī — that illustrious woman — turned her back on that rākṣasa Rāvaṇa and spoke again.

**5.19.6**  "I am another man's wife — I am not a fitting wife for you. Look to righteousness properly; observe the vow of the virtuous properly."

**5.19.7**  "Just as your own wives are to be protected, so too are the wives of others, O night-wanderer. Taking yourself as the measure, find delight with your own wives."

**5.19.8**  "The wives of other men bring ruin to one who is unsatisfied with his own wives, who is fickle with unsteady senses, whose understanding is base."

**5.19.9**  "Either there are no virtuous men here, or you do not heed the virtuous — your soul is led astray, and you do not follow what the wise have declared to be salutary."

**5.19.10**  "Prosperous kingdoms and cities perish when they obtain a king who has not mastered himself and who delights in unrighteousness."

**5.19.11**  "So too will this Laṅkā, filled with multitudes of jewels, soon perish because of your single transgression."

**5.19.12**  "When one of evil deeds is being slain by the fruits of his own actions, O Rāvaṇa of short sight, all creatures rejoice at his destruction."

**5.19.13**  "Thus will wronged people speak of you, the evil-doer, rejoicing: 'By fate this calamity has come upon the fierce one.'"

**5.19.14**  "I cannot be tempted by sovereignty or wealth. I am inseparable from Rāghava, as radiance is inseparable from the sun."

**5.19.15**  "Having rested my head upon the honored arm of that lord of the world, how could I ever rest it upon the arm of any other?"

**5.19.16**  "I am the fitting wife of that lord of the earth alone — as sacred learning belongs to a brahmin who has bathed at the completion of his vow and who has known the Self."

**5.19.17**  "Do right, Rāvaṇa: restore me, afflicted as I am, to Rāma, as the lord of elephants returns to his she-elephant calling in the forest."

**5.19.18**  "That bull among men, Rāma, is one whose friendship is well worth seeking by you who desire your position to endure and who would avoid a terrible death."

**5.19.19**  "One might escape the thunderbolt once hurled; one might long escape Death itself — but that Rāghava, lord of the world, when enraged, one of your kind could not escape."

**5.19.20**  "You will hear the great roar of Rāma's bow — like the crash of a thunderbolt released by Indra of a hundred sacrifices."

**5.19.21**  "Here, soon, arrows bearing the mark of Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa — well-jointed, with blazing heads like serpents — will rain down."

**5.19.22**  "Those arrows, feathered with vulture-plumes, falling everywhere in this city and slaying rākṣasas on all sides, will leave no room for flight."

**5.19.23**  "That great Garuḍa Rāma will swiftly pluck up the great serpents that are the lords of the rākṣasas, as Vainateya plucks up serpents."

**5.19.24**  "My husband, the subduer of foes, will swiftly recover me from you, as Viṣṇu recovered the blazing royal splendor from the asuras with his three strides."

**5.19.25**  "In Janasthāna, that destroyed place, when the host of rākṣasas was slain, this evil deed was done by you who were powerless to face them directly."

**5.19.26**  "Entering the empty hermitage of those two lion-men while the two brothers had gone to their hunting grounds — thus were you able to carry me off, O vile one."

**5.19.27**  "You could no more stand in the presence of Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa — having merely caught their scent — than a dog could stand before two tigers."

**5.19.28**  "In battle against those two, your wrestling hold would be as unstable as the seizure of Indra's arms by Vṛtra — only to be overcome by a single arm."

**5.19.29**  "That lord of mine, Rāma, together with Saumitrī, will swiftly take your life with his arrows, as the sun dries up a small pool of water."

**5.19.30**  "Whether you go to Kubera's mountain or to Vālin's realm, or enter the court of King Varuṇa — you will assuredly not escape from the son of Daśaratha, as a great tree struck down by time does not escape the thunderbolt."

**5.20.1**  Having heard Sītā's harsh words, the lord of rākṣasas then replied to Sītā — that beautiful-looking woman — with words she would not wish to hear.

**5.20.2**  "The more a man is gentle and yielding to women, the more he is dominated; the more he speaks what is pleasing, the more he is treated with contempt."

**5.20.3**  "The desire that has arisen for you restrains my anger, as a skilled charioteer controls galloping horses on the road."

**5.20.4**  "Love is perverse among humans — it is said that toward the very person in whom one is bound by passion, compassion and affection arise."

**5.20.5**  "For this reason I do not destroy you, O beautiful-faced one — though you deserve death and disgrace, you who delight in a false renunciation."

**5.20.6**  "For each and every harsh word you speak to me, a terrible death would befit you, O Maithilī."

**5.20.7**  Having spoken thus to Vaidehī, Rāvaṇa, lord of rākṣasas, filled with the fury of anger, spoke further to Sītā.

**5.20.8**  "Two months — the term I have set for you — are to be preserved by me. After that, O woman of splendid complexion, you must ascend my bed."

**5.20.9**  "If, after two months have passed, you still refuse to take me as your husband, my cooks will prepare you as my morning meal."

**5.20.10**  Seeing Jānakī being threatened by the lord of rākṣasas, those large-eyed daughters of gods and gandharvas were deeply distressed.

**5.20.11**  Some comforted Sītā — thus threatened by that rākṣasa — with gestures of their lips, others with expressions of their eyes and faces.

**5.20.12**  Comforted by them, Sītā spoke words beneficial to herself to Rāvaṇa, the lord of rākṣasas, proud of her conduct and virtue.

**5.20.13**  "Surely there is no one in your company who stands devoted to your highest good — no one who restrains you from this censurable deed."

**5.20.14**  "Who other than you in all three worlds would even desire me in his mind — the wife of the righteous-souled one, as Śacī belongs to her lord Indra?"

**5.20.15**  "O vilest of rākṣasas, you have spoken sin against the wife of Rāma of boundless glory — wherever you flee, how will you escape its consequence?"

**5.20.16**  "As a proud elephant and a hare stand together in the forest, so Rāma is to be reckoned as the elephant and you, O lowly one, as the hare."

**5.20.17**  "You who revile the lord of the Ikṣvākus here feel no shame — you have not even come within range of his sight."

**5.20.18**  "These cruel, ugly, dark-reddish eyes of yours — why have they not fallen to the ground, since with them, unworthy as you are, you have gazed at me?"

**5.20.19**  "O sinner, how does your tongue not crumble as you address me — the wife of that righteous-souled one, the daughter-in-law of Daśaratha?"

**5.20.20**  "Only because Rāma has given me no such command, and out of preservation of my austerity, do I not reduce you, O ten-necked one, to ashes with my own blazing power."

**5.20.21**  "I cannot truly be carried off from that wise Rāma. Fate has ordained your destruction — of this there is no doubt."

**5.20.22**  "You who are the brother of Kubera, endowed with heroism and forces — why did you resort to the theft of a wife by luring Rāma away?"

**5.20.23**  Having heard Sītā's words, Rāvaṇa, the lord of rākṣasas, widening his cruel eyes, gazed at Jānakī.

**5.20.24**  He was dark as a storm-cloud, with mighty arms and neck, moving with the bearing of a lion, resplendent, with a flaming tongue and fierce eyes.

**5.20.25**  His towering crown swayed; he was tall, adorned with variegated garlands and unguents, wearing red garlands and garments, and ornamented with blazing armlets.

**5.20.26**  He was well girt with a great waist-cord made with the mekaka-jewel, bound with the serpent that had produced the nectar of immortality, like Mount Mandara encircled by a serpent.

**5.20.27**  He was adorned with earrings the color of the newly-risen sun, like a mountain adorned with red-tipped blossoms of the aśoka tree.

**5.20.28**  Gazing at Vaidehī with eyes reddened by anger, Rāvaṇa spoke to Sītā, hissing like a serpent.

**5.20.29**  "You who are possessed of unrighteousness, bereft of good judgment, following your vow — I shall destroy you this very day, as the sun destroys the twilight with his brilliance."

**5.20.30**  Having spoken thus to Maithilī, King Rāvaṇa — the destroyer of enemies — then commanded all the rākṣasī guards of terrible appearance.

**5.20.31**  He commanded the one-eyed one, the one-eared one, and the one whose ears served as a cloak; the cow-eared, the elephant-eared, the pendant-eared, and the earless one.

**5.20.32**  He commanded the elephant-footed ones, the dog-footed ones, the cow-footed, and the one with feet small as a talon; the one-eyed one, the one-legged, the broad-footed, and the footless.

**5.20.33**  He commanded those with exceedingly large heads and necks, those with exceedingly large breasts and bellies, those with exceedingly large mouths and eyes; the long-tongued, the tongueless, the noseless, those with lion-faces, cow-faces, and pig-faces.

**5.20.34**  "Do whatever must be done, all of you rākṣasīs, gathering quickly, so that Jānakī Sītā swiftly comes under my control."

**5.20.35**  "Turn Vaidehī around by contrary and favorable means, by conciliation, by gifts, and by the threat of punishment."

**5.20.36**  Having thus commanded them again and again, the lord of rākṣasas, his soul consumed by passion and fury, threatened Jānakī repeatedly.

**5.20.37**  Then the rākṣasī Dhānyamālinī quickly approached and, embracing the ten-necked one, spoke these words.

**5.20.38**  "Sport with me, O great king — what use is this Sītā to you? When one who desires courts an unwilling woman, his body is tormented; when one who desires courts a willing woman, joy is fair and full."

**5.20.39**  Thus addressed by the rākṣasī, that mighty one was lifted up and entered the inner dwelling that blazed with the radiance of the sun.

**5.20.40**  Then those daughters of gods, gandharvas, and nāgas surrounded the ten-necked one and entered that excellent mansion.

**5.20.41**  Having threatened Maithilī — who stood firm in her devotion to righteousness, trembling — Rāvaṇa, deluded by passion, abandoned Sītā and entered his own luminous dwelling.

**5.21.1**  Having spoken thus to Maithilī and having commanded all the rākṣasīs, King Rāvaṇa — the destroyer of enemies — departed.

**5.21.2**  When the lord of rākṣasas had gone out and returned to the inner apartments, those rākṣasīs of terrible form rushed at Sītā.

**5.21.3**  Then those rākṣasīs, maddened with rage, approached Sītā and spoke these words to Vaidehī in the harshest of tones.

**5.21.4**  "Sītā, you do not think highly of becoming the wife of the most excellent Paulastya — the great-souled Rāvaṇa, the ten-necked one."

**5.21.5**  Then a rākṣasī named Ekajaṭā, red-eyed with anger, addressed Sītā — whose palms were as tender as her belly — and spoke these words.

**5.21.6**  "Among the six progenitors, the fourth lord of creatures is Pulastya — the mind-born son of Brahmā, renowned by that name."

**5.21.7**  "And of Pulastya's mind-born son is the illustrious great sage Viśravā by name, equal in splendor to a lord of creatures."

**5.21.8**  "His son is Rāvaṇa, the destroyer of enemies, O wide-eyed one. You deserve to become the wife of that lord of rākṣasas. The words I have spoken, O beautiful-limbed one — why do you not consent to them?"

**5.21.9**  Then a rākṣasī named Harijaṭā, with eyes like a cat's, widening them in anger, spoke these words.

**5.21.10**  "By whom the thirty-three gods and even the king of gods were conquered — you deserve to become the wife of that lord of rākṣasas."

**5.21.11**  "Of one who is filled with valor, a hero who never retreats from battle, who is powerful and endowed with might — why would you not attain the status of wife?"

**5.21.12**  "Setting aside his beloved and highly-regarded wives, that king of great might, Rāvaṇa — greatest among all — will come to you."

**5.21.13**  "Abandoning the inner apartments filled with a thousand women, adorned with jewels of every kind, Rāvaṇa will come to you."

**5.21.14**  "He who has repeatedly defeated in battle gods, nāgas, gandharvas, and dānavas in combat — he has come to your side."

**5.21.15**  "Of that great-souled Rāvaṇa, fully prosperous in every way — why do you not desire, O base one, to be the wife of the lord of rākṣasas?"

**5.21.16**  "The sun does not burn out of fear of him; the wind does not blow in his presence — O woman of lovely glances, why do you not submit to him?"

**5.21.17**  "Trees out of fear of him let fall a rain of flowers; mountains yield water whenever he wishes; clouds yield rain at his will — O beautiful-browed one."

**5.21.18**  "Of that king of the nairṛtas, the king of kings, O passionate woman — why do you not turn your mind to becoming the wife of Rāvaṇa?"

**5.21.19**  "What has been truly told to you, O goddess, is truly well spoken, O passionate one. Accept these words, O sweet-smiling one — otherwise, things will not go well for you."

**5.22.1**  Then the rākṣasīs, hideous of face, approached Sītā, and those harsh women spoke harsh and unpleasant words to her.

**5.22.2**  "Sītā, why do you not consent to live in this inner palace — delightful to all beings and furnished with the most worthy couches?"

**5.22.3**  "You are a mortal woman, and you prize so highly being the wife of a mere mortal. Turn your mind away from Rāma — you will never be his again."

**5.22.4**  "You are a mortal woman, yet you desire that mortal Rāma — he who has been cast out of his kingdom, whose purpose has not been accomplished, and who is miserable and forlorn, O blameless one."

**5.22.5**  Having heard the words of the rākṣasīs, Sītā, lotus-eyed, with both eyes brimming with tears, spoke these words.

**5.22.6**  "What you all say together is hateful to the world; that sinful speech does not take root in my mind."

**5.22.7**  "A mortal woman does not deserve to be the wife of a rākṣasa. Eat me if you will, all of you — I will not do your bidding. Whether destitute or stripped of his kingdom, whoever is my husband is my lord."

**5.22.8**  Having heard Sītā's words, the rākṣasīs, overwhelmed with rage and urged on by Rāvaṇa, began to threaten her with harsh words.

**5.22.9**  Hanūmān, hidden and silent in the śiṃśapā tree, heard those rākṣasīs as they fiercely threatened Sītā.

**5.22.10**  Rushing at her furiously from all sides as she trembled, they licked their blazing, protruding lips again and again.

**5.22.11**  And utterly enraged, quickly brandishing their battle-axes, they said: "She does not deserve Rāvaṇa, lord of the rākṣasas, as her husband."

**5.22.12**  She — the lovely-faced one — being menaced by those terrible rākṣasīs, wiping away her tears, drew near to that śiṃśapā tree.

**5.22.13**  Then Sītā, wide-eyed, surrounded by the rākṣasīs, approached that śiṃśapā and stood there, utterly submerged in grief.

**5.22.14**  Those terrible rākṣasīs fell to threatening her on all sides — her who was thin, wearing a dejected face, and clad in soiled garments.

**5.22.15**  Then a rākṣasī named Vinatā, fearsome to behold, gaunt-bellied and fierce, with the bearing of one enraged, addressed her.

**5.22.16**  "Sītā, enough — you have shown your love for your husband sufficiently. O auspicious one, excess in all things leads to ruin."

**5.22.17**  "I am satisfied; well done, the rites due to your mortal husband have been observed. But heed also my words, O Maithilī, who speak for your benefit."

**5.22.18**  "Embrace Rāvaṇa as your husband — the lord of all rākṣasas, valiant and handsome, like Vāsava the king of gods."

**5.22.19**  "Forsake the wretched mortal Rāma — noble, generous, and dear-speaking though he be to all — and seek refuge in Rāvaṇa."

**5.22.20**  "O Vaidehī, anointed with divine perfumes, adorned with divine ornaments, become from this day the mistress of all the worlds — as Svāhā is to Agni, as Śacī to Indra, O beautiful one."

**5.22.21**  "What is Rāma to you, O Vaidehī — that wretched man whose lifespan is spent?"

**5.22.22**  "And if you do not act on the words I have spoken, we will devour you this very instant, all of us together."

**5.22.23**  Then another, named Vikaṭā, with dangling pendulous breasts, angered and roaring, raised her fist and addressed Sītā.

**5.22.24**  "Many unseemly words from you, O foolish one, have been endured by us out of compassion and gentleness, O Maithilī — yet you still do not act on our timely, beneficial counsel."

**5.22.25**  "You have been brought by others across the ocean, which is difficult to traverse; you have entered the terrible inner palace of Rāvaṇa, O Maithilī."

**5.22.26**  "Confined in Rāvaṇa's house and well-guarded by us, not even Purandara himself in person is capable of rescuing you."

**5.22.27**  "Do as I say, O Maithilī, for I speak what is beneficial. Enough of these flowing tears — abandon this purposeless grief."

**5.22.28**  "Embrace joy and delight; give up this perpetual wretchedness. Sītā, sport with the king of the rākṣasas as you please."

**5.22.29**  "For you know, O timid one, how fleeting is the youth of women. Before it slips away from you, enjoy pleasure."

**5.22.30**  "Roam the lovely gardens and mountain-groves together with the king of the rākṣasas, O you of intoxicating eyes."

**5.22.31**  "Seven thousands of women will remain at your command, O beautiful one. Embrace Rāvaṇa as your husband, the lord of all rākṣasas."

**5.22.32**  "Or I shall tear out your heart and devour it, O Maithilī, if you do not act on the words I have spoken as I have told you."

**5.22.33**  Then a rākṣasī named Caṇḍodarī, cruel in appearance, whirling a great spear, spoke these words.

**5.22.34**  "Upon seeing this woman with eyes like those of a doe, her breasts trembling in terror, abducted by Rāvaṇa, a great craving seized me."

**5.22.35**  "My mind is set on devouring her liver, her spleen, her heart with its vessels, her entrails, and her head too."

**5.22.36**  Then a rākṣasī named Praghasā spoke: "Why do we wait? Let us squeeze the throat of this cruel woman."

**5.22.37**  "Then let it be reported to the king that the mortal woman is dead. There is no doubt he will say: 'Eat her then.'"

**5.22.38**  Then a rākṣasī named Ajāmukhī spoke: "Slaughter her, then make even morsels of everything."

**5.22.39**  "Then let us all divide the portions — I have no liking for quarreling. Let drink be fetched quickly, and garlands of many kinds in abundance."

**5.22.40**  Then a rākṣasī named Śūrpaṇakhā spoke: "What Ajāmukhā has said is exactly what pleases me too."

**5.22.41**  "And let wine be quickly brought — the destroyer of all sorrow. Having obtained this human flesh, let us dance at the Nikumbhilā shrine."

**5.22.42**  Thus threatened in this manner by those utterly terrible rākṣasīs, Sītā — who resembled the daughter of a god — abandoned her composure and wept.

**5.23.1**  As those unsightly rākṣasīs spoke many harsh and dreadful words in this way, the daughter of Janaka wept.

**5.23.2**  But Vaidehī, that high-minded woman, thus addressed by the rākṣasīs, spoke in a voice choked with tears, utterly trembling with fear.

**5.23.3**  "A mortal woman does not deserve to become the wife of a rākṣasa. Eat me, all of you, if you will — I will not do your bidding."

**5.23.4**  She, Sītā — who resembled the daughter of a god — standing in the midst of the rākṣasīs, found no peace, afflicted by grief and threatened by Rāvaṇa.

**5.23.5**  Sītā shuddered violently, as though her own limbs were failing her — like a doe separated from her herd in the forest and harassed by cuckoos.

**5.23.6**  She, supporting herself on a large flowering branch of the aśoka tree, thought with grief of her husband, her heart broken.

**5.23.7**  She, bathing her ample breasts with the flow of tears from her eyes, could find no end to her grief even as she reflected.

**5.23.8**  Trembling like a plantain tree fallen in a gust of wind, stricken with fear of the rākṣasīs, she became pale of face.

**5.23.9**  Then the long, thick braid of trembling Sītā was seen quivering like a serpent crawling along.

**5.23.10**  She, sighing deeply, afflicted by suffering, her senses struck down by grief, distressed, shed tears — Maithilī lamented.

**5.23.11**  "Ah, Rāma!" she cried, afflicted by pain, then again "Ah, Lakṣmaṇa! Ah, my mother-in-law Kausalyā! Ah, Sumitrā, dear to me!"

**5.23.12**  "This saying repeated by the wise is indeed true: death is hard to come by at the wrong time, whether for a woman or a man."

**5.23.13**  "For here I am, tormented by these cruel rākṣasīs in this place, living — bereft of Rāma, suffering — even for a single moment."

**5.23.14**  "I, this one of little merit, wretched, shall perish without a protector, like a fully laden boat struck by the force of wind in the middle of the ocean."

**5.23.15**  "Not seeing that husband of mine, having fallen under the power of rākṣasīs, I sink in grief like a river-bank undermined by water."

**5.23.16**  "Blessed are those who see my lord — he of lotus-petal eyes, lion-like gait, grateful, and dear of speech."

**5.23.17**  "Bereft in every way of that Rāma of high-souled wisdom, my life is as hard to sustain as though I had swallowed sharp poison."

**5.23.18**  "What sin did I commit in a previous birth, in another body, on account of which I incur this terrible and most dreadful suffering?"

**5.23.19**  "Enveloped in great grief, I wish to abandon my life — guarded as I am by rākṣasīs, Rāma cannot be reached by me."

**5.23.20**  "Shame indeed upon being mortal, shame upon being under another's power — that one cannot abandon life of one's own free will."

**5.24.1**  Speaking thus, with her face streaming with tears, the young daughter of Janaka, her face cast downward, began to lament.

**5.24.2**  Grieving, like one maddened, like one bewildered, like one whose mind has wandered, she turned about and rolled upon the ground like a young mare.

**5.24.3**  "I, belonging to the ever-vigilant Rāghava, have been seized and forcibly brought by the rākṣasa Rāvaṇa, who changes his form at will, though I cried out."

**5.24.4**  "Having fallen under the power of the rākṣasīs, menaced in a most dreadful manner, reflecting on all this and afflicted by the deepest sorrow, I dare not live."

**5.24.5**  "I have no use for life, nor for riches, nor for ornaments, dwelling in the midst of rākṣasīs without Rāma, the great warrior."

**5.24.6**  "Shame on me, ignoble and wicked — who preserves her life even for a moment, having been separated from him; living this sinful life."

**5.24.7**  "What faith do I have in life or in happiness, without that dear husband of mine, beloved of all the earth bounded by the ocean?"

**5.24.8**  "Let my body be torn apart or devoured — I am giving it up. For I could not long endure this suffering bereft of my beloved."

**5.24.9**  "I would not touch the night-wanderer Rāvaṇa even with my left foot — how then could I desire that contemptible one?"

**5.24.10**  "He who is cruel by nature and desires to court me does not know he has been rejected, nor does he know himself or his own lineage."

**5.24.11**  "Cut to pieces, torn asunder, divided, or cast into blazing fire — I would still not submit to Rāvaṇa. What is the use of this babble to you at length?"

**5.24.12**  "Rāghava is renowned as wise, grateful, and compassionate, virtuous in conduct. I suspect it is from the exhaustion of my own fortune that he shows no compassion."

**5.24.13**  "He who alone drove away fourteen thousand rākṣasas at Janasthāna — why does that one not come to me?"

**5.24.14**  "I am confined by Rāvaṇa, a rākṣasa of little true valor. My husband is indeed capable of slaying Rāvaṇa in battle."

**5.24.15**  "He by whom Virādha, that bull among rākṣasas, was slain in battle in the Daṇḍaka forest — why does that Rāma not come to me?"

**5.24.16**  "Indeed this Laṅkā in the midst of the ocean may be difficult to assail, yet there exists no obstacle to the passage of Rāghava's arrows."

**5.24.17**  "What then is the reason why Rāma, of firm valor, does not come for his beloved wife who has been abducted by a rākṣasa?"

**5.24.18**  "I suspect that Lakṣmaṇa's elder brother does not know I am here. For knowing it, that fiery hero would not tolerate this violation."

**5.24.19**  "Even the king of the vultures, Jaṭāyu, who found me and would have reported to Rāghava that I had been taken away, has been struck down in battle by Rāvaṇa."

**5.24.20**  "A great deed was done by old Jaṭāyu, who at that time came to my aid and stood in single combat against Rāvaṇa."

**5.24.21**  "If that Rāghava knew I am here in this state, he would today, with his arrows, enraged, rid the world of rākṣasas."

**5.24.22**  "He would scatter the city of Laṅkā and dry up the great ocean, and he would destroy the name and fame of the vile Rāvaṇa."

**5.24.23**  "Then, searching out the Laṅkā of the rākṣasas, Rāma together with Lakṣmaṇa would without doubt cause rākṣasī women in every house, their lords slain, to weep just as I weep now."

**5.24.24**  "For no enemy seen by those two survives even for an instant. Before long, this Laṅkā would become like a cremation ground, its pathways dense with funeral-pyre smoke and circles of vultures."

**5.24.25**  "Before long I shall attain my heart's desire. These omens of evil portent foretell the reversal of fortune for all of you."

**5.24.26**  "As for the inauspicious signs that are visible in Laṅkā — before long she will be robbed of her splendor."

**5.24.27**  "Surely, when the sinful Rāvaṇa, lord of the rākṣasas, is slain, Laṅkā, formerly invincible, will wither like a widow woman."

**5.24.28**  "The city of Laṅkā, once prosperous with festivities and celebrations, rākṣasas and all, will become a city whose lord is lost, like a woman whose husband has perished."

**5.24.29**  "Surely before long I shall hear here the sounds of rākṣasa daughters weeping in grief in every house."

**5.24.30**  "The city of Laṅkā will be plunged in darkness, stripped of its radiance, its great rākṣasa warriors slain, consumed by Rāma's arrows."

**5.24.31**  "If only that hero Rāma of red-cornered eyes would come to know that I am dwelling here in Rāvaṇa's abode."

**5.24.32**  "But the term that was set for me by this cruel and base Rāvaṇa — that time has now arrived."

**5.24.33**  "These Nairṛtas, doers of evil, who do not know what should not be done — from their unrighteousness a great catastrophe will soon befall."

**5.24.34**  "These rākṣasas, flesh-eaters, do not know what is right. Surely the rākṣasa will prepare me as his morning meal."

**5.24.35**  "How shall I, utterly grief-stricken, carry on without him — without seeing that Rāma, lovely to behold, with red-cornered eyes?"

**5.24.36**  "If there were someone here today who could give me poison, I would quickly behold the god Vaivasvata, separated as I am from my husband."

**5.24.37**  "That Rāma, Lakṣmaṇa's elder brother, does not know I am alive; for if those two knew, they would not fail to search for me across all the earth."

**5.24.38**  "Surely that hero, the elder of Lakṣmaṇa, has departed from here to the world of the gods, abandoning his body on the earth, out of grief for me alone."

**5.24.39**  "Blessed are the gods together with the gandharvas, the siddhas and the great seers, who behold my lord Rāma of lotus eyes."

**5.24.40**  "Or perhaps for the sake of that wise Rāma, devoted to dharma, that royal sage of the highest spirit — for his sake, as his wife —"

**5.24.41**  "— when seen, he would rejoice; when unseen, there is no friendship. Ingrates destroy, but Rāma will not destroy."

**5.24.42**  "Do I then have no virtues whatsoever, or is it the exhaustion of my fortune, that I, Sītā, am separated from Rāma who deserves the best, I who am so passionate?"
