**5.24.43**  "Better for me to die than to live, bereft as I am of that great-souled Rāma of unblemished conduct, that hero who destroys enemies."

**5.24.44**  "Or perhaps those two best of men, the brothers, have laid down their weapons and roam the forest living on roots and fruits, wandering through the wilderness."

**5.24.45**  "Or perhaps those two heroes, the brothers Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa, have been slain by deception by the evil-souled lord of rākṣasas, Rāvaṇa."

**5.24.46**  "I, with matters having come to this pass, wish in every way to die — yet death is not ordained for me even as I endure this suffering."

**5.24.47**  "Truly blessed are those great-souled sages who are honored as truthful, who have conquered the self, the greatly fortunate ones, for whom pleasant and unpleasant do not exist."

**5.24.48**  "From what is dear, grief does not arise; from what is unpleasant arises fear — greater than grief. Homage to those great souls who are free from both."

**5.24.49**  "I, abandoned here by my dear Rāma of high wisdom, having fallen under the power of the sinful Rāvaṇa, shall give up my life."

**5.25.1**  Thus harshly addressed by Sītā, the rākṣasīs, overcome with rage, some of them went swiftly to report this to Rāvaṇa.

**5.25.2**  Then those dreadful-looking rākṣasīs came back to Sītā and spoke harsh words again, all with one purpose — her ruin.

**5.25.3**  "Now, O ignoble Sītā, O one resolved upon sin, the rākṣasīs will eat this flesh of yours at their pleasure."

**5.25.4**  Seeing Sītā then being thus threatened by those ignoble ones, the aged rākṣasī Trijaṭā, lying there, spoke these words.

**5.25.5**  "Devour yourselves, O ignoble ones! You will not eat Sītā — she who is the beloved daughter of Janaka and the daughter-in-law of Daśaratha."

**5.25.6**  "For today I have seen a terrible dream that makes the hair stand on end — portending the destruction of the rākṣasas and the welfare of this one's husband."

**5.25.7**  Thus addressed by Trijaṭā, the rākṣasīs, overcome with anger and yet afraid, all said these words to her.

**5.25.8**  "Tell us — what kind of dream did you see in the night?"

**5.25.9**  Hearing the words that had issued from the mouths of the rākṣasīs, Trijaṭā spoke in due time these words concerning her dream.

**5.25.10**  "Rāghava himself mounted a divine celestial palanquin fashioned of elephant ivory, moving through the sky, yoked to a thousand horses —"

**5.25.11**  "— and in the dream today I also saw Sītā clad in white garments, standing upon a white mountain encircled by the ocean, united with Rāma, like the radiance joined with the sun."

**5.25.12**  "And I saw Rāghava mounted on a great four-tusked elephant, mountain-like, roaming together with Lakṣmaṇa."

**5.25.13**  "Then those two tiger-like men, blazing with their own splendor, clad in white garlands and garments, drew near to Jānakī."

**5.25.14**  "Then, at the summit of that elephant standing in the sky, with her husband clasping her, Jānakī rested upon his shoulder."

**5.25.15**  "Then springing up from her husband's lap, the lotus-eyed one — so I saw — caressed the moon and sun with her two hands."

**5.25.16**  "Then that best of elephants, mounted by those two princes and by the wide-eyed Sītā, stood above Laṅkā."

**5.25.17**  "Himself clad in white garlands and garments, he came together with Lakṣmaṇa on a chariot drawn by eight white oxen, with his brother Lakṣmaṇa and his wife Sītā."

**5.25.18**  "Today Rāvaṇa was seen fallen from the Puṣpaka aerial chariot to the ground, dragged by the hands by a woman, shaven-headed and clad in black garments."

**5.25.19**  "Adorned with red garlands and unguents, he departed on a chariot yoked with asses toward the southern quarter and entered a pool of mire."

**5.25.20**  "A dark woman clad in red garments, her body smeared with mire, bound the ten-necked one by the neck and dragged him toward the southern quarter."

**5.25.21**  "Daśagrīva was carried off on a boar, Indrajit on a porpoise, and Kumbhakarṇa on a camel — all proceeding toward the southern quarter."

**5.25.22**  "A great assembly took place with the sounds of song and musical instruments, of rākṣasas wearing red garlands and red garments, drinking."

**5.25.23**  "And this lovely city of Laṅkā, filled with horses and chariots, was seen fallen into the ocean with its gateways and arches shattered."

**5.25.24**  "Having drunk oil and dancing about, laughing loudly, all the rākṣasa women were in Laṅkā made rough with ash."

**5.25.25**  "And all these foremost rākṣasas, Kumbhakarṇa and the rest, clad in red garments, entered a pool of cow-dung."

**5.25.26**  "Depart! Be gone! Rāghava will recover Sītā — he of supreme wrath will slay together with all the rākṣasas."

**5.25.27**  "Rāghava will not tolerate his beloved, his esteemed wife who followed him faithfully into forest exile, being threatened and terrified thus."

**5.25.28**  "Therefore enough of cruel words from you — let only gentle speech be spoken. Let us supplicate Vaidehī — this is what pleases me."

**5.25.29**  "For she who, afflicted with sorrow, beholds such a dream will be freed from many sorrows and attain her most beloved one."

**5.25.30**  "Even though she has been threatened, seek her favor, O rākṣasīs — what good is further harshness? Terrible fear from Rāghava is now imminent for the rākṣasas."

**5.25.31**  "For Maithilī, the daughter of Janaka, when pleased by obeisance, is capable of protecting the rākṣasīs from great danger."

**5.25.32**  "And in the limbs of this wide-eyed one I perceive nothing contrary, not even the slightest inauspicious omen."

**5.25.33**  "Only the dimness of her luster gives me concern — that sorrow has come upon this goddess who does not deserve suffering, this one destined for the sky."

**5.25.34**  "Indeed I see the attainment of Vaidehī's purpose now at hand — the destruction of the lord of rākṣasas and the victory of Rāghava."

**5.25.35**  "And this is the omen for her to hear great good news — her eye is seen to quiver, lotus-petal-like and long."

**5.25.36**  "And there is a slight bristling of the hair of her right side which is inauspicious, and suddenly one arm of Vaidehī trembles."

**5.25.37**  "And her left thigh, incomparable, resembling an elephant's trunk, quivering, seems to point out Rāghava standing before her."

**5.25.38**  "And a bird that has entered its branch-nest, speaking excellent soothing words, joyfully seems to urge on again and again the utterance of welcoming words."

**5.26.1**  Hearing those words of Rāvaṇa, lord of rākṣasas — words that were cruel and whose import was cruelty — Sītā, stricken with misery, trembled as a young elephant princess set upon by a lion at the forest's edge.

**5.26.2**  Surrounded by rākṣasīs and afraid, severely threatened with words by Rāvaṇa, left alone in the midst of a wilderness — Sītā lamented like a young girl abandoned in a deserted forest.

**5.26.3**  "Truly do the wise say in this world that untimely death does not come — for here am I, wretched and without merit, living even a moment while being reviled thus."

**5.26.4**  "Deprived of happiness and filled with much sorrow, my heart is surely firm — for it does not today split asunder in a thousand fragments like the peak of a mountain struck by the thunderbolt."

**5.26.5**  "Surely there is no fault in me that I should be slain by this one of hateful aspect — nor am I able to yield to his desire, as a twice-born man cannot impart a sacred mantra to one who is not twice-born."

**5.26.6**  "Surely this ignoble lord of rākṣasas will before long sever my limbs with sharp weapons — cutting like an arrow piercing a creature within the womb — since the lord of the worlds does not come."

**5.26.7**  "How painful indeed are these two months that lie far ahead for me, afflicted as I am — like the night's end for a prisoner condemned to death, a thief who has offended the king."

**5.26.8**  "Alas Rāma! Alas Lakṣmaṇa! Alas Sumitrā! Alas Rāma, my mother and my birth-mother both — I, wretched and ill-fated, am perishing like a ship on the great ocean tossed by a wayward wind."

**5.26.9**  "Those two swift sons of the lord of men, those two lion-bulls, were surely slain on my account by that being that held the form of a deer — like two lightning-struck bulls."

**5.26.10**  "Surely that Time itself in the form of a deer lured me, wretched and ill-fated, at the moment when, deluded, I sent away my lord's son and also Rāma's younger brother, with Lakṣmaṇa going ahead."

**5.26.11**  "Alas Rāma, keeper of vows and truth, O long-armed one! Alas, you whose face resembles the full moon! Alas, you who are the benefactor and beloved of all living beings — you do not know that I am being condemned to death by the rākṣasas!"

**5.26.12**  "This devotion to none other than you, this forbearance, this sleeping on the ground, this adherence to dharma, this faithfulness as a wife — all this of mine has been done in vain, like kindness done to ungrateful men."

**5.26.13**  "Indeed, the dharma I have practiced is in vain, and this fidelity to one husband is without purpose — I who am emaciated and pale do not see you, deprived of you, and without hope of union with you."

**5.26.14**  "Having dutifully fulfilled your father's command and having returned from the forest with your vow accomplished, I think you are now sporting with large-eyed women, free of fear, your purpose achieved."

**5.26.15**  "But I, Rāma, who conceived love for you, have long had my heart bound to destruction. Having practiced austerity and vows in vain, I, ill-fated, will abandon my life — fie upon this existence!"

**5.26.16**  "I would quickly abandon my life — by poison or by a sharp weapon — but there is none here in the rākṣasa's dwelling to give me poison or a weapon."

**5.26.17**  "Scorched by grief and having reflected in many ways, Sītā then resolved: taking the cord that binds my braid, I will quickly hang myself with it and go to Yama's abode."

**5.26.18**  Thus lamenting in many ways, with her whole being remembering Rāma, trembling, her face parched, Sītā approached a great flowering tree.

**5.26.19**  She who was gentle in all her limbs drew near and grasped a branch of that tree. As she, of auspicious body, was deeply pondering upon Rāma, upon Rāma's younger brother, and upon her own lineage —

**5.26.20**  — then many auspicious omens, foremost among those recognized in the world as causes of relief from sorrow, omens that had been proven true before as well, appeared to her.

**5.27.1**  As she stood thus — afflicted, blameless, drained of joy, her mind dejected, auspicious — auspicious omens befell her as dependants approach a man graced with fortune.

**5.27.2**  Her left eye — auspicious, with curved lashes, striped and reddened, with a large dark iris and white, that of the beautiful-haired one — began to quiver, like a lotus reddened by a fish striking it.

**5.27.3**  And her left arm — gracefully curved, plump and rounded, worthy of priceless black aloe and sandalwood, long occupied by the most excellent beloved — quickly began to tremble.

**5.27.4**  And her thigh — plump, resembling an elephant king's trunk, well-proportioned where the two thighs join — trembling again, seemed to point out Rāma standing before her.

**5.27.5**  And the auspicious garment of the clear-eyed, beautiful-limbed one — golden in color, just slightly dulled with dust — slipped a little as she stood with teeth like the tips of lotus buds.

**5.27.6**  Awakened by these and other omens — proven true by the virtuous in earlier times as well — she of beautiful brows revived in joy, like a seed long wilted by wind and heat revived by rain.

**5.27.7**  And her face once more shone forth — with lips like the bimba fruit, lovely eyes, brows and the hair's edge with curved lashes, with gleaming white teeth — like the moon released from Rāhu's mouth.

**5.27.8**  She, freed of sorrow, her drowsiness dispelled, her fever calmed, her spirit awakened with joy, shone brilliantly with her noble face like a white night brightened by the risen cool-rayed moon.

**5.28.1**  The valiant Hanūmān heard everything truly — the menacing of Sītā by Trijaṭā and by the demonesses.

**5.28.2**  Gazing upon that goddess-like lady as if she were a deity in a pleasure-grove, the monkey then turned over in his mind a great variety of thoughts.

**5.28.3**  She whom many thousands and tens of thousands of monkeys are searching for in all directions — that very one has been reached by me.

**5.28.4**  By moving in well-devised espionage, I have observed the strength of the enemy; moving covertly, all this has been surveyed by me.

**5.28.5**  The particulars of the rākṣasas and this city have been observed, as has the power of this lord of the rākṣasas, Rāvaṇa.

**5.28.6**  It is fitting that I console the wife of that immeasurable one, who is compassionate to all beings — she who longs to behold her husband.

**5.28.7**  I shall console her whose face resembles the full moon, who has never before known such suffering, and who now finds no end to her grief.

**5.28.8**  For if I depart without consoling this lady whose mind is overwhelmed with grief, my departure would be blameworthy.

**5.28.9**  For when I have gone back, this glorious princess, Jānakī, finding no refuge, would abandon her life.

**5.28.10**  And that great-armed one whose face resembles the full moon, who yearns to behold Sītā — it is right that I console her on his behalf.

**5.28.11**  Speaking in the presence of these night-wandering demonesses is also not easy — how indeed is this to be accomplished? For I am in a difficult situation.

**5.28.12**  If I do not console her before the remainder of this night passes, there is no doubt whatsoever that she will abandon her life.

**5.28.13**  And if Rāma were to ask me, 'What word did Sītā speak to you?' — what could I reply to him, having spoken not at all to the slender-waisted one?

**5.28.14**  Kākutstha, angry, might even burn me with his fierce gaze, seeing me returned from here in haste, bereft of any message from Sītā.

**5.28.15**  And if I set her husband Rāma in motion because of this, the coming of him along with his army would be fruitless.

**5.28.16**  But finding an opportune moment, stationed here among the demonesses, I shall gently console her who is filled with anguish.

**5.28.17**  Though I am exceedingly small and especially a monkey, I shall speak here in the human tongue, in refined Sanskrit.

**5.28.18**  If I were to speak in refined Sanskrit as a brahmin would, Sītā, thinking me to be Rāvaṇa, would become frightened.

**5.28.19**  I must certainly speak a meaningful human word; there is no other way this blameless lady can be soothed.

**5.28.20**  Jānakī, seeing my form and hearing such speech, already frightened by the rākṣasas, will become frightened once more.

**5.28.21**  Then the high-minded, large-eyed lady, overcome with terror, knowing Rāvaṇa's ability to assume any form he wishes, would cry out.

**5.28.22**  And at the cry raised by Sītā, the host of demonesses, terrible and armed with various weapons, resembling Death itself, would suddenly rush together.

**5.28.23**  Then, surrounding me on all sides with their hideous faces, they would exert their full strength in killing or capturing me.

**5.28.24**  Seeing me leap about among the branches, sub-branches, and trunks of tall trees, they might become apprehensive with fear.

**5.28.25**  And the demonesses with their hideous faces, beholding my great form as I roam through the forest, might be convulsed with fear.

**5.28.26**  Then the demonesses would summon the rākṣasas as well — those posted by the lord of the rākṣasas within the abode of the lord of the rākṣasas.

**5.28.27**  Those bearing in their hands spears, arrows, swords, and weapons of every kind would rush upon me in this combat, swift and causing alarm.

**5.28.28**  Though enraged and capable of scattering the forces of the rākṣasas on every side, I could not then reach the far shore of the great ocean.

**5.28.29**  Or many swift-acting ones might leap upon me and seize me; the purpose would remain unaccomplished, and I would be captured.

**5.28.30**  Or those who delight in harm might harm Janaka's daughter — then this task of Rāma and Sugrīva would be ruined.

**5.28.31**  Jānakī resides in this place where the path is lost, surrounded by rākṣasas, enclosed by the ocean, and closely guarded.

**5.28.32**  Whether I am slain or captured by the rākṣasas in battle, I see no other helper capable of accomplishing Rāma's task.

**5.28.33**  Reflecting carefully, I see no other monkey who, if I were killed, could leap across the great ocean extending a hundred yojanas.

**5.28.34**  I am certainly capable of slaying even thousands of rākṣasas, but I would not then be able to reach the far shore of the great ocean.

**5.28.35**  And battles whose outcome is uncertain do not please me; what wise man would place certain success in jeopardy through a doubtful course of action?

**5.28.36**  This is indeed a great danger in my speaking to Sītā; yet the abandonment of life by Vaidehī would be the danger in my not speaking.

**5.28.37**  Even accomplished purposes come to ruin when thwarted by wrong place and time, or when encountering a confused envoy — as darkness at sunrise.

**5.28.38**  Even a resolute judgment fails to shine when it wavers between the right course and its ruin; for envoys who fancy themselves wise destroy their missions.

**5.28.39**  How can this mission not be destroyed? How can confusion be avoided? And how may the crossing of the ocean not become in vain?

**5.28.40**  How indeed can she hear my words and yet not be alarmed? — pondering thus, the wise Hanūmān made up his mind.

**5.28.41**  Singing the praises of Rāma of tireless deeds, her own kinsman, I shall not disturb this lady whose heart is fixed upon that kinsman.

**5.28.42**  Offering auspicious words full of righteousness about Rāma, the foremost of the Ikṣvākus, the self-knowing one —

**5.28.43**  I shall cause her to hear all of them, speaking in a sweet voice; arranging everything in such a way that she may have faith.

**5.28.44**  Thus having deliberated in many ways, the great-souled Hanūmān, gazing upon the wife of the lord of the world, perched within the foliage of the tree, spoke words that were sweet and true.

**5.29.1**  Having thus considered his many thoughts, the great monkey spoke a sweet word within earshot of Vaidehī.

**5.29.2**  There was a king named Daśaratha, foremost of those possessing chariots, elephants, and horses; of virtuous conduct and great fame, upright and of mighty glory, born in the lineage of universal sovereigns, equal in strength to Purandara.

**5.29.3**  Devoted to non-violence, not petty, compassionate, of true valor, the foremost of the Ikṣvāku lineage, possessed of prosperity and the increaser of prosperity.

**5.29.4**  Endowed with the marks of royalty, of broad renown, a bull among kings, renowned across the earth with its four quarters, a giver of happiness and himself happy.

**5.29.5**  His beloved eldest son, whose face resembles the lord of the stars, named Rāma, skilled in discernment, the foremost of all wielders of the bow.

**5.29.6**  The scorcher of enemies, a guardian of his own conduct, a guardian of his own people, a guardian of the world of the living and of righteousness.

**5.29.7**  That hero, true to his pledges, at the word of his aged father, went forth with his wife and his brother into the forest.

**5.29.8**  By him, ranging in that great forest in pursuit of game, was the slaughter at Janasthāna heard; and Khara and Dūṣaṇa were slain. Thereafter Jānakī was carried off by Rāvaṇa, overcome with fury.

**5.29.9**  As to her form, her complexion, her beauty as described and determined — I heard of her from Rāghava; and that very one has been reached by me here.

**5.29.10**  Having spoken thus, that bull among monkeys fell silent; and Jānakī, having heard those words, was filled with the utmost amazement.

**5.29.11**  Then she whose hair-ends were curled, the beautiful-haired one, trembling, raised her face — wrapped about by her hair — and looked up at the aśoka tree.

**5.29.12**  Looking sideways, upward, and also downward, she beheld the minister of the lord of the tawny ones — that son of the wind — of unimaginable intellect, like the sun at its rising.

**5.30.1**  Then, her mind stirred, seeing him concealed within the branches, she beheld there the monkey crouching humbly, ready to speak pleasant words.

**5.30.2**  But she, the passionate Maithilī, seeing the foremost of monkeys standing before her in a humble manner, thought to herself: 'This is a dream.'

**5.30.3**  Gazing intently at him, Sītā became profoundly overwhelmed, as if bereft of life; and after a time recovering her senses, the wide-eyed one began to reflect.

**5.30.4**  'Today I have seen a strange dream — a tree-dwelling monkey, which the śāstras declare to be inauspicious. May there be well-being for Rāma, and likewise for Lakṣmaṇa, and for my father, King Janaka.'

**5.30.5**  'Yet this is not even a dream, for I have no sleep, tormented as I am by grief and sorrow. There is no happiness for me, since I am separated from him whose face is like the orb of the full moon.'

**5.30.6**  'I am now crushed by longing for him; my entire being is fixed upon him; ever meditating on him alone, I see him thus and I hear him thus.'

**5.30.7**  'I think it may be a fancy of the mind; and yet I reason with my understanding: what is the cause? For there is no form of his here, yet this one with clearly distinct form speaks to me.'

**5.30.8**  'Homage to Vācaspati, to the thunderbolt-wielding Indra, to the Self-born Brahmā, and to Fire — what this forest-dweller has spoken here before me, may that be true and not otherwise.'

**5.31.1**  Then the great-spirited Hanūmān, son of the Wind, addressed her — Sītā — with a sweet voice, placing his joined hands upon his head.

**5.31.2**  Who are you, O blameless one with lotus-petal eyes, clad in soiled silk, who stands clinging to the branch of a tree?

**5.31.3**  Why does water born of grief flow from your two eyes — like water scattered from the petals of white lotus flowers?

**5.31.4**  O fair one, are you of the gods, the asuras, the nāgas, the gandharvas, the rākṣasas, the yakṣas, or the kiṃnaras — which of these do you belong to?

**5.31.5**  O lovely-faced one, O shapely one — are you of the Rudras, or of the Maruts, or of the Vasus? You appear to me a divine being.

**5.31.6**  Or could you be Rohiṇī, chief among the asterisms, endowed with every virtue, fallen from the celestial abode, separated from the Moon?

**5.31.7**  O dark-eyed one, O auspicious one — could you be Arundhatī, who having angered her husband Vasiṣṭha in wrath or delusion, is no longer with him?

**5.31.8**  O slender-waisted one, which son, father, brother, or husband of yours — gone from this world to the other — do you grieve for?

**5.31.9**  For the marks and auspicious signs that I observe on you lead me to believe that you are the chief queen or the daughter of a king.

**5.31.10**  If you are Sītā, forcibly carried away from Janasthāna by Rāvaṇa — good fortune to you — then tell me, for I am asking.

**5.31.11**  She — Vaidehī — hearing his words, gladdened by the mention of Rāma's name, spoke these words to Hanūmān who sat in the tree.

**5.31.12**  "I am the daughter of Janaka, the great-souled king of Videha; by name I am called Sītā, and I am the wife of the wise Rāma."

**5.31.13**  "For twelve years I dwelt in the household of Rāghava, enjoying human pleasures, fulfilled in every desire."

**5.31.14**  "Then in the thirteenth year, the king, together with his preceptors, began the preparations to consecrate the delight of the Ikṣvākus with the kingdom."

**5.31.15**  "But while those preparations for Rāghava's consecration were being assembled, the queen named Kaikeyī spoke these words to her husband."

**5.31.16**  "'I shall neither drink nor eat — this is the end of my life each day — if Rāma is consecrated.'"

**5.31.17**  "'If that promise spoken by you with affection, O best of kings, is not to be made false, then let Rāghava go to the forest.'"

**5.31.18**  "That king, ever true to his word, recalling the boon granted to the queen, fainted upon hearing Kaikeyī's cruel and disagreeable words."

**5.31.19**  "Then that aged king, steadfast in truth and righteousness, weeping, begged his eldest and glorious son to accept the kingdom."

**5.31.20**  "He — the glorious one — having first accepted in his mind the father's word, which was dearer than the consecration itself, accepted it with his speech."

**5.31.21**  "Rāma, whose valor lies in truth, would give but never take, and would not say anything disagreeable — even for the sake of his own life."

**5.31.22**  "He — the greatly renowned — casting off his precious upper garments and renouncing the kingdom in his heart, entrusted me to his mother."

**5.31.23**  "And I, quickly setting out before him as a wanderer of the forest — for to me, separated from him, even heaven is not pleasing."

**5.31.24**  "And before us, the greatly fortunate Saumitri, the joy of his friends, had adorned himself with bark-cloth garments to accompany his elder brother."

**5.31.25**  "We three, honoring the command of the husband and firm in our vows, entered from the city into the forest — vast and deep in appearance."

**5.31.26**  "While I was dwelling with him — that one of boundless might — in the Daṇḍaka forest, I, his wife, was abducted by the rākṣasa Rāvaṇa, of evil soul."

**5.31.27**  "By him a term of two months was granted to me as a favor to preserve my life; but beyond those two months, I shall then abandon my life."

**5.32.1**  Hearing those words of hers — overwhelmed with grief upon grief — Hanūmān, leader of the monkey host, spoke a soothing reply.

**5.32.2**  "O goddess, I have come to you as a messenger bearing Rāma's tidings. O Vaidehī, Rāma is well, and he has conveyed his welfare to you."

**5.32.3**  "He who knows the Brahmā-weapon and the Vedas, the foremost among those who know the Vedas — that Rāma, son of Daśaratha, O goddess, has conveyed his welfare to you."

**5.32.4**  "And Lakṣmaṇa, of great splendor, the beloved attendant of your husband, scorched with grief, has made obeisance to you with his head."

**5.32.5**  She — the goddess — hearing the welfare of those two lion-like men, with all her limbs thrilling with joy and delight, then spoke to Hanūmān.

**5.32.6**  "Truly this common saying seems to me auspicious: 'One meets a living man with joy even after a hundred years.'"

**5.32.7**  In that encounter between the two of them, a wondrous joy arose; and trusting each other, the two began a mutual conversation.

**5.32.8**  Hearing those words of hers, Hanūmān, leader of the monkey host, drew near to Sītā, who was wasted with grief.

**5.32.9**  The more Hanūmān crept closer to her, the more Sītā suspected him to be Rāvaṇa.

**5.32.10**  "Alas, shame, shame — what I have done! For what I told him about myself — having assumed a different form, this is surely Rāvaṇa himself."

**5.32.11**  She — the flawless-limbed one, wasted with grief — releasing that branch of the aśoka tree, sat down upon the very ground beneath it.

**5.32.12**  Then the long-armed one bowed down to that daughter of Janaka; but she, trembling with fear, did not look up at him again.

**5.32.13**  Seeing him bowing, Sītā — whose face was like the moon — having drawn a long breath, spoke to that monkey in a sweet voice.

**5.32.14**  "If you are Rāvaṇa himself, a master of illusion who has entered into illusion, you are again creating anguish for me — that is not right."

**5.32.15**  "You who abandoned your own form and took the guise of an ascetic wanderer — you are that very Rāvaṇa I saw in Janasthāna."

**5.32.16**  "O night-roaming shapeshifter, you torment me again — thin from fasting and wretched as I am. That is not right."

**5.32.17**  "But if you have truly come as Rāma's messenger — good fortune to you! I ask you, O foremost of monkeys, for the story of Rāma is dear to me."

**5.32.18**  "Tell me the virtues of Rāma, my beloved, O monkey. You steal away my heart, O gentle one, as a current steals away a riverbank."

**5.32.19**  "Ah, how sweet is a dream! — I who have been carried away for so long, now seem to see a forest-dweller sent by Rāghava."

**5.32.20**  "Even if I were to see the heroic Rāghava along with Lakṣmaṇa only in a dream, I would not sink into despair — even a dream begrudges me this."

**5.32.21**  "But I do not think this is a dream — for in a dream one cannot attain good fortune by seeing a monkey; and yet good fortune has come to me."

**5.32.22**  "Could this be a confusion of the mind? Or is this a phantom of the wind? Or could this be a disorder arising from madness? Or is this a mirage?"

**5.32.23**  "Or else, this is not madness, nor is this confusion bearing the marks of madness — for I am fully conscious of myself and of this forest-dweller as well."

**5.32.24**  Thus reflecting in many ways upon the pros and cons, Sītā, because of the rākṣasas' power of taking any form at will, took him to be the lord of rākṣasas.

**5.32.25**  Having arrived at this conclusion then, that slender-waisted Sītā, daughter of Janaka, did not reply to the monkey.

**5.32.26**  Knowing what Sītā was thinking, Hanūmān, son of the Wind, then delighted her with words pleasing to the ears.

**5.32.27**  "Rāma is radiant as the sun, beloved of the world as the moon, the king of all the world, like the god Vaiśravaṇa —

**5.32.28**  endowed with valor like the greatly glorious Viṣṇu, a speaker of truth with sweet speech like the god Bṛhaspati —

**5.32.29**  handsome, fortunate, glorious, like Kandarpa in embodied form, skilled in striking anger at the right place, the foremost great chariot-warrior in the world — the world rests within the shelter of the arms of that great-souled one.

**5.32.30**  "You shall see the fruit that awaits him by whom Rāghava was lured away from the hermitage in the guise of a deer, and you were carried off in the empty forest."

**5.32.31**  "He of great valor will, before long, slay Rāvaṇa in battle with blazing arrows released in fury, burning like fire."

**5.32.32**  "By him I have been sent as a messenger and have come here to your side; he, afflicted with grief from separation from you, has conveyed his welfare to you."

**5.32.33**  "And Lakṣmaṇa of great splendor, the increaser of Sumitrā's joy, the long-armed one — he too, having paid his respects, has conveyed his welfare to you."

**5.32.34**  "And the friend of Rāma, O goddess — a monkey named Sugrīva, king of the foremost monkeys — he too has conveyed his welfare to you."

**5.32.35**  "Rāma constantly remembers you, together with Sugrīva and Lakṣmaṇa. By good fortune you live, O Vaidehī, though fallen into the power of the rākṣasī."

**5.32.36**  "Before long you will see Rāma, and Lakṣmaṇa the great chariot-warrior, in the midst of tens of millions of monkeys, and Sugrīva of boundless might."

**5.32.37**  "I am a monkey named Hanūmān, minister of Sugrīva. Having leapt across the great ocean, I have entered the city of Laṅkā."

**5.32.38**  "Placing my foot upon the head of the evil-souled Rāvaṇa, I have come to see you, relying upon my own valor."

**5.32.39**  "O goddess, I am not what you take me to be. Let this suspicion be abandoned; trust the words I speak to you."

**5.33.1**  But Vaidehī, having heard those words about Rāma from that bull among monkeys, spoke this soothing speech in a sweet voice.

**5.33.2**  "What is your connection with Rāma? How do you know Lakṣmaṇa? How did the meeting of monkeys and men come about?"

**5.33.3**  "O monkey, describe once more the distinguishing marks of Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa, so that grief may not overwhelm me."

**5.33.4**  "What is his bearing, what is Rāma's form? What are his thighs like, what are his arms like? Tell me also of Lakṣmaṇa."

**5.33.5**  Thus addressed by Vaidehī, Hanūmān, son of the Wind, then proceeded to describe Rāma truthfully and accurately.

**5.33.6**  "By good fortune, O Vaidehī, O lotus-eyed one, though knowing me, you yet ask me — about the description of your husband and of Lakṣmaṇa."

**5.33.7**  "O wide-eyed one, hear from me as I speak of those identifying marks of Rāma and of Lakṣmaṇa as they have been observed."

**5.33.8**  "Rāma is lotus-petal-eyed, captivating the hearts of all beings, endowed with beauty and grace, O daughter of Janaka."

**5.33.9**  "In brilliance he is like the sun, in forbearance equal to the earth, in wisdom equal to Bṛhaspati, in glory comparable to Vāsava."

**5.33.10**  "He is the protector of the living world, the protector of his own people, the protector of his own conduct and of dharma — the scorcher of foes."

**5.33.11**  "O passionate one, Rāma is the protector of the four social orders of the world, and the maker and enforcer of the boundaries of the world."

**5.33.12**  "Radiant and greatly honored, established in the vow of brahmacarya, he knows how to assist the virtuous and knows the conduct of duties."

**5.33.13**  "Trained in the science of kingship, a devoted attendant upon brāhmaṇas, learned in scripture, endowed with good character, and disciplined — he is a scorcher of foes."

**5.33.14**  "Learned in the Yajurveda, greatly honored by those who know the Vedas, he is established in the science of archery, in the Vedas, and in the Vedāṅgas."

**5.33.15**  "Broad-shouldered, long-armed, with a neck like a conch-shell, of auspicious countenance, with well-concealed collar-bones, reddish-brown eyes — thus is Rāma celebrated among the people, O goddess."

**5.33.16**  "His voice resounds like a kettledrum, his complexion is lustrous, he is of great majesty, his limbs are even and well-proportioned, and his color is dark (śyāma)."

**5.33.17**  "He has three prominences, three extensions, three levels; he is elevated in three places; he bears three folds, is bent down in three; has four outer limbs well-developed and three crown-points."

**5.33.18**  "He has four sections, four lines, four cubits' measure; he is fourfold equal; fourteen pairs of limbs proportionate; four types of teeth; four gaits."

**5.33.19**  "Great in lips, cheeks, and nose; smooth in five places; eight-ribbed; bearing ten lotus-marks; ten great qualities; pervaded by three; doubly bright; elevated in six places; slender in nine — thus Rāghava extends through three dimensions."

**5.33.20**  "Supreme in truth and dharma, glorious, devoted to gathering allies and showing grace, knowing the distinctions of place and time, speaking pleasingly to all the world."

**5.33.21**  "And his brother of different mother, the unvanquished Saumitri — in affection, in form, and in virtues he is of the same kind."

**5.33.22**  "Those two, wandering the earth in search of you alone, encountered the lord of beasts who had been cast down by his elder brother."

**5.33.23**  "On the slopes of Ṛśyamūka, dense with many trees, they found Sugrīva of pleasing appearance, seated, bereft of his brother's wife."

**5.33.24**  "And we served that king of monkeys, Sugrīva, true to his pledges, who had been expelled from the kingdom by his elder brother."

**5.33.25**  "Then those two, clad in bark-cloth garments, holding excellent bows in hand, came to a beautiful region of the Ṛśyamūka mountain."

**5.33.26**  "Seeing those two tiger-like men with bows, that bull among monkeys leapt up to the peak of that mountain, bewildered with fear."

**5.33.27**  "Then that lord of monkeys, stationed on that peak, hastily sent me alone to those two."

**5.33.28**  "I, by the word of Sugrīva, with joined hands approached those two tiger-like lords of men, endowed with fine form and auspicious marks."

**5.33.29**  "Those two bull-like men, whose true purpose I had come to know, being filled with joy, I brought to that place, having set them upon my back."

**5.33.30**  "And having introduced them truly to the great-souled Sugrīva, from their mutual conversation intense joy arose between them."

**5.33.31**  "There those two — endowed with glory, the lord of monkeys and the lord of men — each reassuring the other, spoke of past events."

**5.33.32**  "Then the elder brother of Lakṣmaṇa comforted Sugrīva, who had been expelled by his brother Vālin — of immense energy — on account of a woman."

**5.33.33**  "Then Lakṣmaṇa reported to Sugrīva, the lord of monkeys, the grief born of your disappearance — the grief of Rāma of untiring deeds."

**5.33.34**  "That lord of monkeys, hearing the words spoken by Lakṣmaṇa, became utterly lustrous-less then, like the sun seized by a planet."

**5.33.35**  "Then the masses of ornaments that adorned your body, which had fallen to the earth as you were being carried off by the rākṣasa —"

**5.33.36**  "— all of those the monkey troop leaders brought to Rāma and joyfully showed him, but they did not know the direction you had gone."

**5.33.37**  "Those ornaments given to Rāma, brought by me and placed before him — they were sounding and strewn about upon that one whose mind was shattered."

**5.33.38**  "Having placed those beautiful ornaments in his lap, that god-like one then lamented in many ways."

**5.33.39**  "Looking at them, weeping, fainting again and again — those ornaments fed the fire of grief of that son of Daśaratha."

**5.33.40**  "That great-souled one, tormented with grief, lay prostrate for a long time; and even I with difficulty raised him up again with various words."

**5.33.41**  "Having seen those precious ornaments and shown them again and again, Rāghava, together with Saumitri, reported to Sugrīva."

**5.33.42**  "He — Rāghava — burns constantly from not seeing you, O noble one, with a great blazing fire, like a fire-mountain with fire."

**5.33.43**  "On your account, sleeplessness, grief, and anxiety torment that great-souled Rāghava, as fires torment a fire-chamber."

**5.33.44**  "From grief at not seeing you, Rāghava is shaken, as a great mountain of rock is shaken by a great earthquake."

**5.33.45**  "Wandering through lovely forest groves and river springs, he finds no delight, not seeing you, O daughter of a king."

**5.33.46**  "He — Rāghava, that tiger among men — will come to you quickly, O daughter of Janaka, having slain Rāvaṇa together with his allies and kinsmen."

**5.33.47**  "Together, Rāma and Sugrīva both then made a compact — to slay Vālin and likewise to search for you."

**5.33.48**  "Then Rāma, having swiftly slain Vālin in battle, made Sugrīva lord of all the hosts of bears and monkeys."

**5.33.49**  "O goddess, thus was the union of Rāma and Sugrīva brought about. And know me, Hanūmān, to have come here as the messenger of those two."

**5.33.50**  "Having obtained his own kingdom, Sugrīva assembled the great monkeys and dispatched them, the greatly powerful ones, in all ten directions in search of you."

**5.33.51**  "Commanded by Sugrīva, lord of monkeys, of immense energy, those monkeys — resembling kings of mountains — set out in all directions across the earth."

**5.33.52**  "A glorious monkey named Aṅgada, son of Vālin, of great strength — that tiger among monkeys — set out accompanied by a third of the army."

**5.33.53**  "For us, having strayed and lost our way on the great mountain Vindhya, deeply overcome with grief, days and nights passed by."

**5.33.54**  "We were resolved to abandon our lives — out of despair over our mission, from the lapse of time, and from fear of the monkey king."

**5.33.55**  "Having searched through forest fastnesses and mountain streams, and not finding the track of the goddess, we were resolved to abandon our lives."

**5.33.56**  "Deeply plunged in an ocean of grief, Aṅgada lamented — your disappearance, O Vaidehī, the death of Vālin, our own penance-fast unto death, and the death of Jaṭāyu."

**5.33.57**  "For us who had lost hope and were ready to die, a great and powerful bird came, as if for the sake of our mission — as if summoned by our lord's command."

**5.33.58**  "The full brother of the king of vultures, a king of vultures named Saṃpāti, hearing of his brother's death, spoke these words in anger."

**5.33.59**  "'By whom was my younger brother slain? Where was he destroyed? I wish you, O best of monkeys, to tell me this.'"

**5.33.60**  "Aṅgada told him accurately about the great slaughter in Janasthāna, done by a rākṣasa of terrible form, directed at you."

**5.33.61**  "That son of Aruṇa, grieved upon hearing of Jaṭāyu's death, told them — O shapely one — that you were dwelling in Rāvaṇa's palace."

**5.33.62**  "Hearing those joy-increasing words of Saṃpāti, all of us — with Aṅgada at our head — then set forth, we the leaping ones, thrilled and glad, with enthusiasm born of the desire to see you."

**5.33.63**  "Then I, seeing the monkey army sinking in despair before the ocean, shaking off intense fear, leapt a hundred yojanas."

**5.33.64**  "And Laṅkā, teeming with rākṣasas, was entered by me at night; and Rāvaṇa was seen by me, and you — oppressed with grief."

**5.33.65**  "All this has been told to you as it occurred, O blameless one. Speak to me, O goddess — I am the messenger of the son of Daśaratha."

**5.33.66**  "Know me, O goddess, to be the Wind-god's son, the minister of Sugrīva — who has exerted himself on Rāma's behalf, and who has come here on your account."

**5.33.67**  "Your Kākutstha — foremost among all bearers of weapons — is well, and Lakṣmaṇa of auspicious marks, devoted to honoring his elder as a guru, is well."

**5.33.68**  "O goddess, I alone have arrived here by the command of Sugrīva, devoted to the welfare of your husband — that valorous one."

**5.33.69**  "By me — wandering alone, able to take any form at will, searching for your trail — this southern quarter has been traversed."

**5.33.70**  "By good fortune, I shall remove the anguish of the monkey army grieving over your loss — by the announcement of my having reached you."

**5.33.71**  "By good fortune, my crossing of the ocean has not been in vain, O goddess; and by good fortune I shall gain this glory earned by having seen you."

**5.33.72**  "And Rāghava of great valor will come to you quickly, having slain Rāvaṇa, the lord of rākṣasas, together with his allies and kinsmen."

**5.33.73**  "O Vaidehī, there is a mountain called Kauraja, the best of mountains; from there the monkey Kesarī went to the mountain Gokarṇa."

**5.33.74**  "And that great monkey — my father — was seen by divine sages, as he destroyed the abode of Śamba at a sacred ford of the lord of rivers."

**5.33.75**  "I was born in the field of that monkey by the Wind-god, O Maithilī; and I am known in the world as Hanūmān by my own deeds. And O Vaidehī, the virtues of your husband have been spoken by me for the sake of establishing trust."

**5.33.76**  Thus Sītā — wasted with grief — being thus reassured with apt proofs of recognition, acknowledged him as the true messenger.

**5.33.77**  And Jānakī, filled with unmatched joy, from that delight shed tears of happiness from her two eyes with their curling lashes.

**5.33.78**  That beautiful face of hers — with long eyes of reddish-white hue — shone, that wide-eyed one's face, like the moon freed from Rāhu; and she clearly understood him, that monkey Hanūmān, to be none other than what he said.

**5.33.79**  Then Hanūmān spoke a further word to that fair-visaged one.

**5.33.80**  "When that demon was slain in battle — Śamba's abode destroyed by the foremost monkey at the urging of the great sages — then was I born of the Wind-god's power, O Maithilī; and a monkey equal to him in might am I."

**5.34.1**  Then that great-spirited Hanūmān, son of the wind-god, spoke again with humble words, for the sake of convincing Sītā.

**5.34.2**  "I am a monkey, O most fortunate lady, the envoy of the wise Rāma. Look, O goddess, at this ring inscribed with Rāma's name. Take heart; all will be well — your suffering is nearing its end."

**5.34.3**  Taking it and gazing at that ring which had adorned her husband's hand, Jānakī rejoiced as though she had recovered her lord himself.

**5.34.4**  Her lovely face — with eyes large, flushed and white — blazed with intense joy, like the moon king freed from Rāhu.

**5.34.5**  Then that modest, young woman, gladdened by her husband's message and fully satisfied on hearing the welcome news, praised the great monkey.

**5.34.6**  "You are brave, you are capable, you are wise, O best of monkeys — you alone have dared to enter this realm of rākṣasas."

**5.34.7**  "The ocean, a hundred yojanas wide, the abode of sea-monsters, was made a mere cow's hoof-print by your praiseworthy stride."

**5.34.8**  "I do not think you an ordinary monkey, O bull among monkeys, for you feel no terror or agitation before Rāvaṇa."

**5.34.9**  "You deserve to be addressed by me, O best of monkeys, if you are truly sent by that Rāma who knows the self."

**5.34.10**  "The invincible Rāma would not send one whose valor is untested and unknown, especially to come to me."

**5.34.11**  "By good fortune, Rāma the righteous-souled, devoted to dharma, is well; and the great-spirited Lakṣmaṇa, who increases the joy of Sumitrā, is well."

**5.34.12**  "If Kākutstha is well, why then does he not burn the ocean-girdled earth with his wrath, blazing like the fire at the world's end?"

**5.34.13**  "Or perhaps those two are capable even of subduing the gods, yet I think there is no reversal of my own sorrows."

**5.34.14**  "Does Rāma grieve? Does he not wander distracted? Does that best of men attend to the tasks that lie ahead?"

**5.34.15**  "Is he not wretched, not bewildered? Does he not lose himself amid his duties? Does that son of a king perform the deeds a man must do?"

**5.34.16**  "Does that scorcher of foes, desiring victory, employ the two-fold and three-fold strategies, and maintain bonds of friendship?"

**5.34.17**  "Does he secure allies and are his allies approaching him? Does he keep good company and is he honored by his friends?"

**5.34.18**  "Does that son of a king seek the grace of the gods? Does he rely on both human effort and divine fate?"

**5.34.19**  "Has Rāghava's love for me not faded on account of my absence? Will the monkey-hero deliver me from this calamity?"

**5.34.20**  "Ever accustomed to happiness and unaccustomed to misery, has Rāma, encountering this great sorrow, not fallen into despair?"

**5.34.21**  "And what of Kausalyā? What of Sumitrā likewise? Is tidings of Bharata's welfare regularly heard?"

**5.34.22**  "Is Rāghava, who deserves all honor, consumed by grief on my account? Is Rāma's mind not elsewhere? Will he save me?"

**5.34.23**  "Will Bharata, devoted to his brothers, send a terrible army with its banners, protected by ministers, for my sake?"

**5.34.24**  "Will Sugrīva, the glorious lord of the monkeys, come for my sake, surrounded by brave monkeys armed with teeth and claws?"

**5.34.25**  "Will the heroic Lakṣmaṇa, who increases the joy of Sumitrā, the knower of weapons, scatter the rākṣasas with his net of arrows?"

**5.34.26**  "Will I see, before long, Rāvaṇa and all his kinsmen slain in battle by Rāma with his terrible weapon?"

**5.34.27**  "Does not that face of his, golden in hue, fragrant as a lotus, wither with grief in my absence, like a lotus withered by the sun when the water dries?"

**5.34.28**  "He who felt no anguish, no fear, no grief when he renounced his kingdom under pretext of duty and led me on foot into the forest — does that one hold steadiness in his heart?"

**5.34.29**  "Neither his mother, nor his father, nor any other holds a love surpassing or equal to mine for him. I shall live only so long, like a messenger, as I can hear tidings of my beloved."

**5.34.30**  Having thus spoken to that lord of monkeys words weighty and sweet in meaning, Rāmā — Sītā — fell silent, longing to hear again his delightful words full of tidings about Rāma.

**5.34.31**  Having heard Sītā's words, the son of the wind-god, of fearsome prowess, placed his joined palms upon his head and spoke in reply.

**5.34.32**  "Lotus-eyed Rāma does not know that you are here. But upon hearing my report, Rāghava will come swiftly."

**5.34.33**  "Leading his great army swarming with monkeys and bears, Kākutstha will pin down the ocean — the abode of Varuṇa, that which cannot be disturbed — with torrents of arrows, and will make this city of Laṅkā free of rākṣasas."

**5.34.34**  "If Death itself, if the gods together with the asuras, should stand in Rāma's path, he will slay them too."

**5.34.35**  "Flooded with the grief born of not seeing you, O noble lady, Rāma finds no peace, like an elephant tormented by a lion."

**5.34.36**  "By the Dardara mountain, O goddess, I swear to you, and by roots and fruits, by Malaya, Vindhya, Meru, and Mandara."

**5.34.37**  "As surely as you shall behold Rāma's face — beautiful-eyed, lovely, with lips red as the bimba fruit, adorned with fair earrings, risen like the full moon."

**5.34.38**  "You will soon see Rāma, O Vaidehī, on Mount Prasravaṇa, seated like Indra of a hundred sacrifices on the summit of heaven."

**5.34.39**  "Rāghava eats no meat and takes no honey; he eats only forest food, properly prepared, at the fifth hour."

**5.34.40**  "Rāghava does not remove from his body the biting flies, the mosquitoes, the insects, or the crawling creatures, his inmost self entirely absorbed in you."

**5.34.41**  "Rāma is ever given to meditation, ever overwhelmed with grief; he thinks of nothing else — he is utterly subject to his longing."

**5.34.42**  "Rāma is ever sleepless; even that best of men, when he does sleep, wakes uttering the sweet syllables 'Sītā'."

**5.34.43**  "On seeing a fruit or a flower or anything else that would delight a woman, he sighs again and again and speaks as if to you: 'Ah, my beloved!'"

**5.34.44**  "He, O goddess, ever burning in anguish, ever calling out your name 'Sītā', that great-souled prince firm in his vow, makes every effort solely for the sake of winning you back."

**5.34.45**  She, the daughter of Videha, her grief dispersed by the recounting of Rāma's virtues yet equal to Rāma's own grief in sorrow, became like the night at the close of autumn with its moon breaking through the last of the clouds.

**5.35.1**  Sītā, her face like the full moon, having heard those words, spoke thus to Hanūmān — words joined with righteousness and meaning.

**5.35.2**  "What you, O monkey, have spoken is nectar mixed with poison: that Rāma's mind is not elsewhere — and yet that he is sunk in grief."

**5.35.3**  "Whether in the vastness of prosperity or in the most terrible adversity, fate drags a man about like one bound by a rope."

**5.35.4**  "Fate for living beings is surely irresistible, O best of monkeys — behold Saumitrī, and me, and Rāma, all bewildered by calamities."

**5.35.5**  "When will Rāghava reach the far shore of this sorrow, like one exhausted from swimming, whose boat has been wrecked in the ocean?"

**5.35.6**  "When will my lord, having destroyed the rākṣasas, having slain Rāvaṇa, having uprooted Laṅkā, come to see me?"

**5.35.7**  "He is to be told: 'Make haste' — for as long as this year is not completed, only so long is my life."

**5.35.8**  "The tenth month has come; two remain, O monkey. Such was the term set for me by the cruel Rāvaṇa."

**5.35.9**  "His brother Vibhīṣaṇa has earnestly beseeched him to restore me, yet Rāvaṇa will not set his mind to it."

**5.35.10**  "Returning me does not please Rāvaṇa — death, subject to time, seeks Rāvaṇa in battle."

**5.35.11**  "The eldest daughter of Vibhīṣaṇa, named Analā, O monkey, was sent by her own mother, and it is she who disclosed this to me."

**5.35.12**  "There is a wise and learned bull among rākṣasas named Avindhya, steadfast and virtuous, aged, and greatly honored by Rāvaṇa."

**5.35.13**  "He urged Rāvaṇa that the destruction of the rākṣasas would come from Rāma, yet that corrupt-souled one does not listen to even his beneficial counsel."

**5.35.14**  "I have hope, O best of monkeys: my lord will reach me soon. My inner self is pure, and in him there are many virtues."

**5.35.15**  "Energy, manhood, valor, compassion, gratitude, heroism, and might — these dwell in the monkey-lord and in Rāghava."

**5.35.16**  "He who slew fourteen thousand rākṣasas at Janasthāna without a brother at his side — what enemy would not tremble before him?"

**5.35.17**  "That bull among men cannot be measured by calamities. I know his glory, as Pulomā's daughter knows that of Indra."

**5.35.18**  "The heroic sun that is Rāma, O monkey, with its rays of arrow-nets, will dry up the water that is filled with the filth of enemies and rākṣasas."

**5.35.19**  As she thus spoke on, emaciated by grief for Rāma's sake, her face filled with tears, the monkey Hanūmān addressed her.

**5.35.20**  "Upon hearing my report, Rāghava will come swiftly, leading his great army swarming with troops of monkeys and bears."

**5.35.21**  "Or else I will free you this very day from the rākṣasa and from this sorrow. Climb upon my back, O blameless one."

**5.35.22**  "With you seated on my back I shall cross the ocean; I have the strength to carry even all of Laṅkā with Rāvaṇa."

**5.35.23**  "I shall deliver you today, O Maithilī, to Rāghava dwelling on Mount Prasravaṇa, as fire delivers the oblation to Indra."

**5.35.24**  "You will see this very day, O Vaidehī, Rāghava together with Lakṣmaṇa, fully resolved for action, like Viṣṇu set upon the slaying of the daityas."

**5.35.25**  "Fired with zeal by the sight of you, mighty, stationed at the hermitage, seated like Purandara upon the head of the king of serpents."

**5.35.26**  "Mount my back, O goddess; do not hesitate, O beautiful one. Seek your union with Rāma as Rohiṇī seeks the moon."

**5.35.27**  "Like one who speaks with the moon, like Suvarcalā with the sun — mounting my back, you shall cross the great sky-ocean of stars."

**5.35.28**  "For when I set forth taking you away from here, O lady, not all the inhabitants of Laṅkā are capable of following my course."

**5.35.29**  "Just as I arrived here, so without doubt shall I depart — see, O Vaidehī, I will lift you and go through the sky."

**5.35.30**  Maithilī, having heard from that best of monkeys these astonishing words, her every limb thrilled with wonder and joy, then spoke to Hanūmān.

**5.35.31**  "Hanūmān, how do you wish to carry me on the long journey? That, I think, is indeed your monkey-nature, O leader of monkey-troops."

**5.35.32**  "How can you, with so small a body, wish to take me from here to the presence of the lord of the monkey-host, my husband, O bull among monkeys?"

**5.35.33**  Hearing Sītā's words, the glorious Hanūmān, son of the wind-god, reflected — a fresh slight had been given to him.

**5.35.34**  "The dark-eyed one does not know my strength or my power; therefore let Vaidehī see whatever form of mine I choose."

**5.35.35**  Having reflected thus, that best of monkeys, the crusher of foes Hanūmān, thereupon revealed his true form to Vaidehī.

**5.35.36**  That wise bull among monkeys leaped down from that tree and then began to grow in size, for the sake of convincing Sītā.

**5.35.37**  Like Meru or Mandara in appearance, blazing with the radiance of a bright fire, that bull among monkeys stood before Sītā.

**5.35.38**  That monkey, mountain-like, with a copper-red face, of great strength, his teeth and claws hard as thunderbolts, terrible, spoke these words to Vaidehī.

**5.35.39**  "I have the power to carry away this very Laṅkā, with its tracts of forest and mountains, with its towers and walls and gateways, together with its lord."

**5.35.40**  "Therefore abandon that thought; enough of doubt, O goddess. Make Rāghava and Lakṣmaṇa free from sorrow, O Vaidehī."

**5.35.41**  Seeing him, mountain-like, Janaka's daughter, she of wide lotus-petal eyes, spoke to the true-born son of the wind-god.

**5.35.42**  "I know your valor and your strength, O great monkey; your speed is like the wind's, your energy wonderful as fire's."

**5.35.43**  "How could any ordinary being deserve to cross to the far shore of this immeasurable ocean and come to this land, O bull among monkeys?"

**5.35.44**  "I know your power in going and in bringing me; the success of our enterprise here must be quickly considered."

**5.35.45**  "Yet it would be improper for me to go with you, O best of monkeys; the speed of your speed — swift as the wind — would bewilder me."

**5.35.46**  "Suspended in the sky, above the ocean, I would fall from your back out of fear as you go with speed."

**5.35.47**  "Fallen into the ocean, teeming with timi-fish, sharks, and great sea-creatures, helpless, I would quickly become the choicest meal of the denizens of the deep."

**5.35.48**  "And I cannot go together with you, O destroyer of foes; even in you, who have a wife, doubt would arise, without question."

**5.35.49**  "Seeing me being carried away, the rākṣasas of fearsome prowess, dispatched by the evil-souled Rāvaṇa, would pursue us."

**5.35.50**  "Surrounded by those warriors brandishing spears in their hands, you — a hero with a wife to protect — would be placed in peril on my account."

**5.35.51**  "The many rākṣasas are armed, in the sky, while you are without weapons — how will you be able to fight and protect me at the same time?"

**5.35.52**  "While you fight those cruel-acting rākṣasas, I, overcome with fear, would fall from your back, O best of monkeys."

**5.35.53**  "Or if those fearsome, great, and powerful rākṣasas should by some chance overcome you in combat, O best of monkeys —"

**5.35.54**  "— or as you fight, your back turned away, I might fall; and having seized me fallen, the wicked rākṣasas would carry me off."

**5.35.55**  "They might snatch me from your grasp or even kill me — for victory and defeat in battle are seen to be inconstant."

**5.35.56**  "Or I might perish, terrorized by the rākṣasas; your effort, O best of monkeys, would then be entirely fruitless."

**5.35.57**  "You may well be capable of slaying all the rākṣasas, but Rāghava's glory would be diminished if the rākṣasas were slain by you."

**5.35.58**  "Or the rākṣasas might take me away and conceal me in a hidden place where neither the monkeys nor Rāghava could find me."

**5.35.59**  "This undertaking on my behalf would thus be rendered meaningless for you; but great is the merit in Rāma himself coming with you."

**5.35.60**  "On my survival depends the life of the great-souled Rāghava, of his brothers, O mighty-armed one, of you, and of the royal lineage."

**5.35.61**  "Those two, despairing of me, emaciated by the burning of grief, together with all the bears and monkeys, would give up their hold on life."

**5.35.62**  "Putting my devotion to my husband foremost, O monkey, I would not wish the touch of any man's body but Rāma's, O best of monkeys."

**5.35.63**  "As for Rāvaṇa's touch of my body — that was by force; what could I do, being powerless, without a protector, helpless?"

**5.35.64**  "If Rāma were to slay the ten-headed one here, together with all the rākṣasas, and then take me from this place — that would be fitting for him."

**5.35.65**  "I have heard of and seen the feats of valor of that great-souled crusher of foes — neither gods, gandharvas, serpents, nor rākṣasas are equal to Rāma in battle."

**5.35.66**  "Who, seeing in battle that Rāghava of great strength, wielder of the beautiful bow, equal in valor to Vāsava, together with Lakṣmaṇa — who could endure him, blazing like fire fanned by wind?"

**5.35.67**  "Who could endure in battle, O chief of monkeys, Rāghava with Lakṣmaṇa, the crusher of foes, standing like a maddened elephant of the quarters, his arrows blazing like the sun at the world's end?"

**5.35.68**  "Therefore, O best of monkeys, bring my lord here quickly, together with Lakṣmaṇa and the monkey-chiefs; O chief of monkeys, make me who have long been emaciated by grief rejoice."

**5.36.1**  Then that tiger among monkeys, gladdened by those words, having heard them, spoke to Sītā — he who was skilled in speech.

**5.36.2**  "What you have spoken, O goddess of auspicious appearance, is fitting — befitting the nature of women and the modesty of virtuous women."

**5.36.3**  "Womankind is indeed not capable of crossing the vast ocean, a hundred yojanas in extent, riding upon me."

**5.36.4**  "And the second reason you give, O lady endowed with modesty — 'I do not deserve the touch of any man but Rāma' — this is indeed true, O Jānakī."

**5.36.5**  "This is fitting, O goddess, for the wife of that great-souled one. For who other than you, O goddess, would speak such words?"

**5.36.6**  "And Kākutstha shall hear everything without remainder — all that you have done, O goddess, and all that you have spoken before me."

**5.36.7**  "For many reasons, O goddess, out of my desire to do what is dear to Rāma, with a heart overflowing with affection, I spoke this to you."

**5.36.8**  "Because of the difficulty of entering Laṅkā, the uncrossability of the great ocean, and my own ability — this is why I proposed it."

**5.36.9**  "I wish to bring you to the kinsman of Raghu's line this very day, out of deep affection and devotion — not otherwise was that proposed."

**5.36.10**  "But if you are not willing to go with me, O blameless one, then give me a token of recognition by which Rāghava may know."

**5.36.11**  Addressed thus by Hanūmān, Sītā — equal to a daughter of the gods — spoke slowly, her syllables strung with tears.

**5.36.12**  "This is the best token of recognition; speak it to my beloved: at the northeastern foot of Mount Citrakūṭa —

**5.36.13**  "— where one who dwells in the ascetic hermitage is provided with abundant roots, fruits, and water, in that place of accomplished sages, not far from the Mandākinī river —

**5.36.14**  "— in the groves of that place, fragrant with manifold flowers, having bathed and played in the water, wet with water I sat in your lap."

**5.36.15**  "And the elder brother of Bharata — you — fell asleep in my lap in turn."

**5.36.16**  "Then a crow, eager for flesh, pecked at me with its beak; I took up a clod of earth to drive away that crow."

**5.36.17**  "The crow wounded me and hid in that very place, and even so did not cease from the flesh — that eater of offerings, craving food."

**5.36.18**  "As I was pulling at the cord of my garment, angry at the bird, my robe slipping off — you saw me in that state."

**5.36.19**  "You laughed at me; I was angry, ashamed; wounded by that crow greedy for flesh, I approached you."

**5.36.20**  "Exhausted, I entered your lap again as you sat there; even as I was still angry, you consoled me with delight."

**5.36.21**  "My face filled with tears, slowly wiping my eyes, I was noticed by you, my lord, provoked by the crow."

**5.36.22**  "Hissing like an angry serpent, you spoke: 'Who has wounded the space between your breasts, O you of elephant-trunk-shaped thighs? Who plays with a five-hooded, wrathful serpent?'"

**5.36.23**  "Then looking about, you beheld that crow — its sharp talons bloodied — standing right before me."

**5.36.24**  "That crow is said to be the son of Indra, the best of birds, a rover of the earth's interior, equal in speed to the wind."

**5.36.25**  "Then upon that crow, O best among the wise, the mighty-armed one — his eyes rolling with anger — set a cruel intent."

**5.36.26**  "Taking a blade of darbha grass from the mat and charging it with Brahmā's weapon, he blazed like the time-fire aimed at the bird."

**5.36.27**  "You hurled that blazing reed at the crow; pursued, that bird moved through various courses in its desire for protection, ranging through all the worlds."

**5.36.28**  "Abandoned by its father, by all the gods, and by the great sages, having traversed the three worlds, it came to you alone for refuge."

**5.36.29**  "That one fallen to the ground, a refugee seeking shelter, though deserving of death, O Kākutstha, you protected out of compassion — for finding no safety in any of the worlds, it came to you alone for refuge."

**5.36.30**  "To that one coming before you, dejected and despondent, you said: 'Brahmā's weapon cannot be made fruitless; let it be directed somewhere.'"

**5.36.31**  "Then he struck out the right eye of that crow."

**5.36.32**  "He bowed to you then, and to King Daśaratha, and being released by you, O hero, returned to his own abode."

**5.36.33**  "For my sake Brahmā's weapon was invoked even for a mere crow; O lord of the earth, why do you tolerate him who robs me from you?"

**5.36.34**  "Show great energy, show compassion toward me, O bull among men — I have heard from you yourself that compassion is the highest dharma."

**5.36.35**  "I know you as one of great heroism, great energy, great strength, unfathomable in depth and unshakeable as the ocean, lord of the ocean-girdled earth, equal to Vāsava."

**5.36.36**  "Being thus the best knower of weapons, endowed with fortitude, and strong — why do you not employ your weapon against the rākṣasas, O Rāghava?"

**5.36.37**  "Neither the nāgas, nor the gandharvas, neither the asuras nor the hosts of Maruts are capable of withstanding Rāma's swiftness in battle."

**5.36.38**  "If there is any concern for me in that valorous one, why does he not bring the rākṣasas to their end with sharp arrows?"

**5.36.39**  "Or Lakṣmaṇa the scorcher of foes — taking his brother's command, that hero of great strength — for what reason does he not rescue me?"

**5.36.40**  "If those two tigers among men are equal in energy to the wind and Indra, invincible even to the gods — why do they disregard me?"

**5.36.41**  "There must be some great misdeed of mine, without doubt, since those two scorchers of foes, though capable, do not look to me."

**5.36.42**  "The noble Kausalyā who bore him, the lord of the world — ask after his welfare on my behalf and salute him with a bow of the head."

**5.36.43**  "Garlands, all gems, beloved noble women, and lordship over the broad earth, rare even for the great —"

**5.36.44**  "— Sumitrā, mother of fine sons, having honored and propitiated both father and mother, and accompanied Rāma in going forth, through her dharmic-souled son who set aside unparalleled comfort —"

**5.36.45**  "— that lion-shouldered, mighty-armed, resolute, and handsome one follows Kākutstha his brother, protecting him in the forest."

**5.36.46**  "He behaves toward Rāma as a father, toward me as a mother; yet that hero Lakṣmaṇa did not know I was being carried off at that time."

**5.36.47**  "A servant of elders, glorious, capable, not one for many words, that prince is most dear, worthy of my father-in-law."

**5.36.48**  "Rāma's brother Lakṣmaṇa is always dearer to him than I; whatever burden he is charged with, that valorous one carries it."

**5.36.49**  "Rāma, upon seeing him, never thinks even of the venerable and noble — tell him welfare on my behalf, at my word: gentle always, pure, skilled, dear to Rāma is Lakṣmaṇa."

**5.36.50**  "And say this to my heroic lord Rāma again and again: 'I will sustain my life for one month, O son of Daśaratha; beyond that month I cannot live — I tell you this truthfully.'"

**5.36.51**  "Me, hemmed in by that evil-acting Rāvaṇa by cunning — you, O hero, should rescue as Kauśikī from the netherworld."

**5.36.52**  Then Sītā, loosening from her garment the divine, beautiful cūḍāmaṇi crest-jewel, saying 'This is to be given to Rāghava,' gave it to Hanūmān.

**5.36.53**  The hero, having received that peerless jewel-gem, placed it on his finger — for his arm could not match her form.

**5.36.54**  The best of monkeys, having received the jewel-gem, paid his respects to Sītā, circumambulated her, and stood bowing at her side.

**5.36.55**  Filled with great joy born of the sight of Sītā, in his heart he had already gone to Rāma, though his body stood still.

**5.36.56**  Having taken up that supreme, most worthy jewel held by the daughter of King Janaka, freed by his power as one released by the wind stirring the king of mountains, with a joyful mind he set forth on his return journey.

**5.37.1**  Having given the jewel, Sītā then spoke to Hanūmān: "This token, truly known, is a recognized sign of Rāma."

**5.37.2**  "The hero Rāma, upon seeing this jewel, will surely remember three persons: his mother, me, and King Daśaratha."

**5.37.3**  "And you, O best of monkeys, urged on with renewed energy, reflect upon what is best to be done in the undertaking of this task."

**5.37.4**  "You are the authority in the execution of this task, O best of monkeys; consider that effort which would bring an end to this suffering."

**5.37.5**  Māruti, of fearsome prowess, having agreed with "So be it," bowed his head to the daughter of Videha and prepared to depart.

**5.37.6**  Knowing the son of the Wind to be ready to depart, the goddess Maithilī spoke these words in a voice choked with tears.

**5.37.7**  "O Hanūmān, convey my good wishes to Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa together, to Sugrīva with his ministers, and to all the elder monkeys."

**5.37.8**  "And you ought to arrange that Rāghava, that mighty-armed one, may rescue me from this flood of suffering that surrounds me."

**5.37.9**  "That the illustrious Rāma may know me to be alive—this must be told by you, O Hanūmān; speak the word and you shall attain righteousness."

**5.37.10**  "Hearing these words of mine, always full of urgency, the valor of the son of Daśaratha will grow mightily for the purpose of winning me back."

**5.37.11**  "Hearing words bearing my message from you, the heroic Rāghava will duly arrange the manner of his prowess."

**5.37.12**  Having heard those words of Sītā, Hanūmān, son of the Wind, placing his joined palms upon his head, spoke in reply.

**5.37.13**  "Kākutstha will come swiftly, surrounded by the foremost of monkeys and bears, and having conquered your enemies in battle, will remove your sorrow."

**5.37.14**  "I see none among mortals, nor among the gods, nor among the asuras, who would dare to stand before him in the face of his discharged arrows."

**5.37.15**  "He is capable of withstanding even the Sun, even Parjanya, even Yama the son of Vivasvat in battle—and especially so for your sake."

**5.37.16**  "He desires to rule the earth to the ocean's border; the victory of Rāma has you as its occasion, O daughter of Janaka."

**5.37.17**  Having heard those words of his—perfectly true and well-spoken—Jānakī held them in high regard and then spoke these words.

**5.37.18**  Then Sītā, gazing again and again at the one who was about to depart, out of deep affection surmised the loving words he would carry to her husband.

**5.37.19**  "Or if you think fit, O hero, O subduer of foes, remain one day in some secluded spot; rested, you may depart tomorrow."

**5.37.20**  "For through the company of you, O mighty one, even I, of little fortune, might find momentary relief from this great sorrow."

**5.37.21**  "For when you are gone, O tiger among monkeys, even the continuance of my life until your return will be in doubt—of this there is no question."

**5.37.22**  "The grief born of not seeing you will afflict me further, O monkey, inflaming me who am already tormented by sorrow upon sorrow."

**5.37.23**  "And this very great doubt stands before me, O hero, O lord of monkeys, concerning the monkey and bear troops who are your allies."

**5.37.24**  "How indeed shall those armies of monkeys and bears cross the great, impassable ocean? Or how shall those two sons of the noblest of men?"

**5.37.25**  "Among living beings, only three have the power to leap over this ocean: Vainateya, you yourself, or the Wind."

**5.37.26**  "So in the execution of this task, O hero, so hard to accomplish, what solution do you see? You are the foremost among those who understand what must be done."

**5.37.27**  "Certainly you alone are sufficient for the accomplishment of this task, O slayer of enemy heroes; your growth in power is glorious."

**5.37.28**  "But if the victorious Rāghava were to conquer Rāvaṇa in battle with his full forces and then return to his own city with me—that would bring him glory."

**5.37.29**  "If Kākutstha, the crusher of enemy armies, were to fill Laṅkā with troops and then lead me away—that would be befitting of him."

**5.37.30**  "Therefore arrange it so that the valor of that great-souled hero in battle may be in keeping with his nature as a warrior."

**5.37.31**  Having heard those words—purposeful, coherent, and supported by reasoning—Hanūmān spoke further in reply.

**5.37.32**  "O goddess, Sugrīva, lord of the armies of monkeys and bears, the best among those who leap, endowed with true strength, is firmly resolved for your sake."

**5.37.33**  "Surrounded by tens of millions of thousands of monkeys, that destroyer of rākṣasas will come swiftly, O Vaidehī."

**5.37.34**  "His monkeys, endowed with prowess, full of courage, of great strength, who fly as swift as thought's resolve, stand ready at his command."

**5.37.35**  "Their motion is not obstructed above, below, or sideways; of unlimited splendor, they do not falter in great deeds."

**5.37.36**  "By those ones of vast energy, who travel the path of the wind, the earth together with its ocean and mountains has been circumambulated more than once."

**5.37.37**  "There are forest-dwellers there who are both superior to me and equal to me; there is none inferior to me in Sugrīva's presence."

**5.37.38**  "I, who am merely the least, have arrived here—how much more so those of great strength! For it is not the foremost who are sent as messengers; it is the lesser ones who are sent."

**5.37.39**  "Therefore enough of this anguish, O goddess; let your sorrow depart. With a single bound the leaders of monkey troops will come to Laṅkā."

**5.37.40**  "And those two, like the risen sun and moon, those two beings of great nature, those two lion-men, riding upon my back, will come to your presence."

**5.37.41**  "For those two heroes, those best of men, Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa together, coming to the city of Laṅkā, will scatter it with their arrows."

**5.37.42**  "Having slain Rāvaṇa together with his followers, the joy of the Raghu clan, Rāghava, will return to his own city taking you with him, O lady of beautiful thighs."

**5.37.43**  "So take heart, may good fortune be yours; be patient, waiting for the right moment. Before long you will see Rāma blazing like fire in the wind."

**5.37.44**  "When the lord of rākṣasas has been slain along with his sons, ministers, and kinsmen, you will be united with Rāma as Rohiṇī with the moon."

**5.37.45**  "O goddess, O Maithilī, you will quickly reach the far shore of sorrow, and before long you will see Rāvaṇa slain by Rāma."

**5.37.46**  Having thus reassured Vaidehī, Hanūmān, son of the Wind, having resolved to depart, spoke to Vaidehī once more.

**5.37.47**  "You will soon see that slayer of enemies, the self-mastered Rāghava, and Lakṣmaṇa bow in hand, standing at the gate of Laṅkā."

**5.37.48**  "You will quickly see those heroic monkeys assembled—armed with claws and fangs, striding like lions and tigers, resembling lordly elephants."

**5.37.49**  "O noble lady, you will see the many troops of the chief monkeys—resembling mountains and rain-clouds—roaring upon the slopes of Laṅkā's Malaya peaks."

**5.37.50**  "And Rāma, struck to the vitals by the terrible arrow of the god of love, finds no peace—like an elephant tormented by a lion."

**5.37.51**  "Do not weep, O goddess, from grief; let there be nothing displeasing to your heart. Like Śacī blessed with Śakra, you have a lord and protector as your husband."

**5.37.52**  "Who else is superior to Rāma? Who is equal to the son of Sumitrā? Those two brothers, equal to fire and wind, are your refuge."

**5.37.53**  "You will not dwell long in this place, O goddess, this dreadful one inhabited by hordes of rākṣasas. The coming of your beloved will not be long delayed. Endure only the duration of the time until we meet again."

**5.38.1**  Having heard those words of the great-souled son of the Wind, Sītā, who resembles the daughter of a god, spoke words conducive to her own welfare.

**5.38.2**  "Upon seeing you, O monkey, who speak such dear words, I rejoice greatly—like the earth receiving rain when the crop is half-grown."

**5.38.3**  "Arrange it, out of compassion for me, that I may embrace that tiger among men with these limbs emaciated by grief—this is my desire."

**5.38.4**  "And a token of recognition from Rāma has been given to me, O foremost of monkey troops: the blade of grass thrown in anger at the crow that gouged out one of its eyes."

**5.38.5**  "A mark of red mineral dye was placed on my cheek; he should indeed remember that mark, which has now faded, O monkey."

**5.38.6**  "How can that mighty one, equal to Mahendra and Varuṇa, tolerate Sītā, who has been abducted, dwelling in the midst of rākṣasas?"

**5.38.7**  "This divine crest-jewel has been carefully guarded by me. Upon seeing it I rejoice—as I rejoice at seeing you, O sinless one, in my misfortune."

**5.38.8**  "This auspicious jewel, born of water, is hereby surrendered by me to you. Beyond this I shall not be able to live, consumed with longing in my grief."

**5.38.9**  "I endure unbearable sufferings and words that pierce the heart from those terrifying rākṣasī women—all for your sake."

**5.38.10**  "I shall sustain my life, O slayer of enemies, for one month; beyond one month I shall not live, O son of a king, separated from you."

**5.38.11**  "This rākṣasa king is terrible, and his gaze upon me brings no ease; and if I were to hear of your falling into misfortune, I could not live another moment."

**5.38.12**  Having heard the compassion-stirring words of Vaidehī, spoken amid tears, then the greatly resplendent Hanūmān, son of the Wind, spoke.

**5.38.13**  "Rāma is free of sorrow for none but you, O goddess—I swear to you in truth. And when Rāma is overwhelmed with grief, Lakṣmaṇa too burns with anguish."

**5.38.14**  "You have been found, somehow. This is not the time for lamenting. In this very moment you will see the end of your sorrows, O passionate lady."

**5.38.15**  "Those two tigers among men, those two blameless princes, their enthusiasm kindled by the sight of you, will reduce Laṅkā to ashes."

**5.38.16**  "Having slain the cruel Rāvaṇa together with his kinsmen in battle, both Rāghavas will bring you to your own city, O wide-eyed one."

**5.38.17**  "But whatever token Rāma might recognize, O blameless one, that which will generate delight in him—you ought to give yet another such."

**5.38.18**  She said: "The finest token has already been given by me here. For upon seeing this hair ornament of mine, O Hanūmān, your words, O hero, will be held credible by Rāma."

**5.38.19**  Taking that finest of jewels, the auspicious best of leapers bowed his head to the goddess and prepared to depart.

**5.38.20**  Beholding that bull among monkeys whose enthusiasm had risen for the leap, who was growing in his great velocity, the daughter of Janaka, wretched, her face filled with tears, spoke in a voice choked with sobs.

**5.38.21**  "Hanūmān, convey my good health to the two brothers Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa, who resemble lions, and to Sugrīva with his ministers—to all of them."

**5.38.22**  "And you must arrange that Rāghava, that mighty-armed one, may rescue me from this flood of sorrow that engulfs me."
