**5.38.23**  "And this fierce surge of my sorrow, and the threats made by these rākṣasas—convey all this when you have come into Rāma's presence. May your path be auspicious, O hero among monkeys."

**5.38.24**  That monkey, informed of the princess's wishes, his task accomplished, his heart overjoyed, surveying the little that remained to be done, turned his mind northward.

**5.39.1**  Honored by her with words of praise as he was about to depart, the monkey withdrew from that place and began to reflect.

**5.39.2**  "This task is little short of complete; the dark-eyed lady has been seen. Passing over three of the four means of policy, a fourth presents itself here."

**5.39.3**  "Conciliation avails nothing with rākṣasas; gifts do not apply among those who dwell in accumulated wealth; those intoxicated with the power of their army cannot be won over by sowing dissension. But valor—this is what pleases me here."

**5.39.4**  "No resolution of this task is possible here without valor. For the rākṣasas, their champions slain in battle, might perhaps somehow come to a state of softness."

**5.39.5**  "One who is appointed to a specific task but who might accomplish many things without obstructing the primary task—he may indeed perform those things."

**5.39.6**  "For no single means alone accomplishes even the slightest task here. One who knows a purpose from many angles is capable of its accomplishment."

**5.39.7**  "Having resolved here and now—if I were to go back to the abode of the lord of monkeys, I, who know the essence of the crushing contest between self and enemy, then my master's command would be considered fulfilled."

**5.39.8**  "How then might a battle forced upon me today with the rākṣasas come about pleasingly? And in the same way, might the ten-faced one measure up in combat to my own essential strength?"

**5.39.9**  "This finest grove of that cruel one, comparable to the garden of the gods, pleasing to the eye and heart, filled with many kinds of trees and creepers—"

**5.39.10**  "—this I will destroy, as fire destroys a dry forest. When this is destroyed, Rāvaṇa will then be moved to anger."

**5.39.11**  "Then the lord of rākṣasas will dispatch a great force complete with horses, great chariots, and elephants, armed with tridents, iron bars, and battle-axes—and then this great battle will ensue."

**5.39.12**  "But I, meeting those rākṣasas of fierce valor in combat, of unimpeded prowess, having slain that army dispatched by Rāvaṇa, will depart gladly to the abode of the lord of monkeys."

**5.39.13**  Then Māruti, angry as the wind itself, of fearsome prowess, with the great force of his thighs, began to hurl down the trees.

**5.39.14**  Then the heroic Hanūmān broke apart that pleasure grove, resounding with the calls of intoxicated birds, filled with many kinds of trees and creepers.

**5.39.15**  That grove, with its trees torn up and its pools broken apart and its hilltops pulverized, became a sight unpleasant to behold.

**5.39.16**  With its bower-houses of creepers and its painted houses destroyed, its great serpents and wild beasts driven out, its stone-houses overturned and its buildings shattered, that great grove lost all semblance of its former form.

**5.39.17**  That great monkey, having done great offense to the heart of that wealthy lord, blazing with splendor, desiring single combat with many of great strength, took his stand at the gateway.

**5.40.1**  Then, at the clamour of birds and the crashing of fallen trees, all the inhabitants of Laṅkā were seized with terror and confusion.

**5.40.2**  Beasts and birds fled shrieking in fear, and terrible omens befell the rākṣasas.

**5.40.3**  Then, when sleep had left them, the rākṣasī guards — their faces grotesque — beheld the grove in ruin and that great heroic monkey standing there.

**5.40.4**  When the long-armed, mighty-spirited, immensely powerful one saw them, he assumed a form so tremendous as to strike fear into the rākṣasīs.

**5.40.5**  Having seen that vānara — colossal as a mountain, immeasurably powerful — the rākṣasīs then questioned the daughter of Janaka.

**5.40.6**  "Who is this, whose is he, where does he come from, and for what purpose has he arrived here? And how did conversation come to pass between him and you?"

**5.40.7**  "Tell us, wide-eyed one — have no fear, fortunate lady, dark-cornered eyes — what did this one do, conversing with you?"

**5.40.8**  Then spoke the virtuous Sītā, beautiful in every limb: "What capacity have I to know rākṣasas who assume any form at will?"

**5.40.9**  "You yourselves know who he is and what he intends to do. A serpent alone truly knows a serpent's ways — there is no doubt of this."

**5.40.10**  "I too am afraid of him; I do not know his lineage. I take him to be a rākṣasa who has come here having assumed any form he wished."

**5.40.11**  Having heard the words of the Vaidehī, some rākṣasīs remained hurriedly where they stood, while others went off swiftly to report to Rāvaṇa.

**5.40.12**  The rākṣasīs with their grotesque faces drew near to Rāvaṇa and began to describe the dreadful, mis-shapen monkey.

**5.40.13**  "O king, within the Aśoka grove a monkey of terrifying form stands there, of boundless prowess, having held converse with Sītā."

**5.40.14**  "And fawn-eyed Jānakī Sītā, though questioned by us repeatedly, is unwilling to reveal who that monkey is."

**5.40.15**  "He may be a messenger of Vāsava, or a messenger of Vaiśravaṇa, or one sent by Rāma with desire to search out Sītā."

**5.40.16**  "By him, assuming a form suited to his power, your charming pleasure-grove thronged with various herds of animals has been entirely laid waste."

**5.40.17**  "There is not a single spot he has not destroyed — save only that spot where Jānakī Sītā sits, that alone he left unharmed."

**5.40.18**  "Whether out of intent to protect Jānakī, or out of weariness — weariness is unimaginable in such a one — she alone was guarded by him."

**5.40.19**  "Even the tall Śiṃśapā tree, adorned with lovely shoots and leaves, beneath which Sītā herself had taken her seat — that too he left protected."

**5.40.20**  "For this one of terrible aspect, you should ordain a terrible punishment: he it was who spoke with Sītā and who laid waste your grove."

**5.40.21**  "O lord of the host of rākṣasas, who would dare address your Sītā — she who is held in your heart — unless he were one who has already abandoned regard for his life?"

**5.40.22**  Hearing the words of the rākṣasīs, Rāvaṇa, lord of rākṣasas, blazed with fury like a sacrificial fire, his eyes whirling with wrath.

**5.40.23**  The mighty-splendoured one commanded a troop of rākṣasas called kiṃkaras — heroes fit to match himself — to seize Hanūmān.

**5.40.24**  From that palace there issued forth eighty thousand of those swift kiṃkaras, iron maces and hammers in hand.

**5.40.25**  Huge-bellied, with great fangs, terrible in form, immensely powerful, all were eager for battle, intent on capturing Hanūmān.

**5.40.26**  They rushed upon that monkey where he stood at the gateway arch, charging with great speed like moths hurling themselves into a flame.

**5.40.27**  They struck the foremost of vānaras with ornate maces, with iron bars with golden armlets, and with arrows blazing like the sun.

**5.40.28**  The splendid, glorious Hanūmān, like a mountain in form, slapped his tail upon the ground and let out a great roar.

**5.40.29**  At the sound of his roar they were seized with terror, and they beheld Hanūmān towering like a rain-cloud at the horizon.

**5.40.30**  Yet undeterred by their master's command, those rākṣasas fell upon the monkey from every side with diverse and terrible weapons.

**5.40.31**  Surrounded on all sides by those warriors, the immensely powerful one seized a dreadful iron bar that lay propped against the gateway.

**5.40.32**  Taking up that iron bar, he struck down the night-roaming rākṣasas.

**5.40.33**  As Garuḍa, son of Vinatā, might seize a writhing serpent, so that hero, the son of the wind-god, grasping that bar, ranged through the air.

**5.40.34**  Having slain those rākṣasa kiṃkaras, the heroic son of the wind-god, still eager for battle, returned and stood again at the gateway.

**5.40.35**  Then those few rākṣasas who had escaped from that terror reported to Rāvaṇa that all the kiṃkaras had been slain.

**5.40.36**  On hearing that his mighty host of rākṣasas had been slain, the king's eyes rolled in fury, and he commanded Prahastas's son — unmatched in valour, most difficult to overcome in battle.

**5.41.1**  Then Hanūmān, having slain the kiṃkaras, fell into reflection: "The grove has been destroyed by me, but the shrine-palace has not been demolished. I shall therefore demolish this palace too."

**5.41.2**  Having thus resolved, Hanūmān — displaying his power in his very bearing — leapt to the shrine-palace that rose lofty as the peak of Meru, and the foremost of monkeys, son of the wind-god, scaled it.

**5.41.3**  And having subdued it, the irresistible Hanūmān, blazing with splendour atop the towering shrine-palace, resembled the Pāriyātra mountain.

**5.41.4**  Then Hanūmān, son of the wind-god, assuming his vast body, boldly struck his arms together, filling Laṅkā with the sound.

**5.41.5**  By that great ear-shattering clap, birds were hurled from the sky, and from on high he proclaimed aloud:

**5.41.6**  "Victorious is the supremely powerful Rāma! Victorious is the greatly powerful Lakṣmaṇa! Victorious is King Sugrīva, protected by Rāghava!"

**5.41.7**  "I am Hanūmān, son of the wind-god, servant of Rāma lord of the Kosalas — he of unwearied deeds — the slayer of enemy armies!"

**5.41.8**  "A thousand Rāvaṇas would be no match for me in battle, as I strike with thousands of rocks and trees!"

**5.41.9**  "Having ravaged the city of Laṅkā and paid homage to the Maithilī, my mission accomplished, I shall depart before the eyes of all the rākṣasas!"

**5.41.10**  Having thus spoken, that bull among monkeys, stationed atop the aerial palace on the shrine-terrace, let out a fearsome roar, spreading terror among the rākṣasas.

**5.41.11**  At that great sound, a hundred shrine-guards advanced, bearing various missiles, javelins, swords, and battle-axes; hurling them in deadly volleys they surrounded the son of the wind-god.

**5.41.12**  That host of rākṣasas, encircling the foremost of monkeys, appeared like the wide, mighty swirling eddy of the Gaṅgā's waters.

**5.41.13**  Then the son of the wind-god, furious, assumed a terrible form.

**5.41.14**  With great speed Hanūmān, the mighty son of the wind-god, wrenched out a great pillar of that palace, adorned with gold, and whirled it, that weapon of a hundred edges.

**5.41.15**  Having slain a hundred rākṣasas as Indra slays demons with his thunderbolt, the glorious one, stationed in the sky, spoke these words:

**5.41.16**  "Thousands like me — mighty monkey-lords, powerful, all under Sugrīva's command — have been dispatched by the great-souled one."

**5.41.17**  "Sugrīva will come with hundreds, with hundreds of thousands, with crores, with tens of thousands — to exterminate every one of you!"

**5.41.18**  "This city of Laṅkā will cease to exist, and you will be no more, and Rāvaṇa no more — because the great-souled lord of the Ikṣvāku line has sworn enmity against you!"

**5.42.1**  Commanded by the lord of rākṣasas, the powerful son of Prahastas — Jambumālī of the great fangs — marched out, bearing his bow.

**5.42.2**  He wore red garlands and red garments, was garlanded with flowers, wore bright earrings; large, with rolling eyes, fierce, unconquerable in battle.

**5.42.3**  He twanged his great bow — rivalling Indra's own bow — with its beautiful arrows, speedily, with a sound equal to the thunderbolt and the lightning.

**5.42.4**  By the twanging report of that great bow, the cardinal directions, the intermediate quarters, and the sky itself were suddenly filled.

**5.42.5**  Beholding him approach in a chariot yoked to mules, the swift Hanūmān both rejoiced and roared.

**5.42.6**  Jambumālī of the mighty arms pierced Hanūmān, that great monkey standing on the gateway arch, with sharp arrows.

**5.42.7**  He struck the lord of monkeys with a crescent-headed shaft in the face, with a single barbed arrow on the head, and pierced his two arms with ten iron-headed arrows.

**5.42.8**  His copper-red face, struck by the arrow, shone beautifully — like a full-blown lotus in autumn pierced by the rays of the sun.

**5.42.9**  The great monkey, struck by the rākṣasa's arrows, grew enraged; then he noticed nearby an enormous great boulder.

**5.42.10**  The powerful one wrenched it up swiftly and hurled it mightily; but the furious rākṣasa shattered it with ten arrows.

**5.42.11**  Seeing that effort come to nothing, Hanūmān of fierce valour, mighty in energy, uprooted a vast śāla tree and whirled it.

**5.42.12**  Seeing the immensely powerful monkey whirling that śāla tree, the mighty Jambumālī let fly a great many arrows.

**5.42.13**  He cut the śāla tree with four arrows, struck the monkey with five on each arm, with one arrow in the chest, and with ten in the broad expanse of his breast.

**5.42.14**  His body pierced full of arrows, seized by immense rage, Hanūmān snatched up that very iron bar and whirled it swiftly.

**5.42.15**  The exceedingly swift one, of overwhelming power, whirled and then drove that iron bar down upon the broad chest of Jambumālī.

**5.42.16**  Of him thereafter — no head remained, nor arms, nor knees; no bow, no chariot, no horses were to be seen there, nor his arrows.

**5.42.17**  Thus swiftly slain by him, Jambumālī the great warrior fell dead to the ground, his ornaments and limbs crushed to powder.

**5.42.18**  Hearing that Jambumālī and the mighty kiṃkaras had been slain, Rāvaṇa blazed with fury, his eyes reddened with wrath.

**5.42.19**  His eyes copper-red with swelling rage at the slaying of the mighty son of Prahastas, the lord of night-roaming rākṣasas swiftly commanded the sons of his ministers — men of exceeding valour and prowess.

**5.43.1**  Then those seven sons of the ministers, impelled by the lord of rākṣasas, went forth from that palace, each blazing with the radiance of the seven flames of fire.

**5.43.2**  Attended by great armies, bearing bows, mighty in strength, foremost among those versed in weapons and warfare, each eager to surpass the others in victory.

**5.43.3**  They rode in great chariots covered with a lattice of gold, bearing standards and banners, with a roar like the sound of thunderclouds, yoked with swift horses.

**5.43.4**  Those warriors of immeasurable valor drew their bows adorned with refined gold, and, exulting, they shone like clouds charged with lightning.

**5.43.5**  Then the mothers of those warriors, learning that the kiṃkara guards had been slain, were overwhelmed with grief and anguish, together with their kinsmen and friends.

**5.43.6**  They, clashing against one another, adorned with ornaments of refined gold, rushed upon Hanūmān as he stood stationed at the gateway.

**5.43.7**  Pouring forth showers of arrows, with a sound like the roar of chariots, those foremost among the rākṣasas moved about like rain-laden clouds.

**5.43.8**  Then Hanūmān, pelted by those showers of arrows, became covered in form, like the king of mountains enveloped by rain.

**5.43.9**  That swift monkey evaded their arrows and the charging speed of those heroes' chariots as he moved through the clear sky.

**5.43.10**  That hero, sporting against those bow-bearing warriors in the sky, shone forth as the mighty wind sports among bow-shaped clouds in the heavens.

**5.43.11**  Uttering a terrible roar and terrifying that great army, Hanūmān, full of valor, charged with speed against those rākṣasas.

**5.43.12**  That scorcher of foes smote some with his palm, some with his feet; he struck some with his fist and tore others apart with his nails.

**5.43.13**  The monkey crushed some with his chest, others with his thighs; and some fell to the ground right there from the mere sound of his roar.

**5.43.14**  Then, when those warriors had been felled and fallen to the ground, that entire army fled in all ten directions, overwhelmed with fear.

**5.43.15**  The elephants trumpeted discordantly, the horses fell to the ground, and the earth was strewn with chariots whose housings, standards, and canopies were shattered.

**5.43.16**  That mighty monkey of fierce valor, having slain those formidable rākṣasas, eager to fight still more rākṣasas, returned once again to that very gateway as a hero.

**5.44.1**  Rāvaṇa, concealing his inner state, upon learning that the sons of his ministers had been slain by the great-souled monkey, formed a supreme resolve.

**5.44.2**  He summoned Virūpākṣa, Yūpākṣa, the rākṣasa Durdhara, Praghasa, and Bhāsakarṇa — five leading commanders of his army.

**5.44.3**  The ten-necked one commanded those heroes, skilled in statecraft, eager for the capture of Hanūmān, who were equal to the wind in battle-speed.

**5.44.4**  "Go, all of you, at the vanguard of the army, attended by mighty forces, with horses, chariots, and elephants — let that monkey be subdued."

**5.44.5**  "And whatever outcome befalls them — upon engaging that forest-dweller — an action must be taken that does not contradict the demands of place and time."

**5.44.6**  "For I do not regard that monkey as merely a monkey when I reason from his deeds — in every way he is a great being attended by great power; he may have been created against us by Indra through the force of his austerities."

**5.44.7**  "The nāgas, yakṣas, gandharvas, gods, asuras, and great sages have all been vanquished by me together with all of you."

**5.44.8**  "By them some offense must certainly be carried out against us — of that there is no doubt; therefore seize him by force."

**5.44.9**  "Nor should you underestimate this monkey of fierce prowess — I have indeed seen monkeys who are swift and of vast valor."

**5.44.10**  "Vālin and Sugrīva, and Jāmbavān of great might, and Nīla the general, and others beginning with Dvivida —"

**5.44.11**  "— none of them possess a terrifying speed, nor radiance, nor valor, nor intelligence, nor exuberance of strength, nor a form conceived like this one's."

**5.44.12**  "This being in the form of a monkey must be known as a great entity — therefore, making great effort, let his capture be accomplished."

**5.44.13**  "Truly, the three worlds together with Indra, the gods, asuras, and men are not capable of standing before you on the battlefield."

**5.44.14**  "Nevertheless, by one who is versed in wise counsel and who desires victory in battle, the self must be protected with care — for the outcome of war is uncertain."

**5.44.15**  All those of great energy received their master's command and rushed forth with great speed, blazing like consecrated fire.

**5.44.16**  With chariots, with frenzied elephants, with horses of great swiftness, with various sharp weapons, and attended by their full forces.

**5.44.17**  Then those heroes saw the great monkey blazing, rising like the sun with its rays, crowned with the garland of his own radiant beams.

**5.44.18**  Standing at the gateway — of great speed, great spirit, great strength, great intelligence, great enthusiasm, great body, great might.

**5.44.19**  Beholding him, all of them took their positions in every direction and attacked from all sides with their respective terrible weapons.

**5.44.20**  Five iron arrows, keen and white with golden tips, resembling lotus petals, were shot by Durdhara into his head.

**5.44.21**  Struck in the head by those five arrows, the monkey leaped up roaring into the sky, making the ten directions resound with his cry.

**5.44.22**  Then the mighty hero Durdhara, in his chariot with bow readied, raining hundreds upon hundreds of arrows, pressed his attack with great force.

**5.44.23**  That monkey repelled him in the sky as he poured his rain of arrows, as the wind repels a rain-laden cloud at the end of the monsoon.

**5.44.24**  Harassed by Durdhara, the son of the wind god uttered another great roar, and increased his own swiftness.

**5.44.25**  That monkey of great velocity suddenly leaped from a distance and descended upon Durdhara's chariot, like a bolt of lightning upon a mountain.

**5.44.26**  Then Durdhara, with his eight horses crushed and the axle and yoke of his chariot broken, abandoned it and fell to the ground, his life relinquished.

**5.44.27**  Virūpākṣa and Yūpākṣa, seeing him fallen to the ground, their wrath kindled, those two irresistible crushers of enemies leaped into the air.

**5.44.28**  That long-armed monkey, suddenly leaping upward and standing in the clear sky, was struck in the chest by those two with their war-clubs.

**5.44.29**  The mighty monkey, equal to Garuḍa in valor, repelled the speed of those two swift warriors and descended again to the ground.

**5.44.30**  Reaching a śāla tree and uprooting it, that monkey, son of the wind god, slew both those rākṣasa warriors.

**5.44.31**  Then, learning that those three had been slain by the swift monkey, Praghasa of great speed forcibly charged at the monkey.

**5.44.32**  And the valiant Bhāsakarṇa, enraged, taking up his trident, both of them stood on one side against that tiger among monkeys, that glorious one.

**5.44.33**  Praghasa struck that best of monkeys with a paṭṭiśa of sharp edge, and the rākṣasa Bhāsakarṇa struck him with his trident.

**5.44.34**  With his limbs wounded by those two and his body hair smeared with blood, the monkey became furious and blazed with the radiance of the newly risen sun.

**5.44.35**  Wrenching off a mountain peak together with its deer, serpents, and trees, the heroic Hanūmān, that elephant among monkeys, slew the two rākṣasas.

**5.44.36**  Then, when those five commanders had fallen, the monkey annihilated the remaining army.

**5.44.37**  The monkey destroyed horses with horses, elephants with elephants, warriors with warriors, chariots with chariots, as the thousand-eyed Indra destroys asuras.

**5.44.38**  With the elephants and horses slain, the great chariots with broken axles, and the rākṣasas killed, the earth was blocked in all directions.

**5.44.39**  Then that monkey, having slain in battle those leaders of the bannered army together with their forces and their vehicles, returned and held that very gateway, a hero who had seized his moment, like Death at the time of the destruction of living beings.

**5.45.1**  The king, beholding those five commanders slain by Hanūmān together with their retinues and vehicles, looked upon the prince Akṣa — who was eager and ready to rise up into battle — as one unscathed.

**5.45.2**  Urged on by his father's glance, that prince of great glory, with his bow adorned with gold, sprang forth — as fire is kindled in an assembly by the oblations of foremost twice-born.

**5.45.3**  Then that bull among rākṣasas, full of valor, mounted a great chariot blazing like the young sun, covered with a network of refined gold, and advanced toward that great monkey.

**5.45.4**  That chariot earned through accumulated stores of ascetic power, adorned with a network of refined gold, bearing a banner, with its standard jewel-adorned, well-yoked with eight excellent horses swift as thought —

**5.45.5**  — unconquerable by gods and asuras, moving without obstruction, radiant as the sun, moving through the sky, well-equipped with quivers, furnished with eight attached swords, fitted in order with spears and javelins —

**5.45.6**  — shining, filled with every equipment, adorned with golden strings, resplendent as the moon and sun, bright as the day-maker — mounting this chariot, he who was equal to the gods in valor went forth.

**5.45.7**  Filling both the sky and the earth with its mountains with the sounds of horses, elephants, and great chariots, he approached with his assembled forces the capable monkey seated at the gateway.

**5.45.8**  Approaching that tawny monkey, the tawny-eyed Akṣa, struck with wonder and agitation at the sight of him stationed there like the fire of end-of-age at the time of the annihilation of creatures, regarded him with eyes of deep respect.

**5.45.9**  That son of a king, mighty himself, pondering the speed and valor of that great-souled monkey among his enemies, and his power in the sky, blazed forth like the sun at the end of winter.

**5.45.10**  His wrath aroused, beholding that valor which is impossible to restrain in battle, he stood firm and collected in spirit, and provoked Hanūmān in combat with three sharp arrows.

**5.45.11**  Then Akṣa, his spirit deeply stirred, arrow in hand and bow raised, gazed upon that monkey who was proud, unwearied by exertion, and who had conquered both enemies and exhaustion.

**5.45.12**  He, adorned with gold necklace, armlets, and beautiful earrings, swiftly advanced in valor toward the monkey — and the encounter between those two was incomparable and caused awe even among gods and asuras.

**5.45.13**  The earth rumbled, the sun did not blaze, the wind did not blow, the mountain quaked — beholding the combat of the monkey and the prince, the sky roared and the ocean heaved.

**5.45.14**  Then that hero, a knower of the true principles of concentration, aim, and release, shot three feathered arrows with beautiful faces, gold-plumed, like venomous serpents, and brought them down upon the monkey's head.

**5.45.15**  Struck on the head simultaneously by those arrows, his eyes wide open and smeared with flowing blood, shining like the newly risen sun, resplendent with arrow-rays, he blazed like the sun with its garland of rays.

**5.45.16**  Then that foremost among the ministers of the lord of tawny-eyed monkeys, beholding in battle that son of the best of kings, bearing his excellent variegated weapons and adorned bow, was filled with joy and swelled with eagerness for combat.

**5.45.17**  Like the sun stationed atop Mount Mandara, his wrath fully kindled and united with strength and valor, he then scorched the prince Akṣa together with his forces and his vehicle with the burning rays of the fire of his eyes.

**5.45.18**  Then he, whose bow was like Indra's bow, who was a cloud of rākṣasas raining arrows in battle, swiftly released his shafts upon that mountain-like lord of monkeys, as a cloud-bank pours rain upon the highest mountain.

**5.45.19**  Then the monkey, equal to a cloud in valor, beholding in battle the prince Akṣa with his fierce valor in combat and his arrows charged with increasing radiance, strength, and might, roared with joy.

**5.45.20**  He, proud in his valor despite his youth, his wrath grown great, with eyes like blood, the peerless one approached the monkey in battle like an elephant approaching a great well covered over with grass.

**5.45.21**  Struck forcibly by those arrows, he whose sound was like the sound of a thundercloud uttered a roar; the son of the wind swiftly leaped into the sky, terrible to behold as he swung his arms and thighs.

**5.45.22**  That mighty foremost of rākṣasas, full of glory, the chariot-warrior on the best of chariots, charged at him as he leaped upward, raining arrows upon him as a cloud pelts a mountain with a shower of stones.

**5.45.23**  That heroic monkey, eluding those arrows as they were released, moved along the path frequented by the wind, darting between the arrows like the wind itself, swift as thought, of fierce valor in battle.

**5.45.24**  The son of the wind god regarded Akṣa — who had seized his bow and was eager for battle, carpeting the sky with various excellent arrows — with eyes of deep regard, and fell into contemplation.

**5.45.25**  Then the long-armed monkey, his chest pierced by the arrows of that great-souled prince, roaring, being a knower of the true particulars of action, pondered his course of valor in battle.

**5.45.26**  "This mighty one, radiant as the young sun, performs great deeds as if not young at all — yet my mind does not incline toward slaying him who is illustrious in every feat of battle."

**5.45.27**  "This one is great-souled and vast in valor, composed and most enduring in battle — without doubt, owing to the excellence of his deeds, he is honored by the nāgas, yakṣas, and sages."

**5.45.28**  "With a spirit swelling with valor and enthusiasm he faces me and stands watching — for his valor would shake the minds even of gods and asuras who act swiftly."

**5.45.29**  "Certainly this one would not fail to overcome if he were left alone — for his valor grows in battle; therefore slaying him pleases me, for a growing fire cannot safely be neglected."

**5.45.30**  Reflecting thus upon the other's surging force and arranging his own course of action, the valiant and mighty great monkey then gathered his speed and resolved upon slaying him.

**5.45.31**  The hero, son of the wind god, slew those eight horses of his — of great speed, steady, capable of bearing great burdens in wheeling — with slaps of his palm along the path traversed by the wind.

**5.45.32**  Then that great chariot, defeated by the minister of the lord of tawny-eyed monkeys and struck by his palm, with its housing shattered and its yoke-pole broken, fell to the ground from the sky with its horses slain.

**5.45.33**  He, that great chariot-warrior, abandoning his chariot, with bow and sword, leaped into the sky — that one of fierce valor who by devoted austerity was like a sage — as if leaving his body to enter the abode of the wind-gods.

**5.45.34**  Then the monkey, equal to the wind in speed and valor, approaching him as he moved in the sky — that sky traversed by Garuḍa, the wind, and the siddhas — seized him firmly by the feet, step by step.

**5.45.35**  That best of monkeys, whirling him a thousand times as Garuḍa seizes a great serpent, released him with force onto the earth in battle — equal in valor to his father, Vāyu.

**5.45.36**  That rākṣasa, with arms, thighs, waist, and head broken, streaming blood, his bones and eyes crushed, his joints severed and his sinews scattered, lay slain on the earth by the son of the wind.

**5.45.37**  The great monkey, having crushed him to the ground, wrought great terror upon the lord of rākṣasas.

**5.45.38**  With the great sages of great vows who move in circles, and the beings together with the yakṣas and serpents, and the gods with Indra, all greatly stricken with wonder — with the prince slain, the monkey was gazed upon by them all.

**5.45.39**  Having slain that prince Akṣa, whose radiance was like the son of the Thunderbolt-wielder, his own eyes like blood, that hero returned to that very gateway having seized his moment, like Death at the time of the destruction of living beings.

**5.46.1**  Then the great-souled lord of rākṣasas, having steadied his mind on learning that young Akṣa had been slain by Hanūmān, in wrath dispatched Indrajit, who resembled Indra himself.

**5.46.2**  "You are the foremost among those who bear weapons, the master of missile-lore, who has brought grief to gods and asuras alike; your valor is proven against the gods along with Indra, and your arsenal of weapons has been accumulated through worship of the Grandsire."

**5.46.3**  "When confronted with the force of your missiles, neither asuras nor the Marut hosts — indeed no one in the three worlds — has ever come away unwearied in battle."

**5.46.4**  "Protected by the might of your arms and shielded by your austerities, knowing the distinction of time and place — you alone are the best in wisdom."

**5.46.5**  "There is no feat impossible for you in battle, no counsel beyond your deliberate wisdom; there is no one in the three conflicts who does not know your missile-strength and your prowess."

**5.46.6**  "Your austerity, strength, valor, and missile-might in battle are a fitting match for mine; the mind of one who faces you in the press of war finds no room for fatigue — this is certain."

**5.46.7**  "Slain are all the commanders, the rākṣasa Jambumālī, and the five heroic sons of ministers who led the vanguard of the army."

**5.46.8**  "Your beloved younger brother, the prince Akṣa, has also been killed; yet I place no great stock in those losses, O slayer of enemies — for you remain."

**5.46.9**  "Having now seen this great might of the monkey, his power and valor, and having also measured your own essential strength, O wise one, marshal your speed in keeping with your own force."

**5.46.10**  "As the trampling of one's force subsides when you draw near, just as it ceases when the foe is quelled — weighing your own power against the enemy's, engage now, O foremost among masters of missiles."

**5.46.11**  "This is certainly not the highest counsel — that I send you forth; yet this is the considered course recognized for kingly duty and for the Kṣatriya code."

**5.46.12**  "The proficiency in battle with manifold weapons must certainly be employed, O subduer of enemies, and victory in war is to be desired."

**5.46.13**  Then, having heard his father's words, that hero of unwaning spirit — possessing the prowess of Dakṣa's son — circumambulated his lord, resolved in mind for battle.

**5.46.14**  Then Indrajit, duly honored by his own dear companions, his martial enthusiasm roused for combat, set out for the battlefield.

**5.46.15**  The glorious son of the lord of rākṣasas, lotus-petal-eyed, of great splendor, sallied forth like the ocean at the full moon.

**5.46.16**  Indrajit, resembling Indra, mounted a chariot harnessed with four serpents whose speed equaled that of the king of birds, whose fangs were white and sharp, and whose swiftness was unimpeded.

**5.46.17**  That chariot-warrior, foremost of archers, expert in weapons, and best among masters of missiles, swiftly rode in his chariot to where Hanūmān stood.

**5.46.18**  That hero of the monkeys, hearing the rumble of his chariot and the twang of his bow, became all the more elated.

**5.46.19**  Taking up his great bow and sharp-pointed arrows, the expert in battle advanced toward Hanūmān with clear intent.

**5.46.20**  As he, arrow in hand, set forth for battle with heightened exultation, all the directions turned turbid and fearsome beasts cried out in many voices.

**5.46.21**  Nāgas and Yakṣas, great sages, and Siddhas moving in circles gathered there; flocks of birds filled the sky and cried out aloud, supremely excited.

**5.46.22**  Seeing Indrajit approaching swiftly with his chariot, the swift monkey let out a mighty roar and grew in stature.

**5.46.23**  Indrajit, mounted on his divine chariot with his variegated bow, twanged his bow with a sound as mighty as a thunderbolt.

**5.46.24**  Then those two, of exceedingly sharp speed and great strength, fearless in battle — the monkey and the son of the rākṣasa lord — came together, like the kings of gods and asuras locked in enmity.

**5.46.25**  The immeasurable monkey, grown mighty, deflected the swift rush of arrows of that heroic great-chariot warrior renowned on the battlefield, and ranged about in the path of combat — himself beyond measure, surpassing his father's expectations.

**5.46.26**  Then that hero, slayer of enemy champions, released arrows with long, sharp heads, well-feathered, with gilded colorful nocks, shot in rapid succession with the speed of a falling thunderbolt.

**5.46.27**  Hearing the rumble of his chariot, the sound of kettledrums, war-drums, and battle-drums, and the twang of the straining bow, he leapt into the air again.

**5.46.28**  The great monkey swiftly dodged between the arrows, eluding that adversary's targeting, foiling his aim's concentration.

**5.46.29**  Spreading out his hands, Hanūmān, son of the Wind, again darted forward in front of those arrows and leapt aloft.

**5.46.30**  Those two, both endowed with speed and skilled in the arts of war, waged an incomparable battle that captivated the minds of all living beings.

**5.46.31**  The rākṣasa could find no opening in Hanūmān, nor could Māruti find an opening in that great-souled one; coming together, those two of valor equal to the gods found each other mutually irresistible.

**5.46.32**  Then, as his mark was missed and his arrows fell in vain, that great-souled one fell into deep deliberation, his mind composed in contemplative concentration.

**5.46.33**  Then the son of the rākṣasa king formed a resolve regarding that foremost monkey hero — recognizing the monkey's invincibility, how might he be restrained — for the purpose of his capture.

**5.46.34**  Then that hero of immense splendor, best among masters of missiles, trained the Paitāmaha weapon — the missile of the Grandsire — upon that foremost of monkeys.

**5.46.35**  Knowing this one to be inviolable, the long-armed Indrajit, conversant with the true nature of missiles, bound the son of the Wind-god with that weapon.

**5.46.36**  Bound by that weapon of the rākṣasa, the monkey became motionless and fell to the ground.

**5.46.37**  Then that foremost monkey hero, having recognized the missile's binding through the power of its master, his lesser speed having departed, reflected on the Grandsire's boon and on his own situation.

**5.46.38**  Then Hanūmān contemplated the Brahmā-weapon enchanted with the spells of Svāyaṃbhuva, and the boon he had received from the Grandsire.

**5.46.39**  "I have no power to release myself from this missile-bondage, for it comes from the power of the world's preceptor; thus this missile-bondage, being of such a nature, must be honored by me, the son born of the self-existent."

**5.46.40**  The monkey, having considered the power of the missile and his own capacity for release, and having reflected on the Grandsire's grace, abided by the Grandsire's command.

**5.46.41**  "Even bound by the missile, no fear arises in me, for I am protected by the Grandsire, by Mahendra, and by the Wind-god."

**5.46.42**  "Moreover, in being captured by the rākṣasas I see a great advantage — a conversation with the king of rākṣasas; let the enemies therefore take me."

**5.46.43**  His purpose settled — that slayer of enemy heroes — having considered it like a wise elephant, ceased his efforts; restrained by enemies who pressed upon him by force, he roared while being reviled by them with various insults.

**5.46.44**  Then the rākṣasas, seeing that subduer of enemies motionless, bound him with cords of hemp and with strips of bark from trees twisted together.

**5.46.45**  He approved of this binding and forcible capture by the enemy warriors, his purpose resolved, out of curiosity — thinking, "If only the lord of rākṣasas might decide to see me."

**5.46.46**  Bound by that bark cord, he was in fact released by the missile — for the powerful missile-bond does not accompany any other bond.

**5.46.47**  Then Indrajit the hero, reflecting on that bark-cord binding of that foremost monkey who had been freed from the missile-bond, fell into worry — for one bound by another bond does not remain under the missile's hold.

**5.46.48**  "Alas, a great deed has been done to no purpose! The rākṣasas did not reflect on the lore of missiles; moreover, when one missile is deployed, no other missile can operate — we are all in doubt."

**5.46.49**  Hanūmān, freed by the missile, did not realize his own liberation, being dragged by the rākṣasas and oppressed by those bonds.

**5.46.50**  Then the monkey, beaten by the cruel rākṣasas with fists and wooden clubs, was dragged to the presence of the lord of rākṣasas.

**5.46.51**  Then Indrajit, closely observing him freed from the missile but bound with bark-cord ropes, presented that immensely powerful monkey hero to the king before his assembled retinue.

**5.46.52**  The rākṣasas presented that bound foremost-of-monkeys, like a maddened elephant in bonds, to Rāvaṇa, lord of rākṣasas.

**5.46.53**  "Who is this? Whose is he? Where does he come from? What is his purpose? What is his recourse?" — such discussions arose among the rākṣasa warriors there.

**5.46.54**  "Let him be slain!" "Let him be burned!" "Let him be eaten!" — so the enraged rākṣasas there said to one another.

**5.46.55**  Swiftly traversing the path, that great-souled one arrived before the feet of the lord of rākṣasas and beheld there the senior attendants of the king and the palace adorned with great jewels.

**5.46.56**  The immensely resplendent Rāvaṇa saw that foremost of monkeys being dragged hither and thither by rākṣasas of hideous forms.

**5.46.57**  And that foremost of monkeys also beheld the lord of rākṣasas, endowed with blazing power and strength, blazing like the sun.

**5.46.58**  Ten-faced Rāvaṇa, his gaze reddened with swelling wrath, scrutinized the monkey and then directed the chief counselors seated there — elders distinguished by lineage, conduct, and age — regarding him.

**5.46.59**  Questioned by them in due order as to the purpose of his business and the root cause of the matter, the monkey announced at the outset: "I have come from the presence of Sugrīva, lord of the monkeys, as his messenger."

**5.47.1**  Then Hanūmān of terrible valor, astonished by his deeds, his eyes reddened with wrath, gazed upon the lord of rākṣasas.

**5.47.2**  He beheld him resplendent with a magnificent golden diadem wrapped in a net of pearls, of surpassing brilliance —

**5.47.3**  — adorned with golden ornaments of manifold kinds inset with highly precious gems set with diamonds, fashioned as if by the mind itself;

**5.47.4**  — clad in the most costly silk, smeared with red sandal paste, self-anointed, with varied and diverse decorative patterns;

**5.47.5**  — with broad, striking rākṣasa eyes terrible to behold, with blazing, sharp, and great fangs, and with hanging lips over the teeth;

**5.47.6**  — that hero gleaming with great splendor, with his ten heads, like Mount Mandara covered with various serpents at its peaks;

**5.47.7**  — dark as a heap of collyrium, his necklace gleaming on his chest, his face like the full moon, like a cloud attended by cranes;

**5.47.8**  — his arms bound with armlets and smeared with the finest sandal paste, adorned with gleaming bracelets, massive, like five-headed serpents;

**5.47.9**  — seated on a great, jeweled, variegated crystal throne adorned with the finest gems, spread with the best of cushions;

**5.47.10**  — attended on all sides by exceedingly adorned ladies who held fly-whisks of yak-tail hair and served him from nearby;

**5.47.11**  — accompanied by Durdhara, Prahasta, and the rākṣasa Mahāpārśva, by counselors versed in the true principles of counsel, and by the minister Nikumbha;

**5.47.12**  — flanked by four rākṣasas intoxicated with their strength, surrounding the entire world like the four oceans;

**5.47.13**  — attended by counselors expert in the principles of statecraft and by others of auspicious intelligence, like the lord of gods attended by the gods;

**5.47.14**  Hanūmān beheld the lord of rākṣasas of immeasurable splendor, stationed there like a rain-laden cloud resting on the peak of Meru.

**5.47.15**  Though being pressed upon by those rākṣasas of terrible valor, he was filled with supreme amazement and gazed upon the lord of rākṣasas.

**5.47.16**  Then Hanūmān, seeing the lord of rākṣasas blazing in splendor, reflected in his mind, overwhelmed by his brilliance.

**5.47.17**  "Ah, what form! Ah, what steadiness! Ah, what vigor! Ah, what radiance! Ah, how the king of rākṣasas is endowed with every auspicious mark!"

**5.47.18**  "Were adharma not so powerful in him, this lord of rākṣasas would be the protector of the world of gods together with Indra himself."

**5.47.19**  "It is indeed from this one that the worlds tremble — gods, asuras, and all — for this one, when enraged, has the capacity to reduce the world to a single ocean."

**5.47.20**  Thus the wise monkey turned over many such thoughts in his mind, having seen the power of the king of rākṣasas of immeasurable might.

**5.48.1**  Gazing up at him — that tawny-eyed one standing before him — Rāvaṇa the terror of the world, the long-armed one, was seized with great wrath.

**5.48.2**  That king, his eyes reddened with anger, spoke this extensive and meaningful speech to Prahasta, the best of ministers, in words suited to the moment.

**5.48.3**  "Let this wicked-souled one be questioned: where does he come from? What is his motive? What was his purpose in destroying the grove and in threatening the rākṣasī women?"

**5.48.4**  Hearing Rāvaṇa's words, Prahasta spoke: "Take courage, all good to you; you need have no fear, O monkey."

**5.48.5**  "If you have been sent by Indra to the abode of Rāvaṇa, tell the truth; let there be no fear for you, O monkey — you shall be released."

**5.48.6**  "Or if you belong to Vaiśravaṇa, or to Yama, or to Varuṇa — having assumed this lovely form on behalf of Yama or Varuṇa —"

**5.48.7**  "— or if you have been sent as a messenger by Viṣṇu, who desires victory — for your brilliance is not that of a mere monkey; the form alone is a monkey's —"

**5.48.8**  "— tell the truth now, and then, O monkey, you shall be released; but if you speak falsely, your life will be hard to preserve."

**5.48.9**  "Or else — whatever the cause of your entry into the abode of Rāvaṇa —"

**5.48.10**  Thus addressed, that best of monkeys then spoke to the lord of the rākṣasa host: "I am not of Śakra, nor of Yama, nor of Varuṇa —"

**5.48.11**  "— I have no alliance with Dhanada, nor was I dispatched by Viṣṇu; this is simply my birth-nature — I am a monkey, and I have come here thus."

**5.48.12**  "Since an audience with the lord of rākṣasas is difficult to obtain, I destroyed this grove of the rākṣasa king for the purpose of obtaining that audience."

**5.48.13**  "Then those powerful rākṣasas came, eager for battle; and for the protection of my body I fought back against them in combat."

**5.48.14**  "I cannot be bound by missile-fetters even by gods or asuras — this very boon has come to me as well from the Grandsire."

**5.48.15**  "Desiring to see the king, I allowed the missile to have its effect; I was indeed freed from the missile, yet I was greatly tormented by the rākṣasas."

**5.48.16**  "I am to be understood as a messenger of Rāghava of immeasurable might; let this salutary message of mine be heard, O lord."

**5.49.1**  Beholding that being of immense power, the foremost of monkeys, himself of great courage, composed and unperturbed, spoke words full of meaning to the Ten-faced one.

**5.49.2**  "I have come to your abode, O lord of rākṣasas, bearing a message from Sugrīva. The lord of monkeys, your brother, has sent you his greetings.

**5.49.3**  Hear the counsel of your brother, the great-souled Sugrīva — words grounded in righteousness and prudence, beneficial both in this world and the next.

**5.49.4**  There was a king named Daśaratha, possessor of chariots, elephants, and horses, a kinsman to the world like a father, equal in splendour to the lord of the gods.

**5.49.5**  His eldest son, a mighty-armed lord, beloved and pleasing, departed at his father's command and entered the Daṇḍaka forest.

**5.49.6**  That one of great splendour, named Rāma, accompanied by his brother Lakṣmaṇa and his wife Sītā, followed the path of righteousness.

**5.49.7**  His wife Sītā, devoted to her husband, the daughter of the great-souled King Janaka of Videha, was lost in the forest.

**5.49.8**  That prince, searching for the goddess, together with his younger brother, reached Ṛśyamūka and met with Sugrīva.

**5.49.9**  By him was the search for Sītā pledged, and Rāma in turn granted Sugrīva the kingdom of the monkeys.

**5.49.10**  Then, having slain Vālin in battle, that prince established Sugrīva as king, lord of the hosts of monkeys and bears.

**5.49.11**  That lord of monkeys, Sugrīva, true to his pledge and intent on the search for Sītā, dispatched monkeys in all directions.

**5.49.12**  Thousands, hundreds, and tens of thousands of those monkeys search in all directions, below and above in the sky.

**5.49.13**  Some are equal to Garuḍa, son of Vinatā; others are comparable to the wind; those heroic monkeys move without obstruction, swift and of great might.

**5.49.14**  And I am Hanūmān by name, the own-born son of the Wind-god. For Sītā's sake, crossing in an instant the ocean stretching a hundred yojanas, I came here desiring to find her.

**5.49.15**  Therefore you, who have seen the value of righteousness and profit, who have embraced austerities, O greatly wise one — you ought not to obstruct the wife of another.

**5.49.16**  For the wise, such as you, do not engage in acts opposed to righteousness, fraught with many perils, and destructive to the very root.

**5.49.17**  And who is capable of standing before the arrows released by Lakṣmaṇa, following in the wake of Rāma's wrath — even among gods and asuras?

**5.49.18**  Nor in the three worlds, O king, is there anyone who, having wronged Rāghava, might attain happiness.

**5.49.19**  Therefore consider this counsel, beneficial in all three times, righteous, and attended by profit: let Jānakī be restored to the lord among men.

**5.49.20**  For I have seen the goddess — that which was hard to find here has been found. Whatever remaining action follows from this, for that Rāghava is the instrument.

**5.49.21**  I have marked this Sītā, so wholly given over to grief — she whom you hold without recognising her, as one might grasp a five-hooded serpentess.

**5.49.22**  She cannot be overcome even by gods together with asuras, like food mixed with poison consumed to excess is fatal to one's vital energy.

**5.49.23**  This store of righteousness that you have gained through the heat of austerities — it is not proper to destroy it, together with your own life's refuge.

**5.49.24**  The invulnerability that you perceive for yourself through your austerities — from gods together with asuras — even that is greatly undermined by this very act.

**5.49.25**  For Sugrīva is neither a god, nor an asura, nor a human, nor a rākṣasa, nor a gandharva, nor a yakṣa, nor a serpent.

**5.49.26**  Rāghava is a man, O king, and Sugrīva is the lord of monkeys — how then, O king, will you save your life?

**5.49.27**  Righteousness does not follow the approach of unrighteousness attended by its fruits; rather, righteousness destroys unrighteousness, and each act bears its own consequence.

**5.49.28**  You have already reaped the fruit of your righteous deeds — of that there is no doubt; but you shall swiftly obtain the fruit of this unrighteous act as well.

**5.49.29**  Having understood the slaughter at Janasthāna and the slaying of Vālin, and understanding the alliance of Rāma and Sugrīva — recognise what is beneficial for yourself.

**5.49.30**  Indeed I alone am capable of destroying Laṅkā with its horses, chariots, and elephants — but that is his resolve.

**5.49.31**  For Rāma has pledged, in the presence of the hosts of monkeys and bears, the annihilation of enemies — those by whom Sītā has been violated.

**5.49.32**  For even Purandara himself, should he wrong Rāma, would not attain happiness — what then of a person like you?

**5.49.33**  She whom you know as Sītā, who stands in your power — know her as the night of death, the destroyer of all Laṅkā.

**5.49.34**  Enough — Sītā is the very noose of death embodied, clinging of her own accord to your neck; consider well what is fitting for yourself.

**5.49.35**  Behold this city with its towers and royal highways, scorched by Sītā's blazing power, oppressed by Rāma's wrath, burning even now.

**5.49.36**  Having heard the unpleasant yet eloquent words of the monkey who spoke without timidity — words elegant and well-composed — the Ten-faced one, his eyes wide with rage, commanded the slaying of that great monkey.

**5.50.1**  Having heard those words of the great-souled monkey, Rāvaṇa, overcome with fury, commanded his execution.

**5.50.2**  When the wicked-souled Rāvaṇa had commanded his killing, Vibhīṣaṇa — who had duly announced his embassy — did not approve.

**5.50.3**  Knowing the lord of rākṣasas to be enraged and the matter at hand to be critical, he reflected on the right course of action, firm in the conduct of duty.

**5.50.4**  Having resolved his purpose, and reverently addressing with gentle words his elder brother the conqueror of enemies, that master of speech spoke words of great benefit.

**5.50.5**  "O king, the killing of this monkey is contrary to righteousness, censured by the conduct of the world, and unworthy of you, O hero.

**5.50.6**  Without doubt this enemy is grown mighty and has done an immeasurable wrong; yet the righteous do not prescribe death for an envoy — many punishments have been seen for an envoy, but not death.

**5.50.7**  Disfigurement of the limbs, flogging with a whip, shaving of the head, and the application of Lakṣmaṇa's brand — these are the punishments prescribed for an envoy; but the slaying of an envoy we have not even heard of.

**5.50.8**  And how could one such as you, whose mind is schooled in righteousness and prudence, who is firm in discerning what is high and low, remain in the power of anger? For those of firm character restrain their anger.

**5.50.9**  In discourse on righteousness, in the ways of the world, and in the mastery of the intellect through the śāstras, none equals you, O hero — for you are the supreme one among all gods and asuras.

**5.50.10**  Nor do I see any benefit in killing this monkey; let this punishment fall upon those by whom this monkey was sent.

**5.50.11**  Whether righteous or unrighteous, this one has been dispatched by others; speaking on another's behalf, acting under another's authority, an envoy does not deserve death.

**5.50.12**  Moreover, should this one be killed, O king, I see no other sky-traveller who could come again across the great ocean.

**5.50.13**  Therefore no effort should be made to kill him, O conqueror of enemy cities; you ought to direct your effort against the gods with Indra at their head.

**5.50.14**  If this one perishes, I see no other envoy who could rouse those two sons of a human king to battle — those two who love battle, who are proud, and who are kept away by the long path.

**5.50.15**  It is not fitting, O delight of the mind, for you who are difficult to conquer even by the valiant, spirited, and courageous gods and asuras, to destroy the war-enterprise of the rākṣasas.

**5.50.16**  Well-disposed, heroic, composed, born into illustrious lineages, high-minded, foremost among weapon-bearers, sharp at the blade's edge, and well-maintained — such are your warriors.

**5.50.17**  Let some portion of your forces set out at your command and subdue those two foolish princes, so as to demonstrate your power over your enemies."

**5.51.1**  Having heard those words, the mighty Ten-necked one spoke in reply to his brother's excellent counsel, fitting to time and place.

**5.51.2**  "What you have said is correct: the slaying of an envoy is censured. Yet some punishment other than death must certainly be inflicted upon him.

**5.51.3**  It is said that the tail is dear to monkeys as their ornament — let it be set ablaze at once, and let him depart with it burned.

**5.51.4**  Then let his allies, kinsmen, relatives, and all his friends behold him wretched, afflicted with the disfigurement of his limbs."

**5.51.5**  The lord of rākṣasas commanded: "Let him be led by the rākṣasas through the entire city together with its squares, with his tail set ablaze."

**5.51.6**  Having heard that command, the rākṣasas, hardened with rage, began wrapping his tail in old cotton cloths.

**5.51.7**  As his tail was being wrapped, the great monkey grew larger, like fire in the forests when it reaches dry fuel.

**5.51.8**  Then, having drenched it with oil, they set fire to it.

**5.51.9**  With his tail ablaze, his spirit filled with fury and indignation, his face like the rising sun, he flung those rākṣasas down.

**5.51.10**  Bound again by the cruel rākṣasas who gathered around him, that foremost of monkeys, that hero, formed a resolve suited to the moment.

**5.51.11**  "Indeed, these rākṣasas are not capable of binding me; even now, bound as I am, I could cut the bonds, leap up, and slay them.

**5.51.12**  I am fully capable of defeating all rākṣasas in battle; yet for Rāma's pleasure I shall endure this circumstance."

**5.51.13**  "It would be well if I were to survey Laṅkā once more — for at night I did not see it clearly enough in terms of its fortifications and their arrangement; I must certainly examine Laṅkā before night is spent."

**5.51.14**  "Let the rākṣasas torment me further with bonds and the burning of my tail — my mind feels no weariness."

**5.51.15**  Then those rākṣasas, joyful, seized that mighty monkey who concealed his feelings — that elephant among monkeys, of great courage — and led him away.

**5.51.16**  Those rākṣasas of cruel deeds, proclaiming their own acts with the sounds of conch-shells and kettledrums, led him through the city.

**5.51.17**  Hanūmān was led through the great city of the rākṣasas, and the great monkey observed its wonderful aerial palaces.

**5.51.18**  The monkey observed the enclosed ground areas, the well-laid-out squares, the streets dense with houses, and the crossroads.

**5.51.19**  In the public squares, at the crossroads, and along the royal highway, all the rākṣasas proclaimed the monkey a spy.

**5.51.20**  Then, as the tip of Hanūmān's tail was blazing, those rākṣasa women of distorted eyes reported that unpleasant news to the goddess.

**5.51.21**  "That copper-faced monkey with whom you conversed, O Sītā — this very one is being led about with his tail set on fire."

**5.51.22**  Hearing those cruel words, like a seizure of her own self, Vaidehī, burning with grief, approached the fire-god.

**5.51.23**  With wide eyes, pure and auspiciously disposed, she turned toward the great monkey's well-being and propitiated the bearer of oblations.

**5.51.24**  "If there is service rendered to my husband, if there is austerity performed, if there is fidelity to one husband — be cool for Hanūmān."

**5.51.25**  "If that wise one has any compassion for me, if there remains any residue of my fortune — be cool for Hanūmān."

**5.51.26**  "If that righteous-souled one knows me to be of virtuous conduct and longing for union with him — be cool for Hanūmān."

**5.51.27**  "If the noble, long-armed Sugrīva, true to his pledge, will rescue me from this suffering — be cool for Hanūmān."

**5.51.28**  Then the fire, with its sharp flames, undisturbed, with its crest turning rightward, blazed — as though proclaiming auspiciousness for the monkey to the doe-eyed one.

**5.51.29**  And as his tail burned, the monkey reflected: "This fire is fully kindled — why does it not burn me everywhere?"

**5.51.30**  "It is seen to have great flames, yet it causes me no pain; it is stationed at the tip of my tail like a touch of cool snow."

**5.51.31**  "Or rather, this is clearly the same wonder that I witnessed as I flew — through Rāma's power — when Mainākā appeared in the ocean, lord of rivers."

**5.51.32**  "If the ocean and the wise Mainākā showed such solicitude for Rāma's purpose, why would fire not do the same?"

**5.51.33**  "By reason of Sītā's compassion, by the power of Rāghava, and by my father's friendship with him — Pāvaka does not burn me."

**5.51.34**  Again that elephant among monkeys reflected for a moment, then leaped up with great speed, and the great monkey roared.

**5.51.35**  Then the glorious son of the Wind-god reached the city gate, lofty as a mountain peak, thronged with rākṣasa guards stationed at their posts.

**5.51.36**  Becoming mountain-like in an instant, then again self-composed and reduced to the smallest size, he shook off his bonds.

**5.51.37**  And freed, the glorious one again became like a mountain; looking about, he spied an iron bar resting against the gatehouse arch.

**5.51.38**  Seizing it, long-armed Māruti — that bar fitted with black iron — slaughtered all those guards once more.

**5.51.39**  Having slain them, he of fierce prowess in battle, surveying Laṅkā once more, shone resplendent — his tail ablaze making a garland of flames — like the sun with its garland of rays.

**5.52.1**  Then the monkey, his desire fulfilled, his enthusiasm growing, gazed upon Laṅkā and reflected on what remained yet to be done.

**5.52.2**  "What indeed remains for me to do here at present, by which further pain may be inflicted upon these rākṣasas?"

**5.52.3**  "The grove has been ravaged, distinguished rākṣasas have been slain, a portion of the army has been destroyed — what remains is the destruction of the fortress."

**5.52.4**  "Once the fortress is destroyed, the task will become one of pleasant effort; with little exertion in this undertaking my labour will bear fruit."

**5.52.5**  "This fire, the carrier of oblations, blazing on my tail — it is fitting to give it satisfaction with these finest dwellings."

**5.52.6**  Then, with his tail ablaze, the great monkey moved about the rooftops of Laṅkā's mansions like a thundercloud lit by lightning.

**5.52.7**  Hanūmān released fire whose flames were like those of the fire of universal dissolution.

**5.52.8**  Fanned by the wind, that fire blazed with tremendous speed and great force, spreading and growing like the fire of cosmic time.

**5.52.9**  The wind drove the blazing fire through those dwellings.

**5.52.10**  Those great mansions adorned with lattices of gold and set with pearls and gems collapsed and fell.

**5.52.11**  Those shattered palaces fell to the ground like the celestial abodes of Siddhas falling from the sky at the exhaustion of their merit.

**5.52.12**  He beheld brilliant mineral streams flowing from the mansions — diamond, coral, vaidūrya gem, pearl, and silver, all mingled together.

**5.52.13**  Just as fire is never sated with wood and grass, so too Hanūmān was not in the least sated with the slaying of those lords of the rākṣasas.

**5.52.14**  That Laṅkā, enveloped in the flames of fire, her champions slain, her warriors routed, overcome by the furious power of Hanūmān, appeared like a city struck down by a curse.

**5.52.15**  The great-spirited Hanūmān beheld Laṅkā, her rākṣasas terrified and distraught, blazing with the flames of fire, like the earth struck by the wrath of the Self-born Brahmā.

**5.52.16**  Having slain those many rākṣasas, having broken that grove with its many trees, having set fire to the dwellings of the rākṣasas, that great-souled one turned his mind to Rāma.

**5.52.17**  Having set all of Laṅkā ablaze, that foremost of monkeys, the best of the vānaras, then extinguished the fire of his tail in the ocean.

**5.53.1**  Gazing upon the burning, devastated city of Laṅkā, her bands of rākṣasas terrified, the monkey Hanūmān fell into thought.

**5.53.2**  A great fear arose in him, and self-reproach was born within: "What act have I committed by burning Laṅkā?"

**5.53.3**  "Blessed are those foremost men, those great souls who restrain anger that has arisen in the mind with intelligence, as water restrains a blazing fire."

**5.53.4**  "If this Laṅkā has been burned, then surely noble Jānakī too has been burned. By me, not knowing it, the task of my lord has been destroyed."

**5.53.5**  "The very purpose for which this undertaking was begun has been ruined — for in burning Laṅkā, I did not protect Sītā."

**5.53.6**  "This was a small matter that needed to be done — of that there is no doubt. But overcome by anger, I have brought about its utter ruin."

**5.53.7**  "Jānakī is clearly destroyed — not a single spot of Laṅkā is seen unburned; the entire city has been reduced to ashes."

**5.53.8**  "If indeed this task has been ruined by my perversion of judgment, then laying down my life right here seems most fitting to me."

**5.53.9**  "Shall I fall into the fire today, or into the submarine fire? Or shall I give my body to the creatures that dwell in the ocean?"

**5.53.10**  "How can I, who have destroyed the entirety of the task, ever look upon the lord of monkeys, or those two tiger-like men, while still alive?"

**5.53.11**  "By me, due to the fault of rage, has been displayed what is already renowned in the three worlds — the fickleness and instability of monkeys."

**5.53.12**  "Shame upon this rājasic nature, uncontrolled and unstable, by which even I, capable of mastery, failed through passion to protect Sītā."

**5.53.13**  "With Sītā destroyed, those two will perish as well; with their destruction Sugrīva together with his kinsmen will perish."

**5.53.14**  "Hearing this very news, how will the righteous-souled Bharata, ever devoted to his brothers, along with Śatrughna, be able to survive?"

**5.53.15**  "With the most righteous Ikṣvāku lineage gone to destruction without doubt, all the subjects will be tormented by grief and anguish."

**5.53.16**  "Thus I, devoid of fortune, stripped of the accumulation of dharma and artha, my soul overcome by the fault of rage, am clearly the destroyer of the world."

**5.53.17**  As he was reflecting thus, favorable omens presented themselves — those he had perceived earlier — and he reconsidered.

**5.53.18**  "Or rather — she of beautiful limbs, that auspicious one, is protected by her own radiance and will not perish; fire does not function within fire."

**5.53.19**  "Indeed fire cannot rightfully touch, by the law of dharma, the wife of that one of boundless splendor — her who is protected by her own chastity of conduct."

**5.53.20**  "Surely by the power of Rāma and by the merit of the princess of Videha, this fire — whose very work is burning — did not burn me."

**5.53.21**  "She who is the deity of the three brothers beginning with Bharata, and who is beloved of Rāma's heart — how could she perish?"

**5.53.22**  "Or since this imperishable fire, the lord everywhere whose very work is burning, did not burn my tail — how could it scorch that noble lady?"

**5.53.23**  "By her ascetic practice, by her truthful speech, and by her singular devotion to her husband, she could herself extinguish fire — fire will not burn her."

**5.53.24**  As he thus reflected upon the queen's embrace of dharma, the great-souled Hanūmān heard the words of the Cāraṇas.

**5.53.25**  "Ah, truly a deed nearly beyond endurance has been performed by Hanūmān, who again and again unleashed fearsome fire upon the dwellings of the rākṣasas!"

**5.53.26**  "This city of Laṅkā with its watch-towers, ramparts, and gateways has been burned — yet Jānakī has not been burned. This is a wonder and marvel to us!"

**5.53.27**  By these omens of manifest import, by reasons of great virtue, and by the words of the seers, Hanūmān's heart became glad.

**5.53.28**  Then the monkey, his desired object attained, knowing the princess to be unharmed, and having seen her again in person, resolved to set out on his return journey.

**5.54.1**  Then, going to Jānakī who was seated at the foot of the śiṃśapā tree, he saluted her and said: "By good fortune I see you here unharmed."

**5.54.2**  Then Sītā, gazing again and again upon Hanūmān as he prepared to depart, spoke to him words filled with affection for her husband.

**5.54.3**  "Truly, you alone are sufficient to accomplish this task. O slayer of enemy heroes, your rise of power is glorious."

**5.54.4**  "Yet if Kākutstha, the crusher of enemy armies, were to fill Laṅkā with his forces and then lead me away, that would be befitting of him."

**5.54.5**  "Bring about therefore what is truly befitting of that great-souled hero in battle — cause his valor to manifest as it rightly should."

**5.54.6**  Having heard those words of hers — purposeful, modest, and supported by reasons — Hanūmān spoke this reply to her.

**5.54.7**  "Kākutstha will come swiftly, surrounded by the foremost of monkeys and bears, and having conquered your enemies in battle, he will remove your sorrow."

**5.54.8**  Having thus reassured the princess of Videha, Hanūmān, the son of the wind-god, made up his mind to depart and took leave of Vaidehī.

**5.54.9**  Then that tiger among monkeys, that crusher of enemies, eager to see his lord, ascended the finest of mountains, Ariṣṭa.

**5.54.10**  It was adorned with lofty rows of blue padmaka trees, and thickly set with sāla, tāla, āśvakarṇa, and many bamboo groves.

**5.54.11**  It was ornamented with spreading canopies of flowering creepers, filled with herds of various beasts, and adorned with the flowing streams of mineral ores.

**5.54.12**  It was endowed with many waterfalls, made difficult by accumulations of rock, and frequented by great seers, yakṣas, gandharvas, kiṃnaras, and serpents.

**5.54.13**  Its caverns were dense with creepers and trees and troubled by lions; its slopes were crowded with packs of tigers; its trees bore sweet roots and fruits.

**5.54.14**  That foremost of leaping beings, of surpassing strength, ascended that mountain, spurred on by the joy that was quickened by his eagerness to see Rāma.

**5.54.15**  Trodden by the soles of his feet on the beautiful mountain ridges, the rocks split apart with a loud crash and were pulverized.

**5.54.16**  Having climbed that king of mountains, the great monkey grew in size, seeking to leap from the southern to the northern shore of the salt-sea.

**5.54.17**  Then that hero, son of the wind-god, having mounted the mountain, beheld the fearsome ocean frequented by fish and serpents.

**5.54.18**  That tiger among monkeys, born of the wind-god, sprang through the sky like the wind itself, traversing from south to north.

**5.54.19**  That finest of mountains, pressed by that monkey, roared along with the beings dwelling within it and sank into the earth, its peaks trembling and its trees falling.

**5.54.20**  Churned by the speed of his thighs, flower-bearing trees were uprooted and fell to the ground, as if struck down by Indra's weapon.

**5.54.21**  The terrifying roar of mighty lions dwelling in the hollows of the caves, disturbed and afflicted, was heard splitting the sky.

**5.54.22**  Vidyādharī women, their garments loosened and disheveled, their ornaments thrown into disarray, leapt suddenly from the mountain.

**5.54.23**  Enormous, powerful, blazing-tongued serpents of great venom, their heads and necks pressed down, writhed and coiled about.

**5.54.24**  Kiṃnaras, serpents, gandharvas, yakṣas, and vidyādharas likewise, abandoning that finest of mountains as it was being crushed, took to the sky.

**5.54.25**  And that glorious upholder of the earth, pressed by that mighty one, sank into the netherworld, its lofty peaks and trees intact.

**5.54.26**  That mountain, ten yojanas in breadth and thirty yojanas in height, became level with the earth.

**5.55.1**  The sky, lovely with the moon and water-lilies, auspicious with the sun and ruddy geese, clustered with the Tiṣya and Śravaṇa stars like kadamba blossoms, its meadows of grass like clouds and river-moss —

**5.55.2**  — with Punarvasu as its great fish, Mars as its great crocodile, the Airāvata constellation as its great island, stirred by the Svātī star and its swans —

**5.55.3**  — its waves born of clashing winds, its waters cooled by the moon's rays, its lotuses and water-lilies awakened among serpents, yakṣas, and gandharvas —

**5.55.4**  — as though swallowing the sky, as though painting the lord of stars, as though sweeping away the firmament together with the stellar hosts and the orb of the sun —

**5.55.5**  the glorious great monkey Hanūmān, dweller in wind, traveller through the sky, moved as though dragging apart the masses of clouds — the abode of the wind-god.

**5.55.6**  The great clouds gleamed — some pale and reddish, some dark blue and madder-red, others green and orange-hued.

**5.55.7**  Entering into the masses of cloud and emerging again and again, now hidden and now revealed, he appeared like the moon.

**5.55.8**  Roaring with a mighty roar whose sound rivalled the thunder of clouds, the great-spirited one came again across the middle of the ocean.

**5.55.9**  Having touched the lord of mountains Sunābha, the valiant one of great speed approached like a steel arrow loosed from a bowstring.

**5.55.10**  Having arrived a little way off and beholding the great mountain that resembled Mahendra and a thundercloud, that bull among monkeys let out a roar.

**5.55.11**  Hearing the roar of the roaring one, all those monkeys on every side became eager with longing to behold their companion.

**5.55.12**  Jāmbavān, that foremost of monkeys, his heart thrilled with delight, summoning all the monkeys spoke these words:

**5.55.13**  "In every respect that Hanūmān has accomplished his task — there is no doubt of this. For one who had not accomplished his task could not produce a roar of this kind."

**5.55.14**  Hearing the speed of his arms and thighs and the roar of that great-souled one, the monkeys, overjoyed, leapt up from here and there.

**5.55.15**  Exhilarated, longing to see Hanūmān, they moved from treetop to treetop, from peak to peak.

**5.55.16**  Delighted, those monkeys grasped the well-flowering branches at the treetops and waved them about like gleaming garments.

**5.55.17**  Beholding that great monkey descending, resembling a dense mass of cloud, all those monkeys stood with palms joined in reverence.

**5.55.18**  Then that swift monkey, himself mountain-like, descended upon the tree-covered summit of mount Mahendra.

**5.55.19**  Then all those bulls among monkeys, their hearts full of joy, gathered around and attended upon the great-souled Hanūmān.

**5.55.20**  Surrounding him, they all experienced the highest delight, all with gleaming faces, seeing him returned safe and well.

**5.55.21**  Bringing offerings, roots, and fruits, the monkeys honoured that foremost of monkeys, the son of the wind-god.

**5.55.22**  Some roared with joy, others made the kila-kila cry; exhilarated, those bulls among monkeys brought down branches of trees.

**5.55.23**  Then Hanūmān, that great monkey, reverently saluted his elders and seniors headed by Jāmbavān, and also the prince Aṅgada.

**5.55.24**  Honoured by those two, himself worthy of honour, and gratified by the monkeys, that valiant one reported briefly: "The queen has been found."

**5.55.25**  Taking the son of Vālin by the hand, he sat down in a pleasant wooded place on mount Mahendra.

**5.55.26**  Then Hanūmān, joyful, spoke to those bulls among monkeys: "The daughter of Janaka has been seen — she is dwelling in the Aśoka grove."

**5.55.27**  "Guarded by dreadful rākṣasīs, the blameless young woman, wearing her hair in a single braid, longs to behold Rāma; she is exhausted by fasting, soiled, matted-haired, and emaciated."

**5.55.28**  Then, hearing that great and nectar-like word "she has been seen" from the son of the wind-god, all the monkeys became joyful.

**5.55.29**  Some hissed, some roared, some of great strength bellowed; others made the kila-kila cry, and still others roared in response.

**5.55.30**  Some, those elephant-like monkeys, raised their tails in joy; they curled and waved their long, outstretched tails.

**5.55.31**  And others, those monkeys elephant-like in might, leapt from the mountain peaks and touched Hanūmān with delight.

**5.55.32**  Then Aṅgada, in the midst of all the monkey heroes, spoke these excellent words to Hanūmān who had just finished speaking:

**5.55.33**  "In courage and valour there is no monkey equal to you — you who have leapt across the vast ocean and returned again."

**5.55.34**  "By good fortune you have seen the queen, the glorious wife of Rāma. By good fortune Kākutstha will abandon the grief born of separation from Sītā."

**5.55.35**  Then the monkeys, surrounding Aṅgada, Hanūmān, and Jāmbavān, joyful, took their places on the broad rocks.

**5.55.36**  Those foremost of monkeys, eager to hear of the crossing of the ocean, the seeing of Laṅkā, of Sītā, and of Rāvaṇa, all stood with palms joined, their faces turned toward Hanūmān's countenance.

**5.55.37**  The glorious Aṅgada sat there, surrounded by many monkeys, attended upon like the lord of gods in heaven attended by the celestials.

**5.55.38**  The lofty, great summit of the mountain, occupied with joy by the renowned and illustrious Hanūmān and by Aṅgada with Aṅgada's arm clasped about him, blazed with splendour.

**5.56.1**  Then on the summit of that mountain Mahendra, the mighty monkeys with Hanūmān at their head experienced the highest joy.

**5.56.2**  Then Jāmbavān, himself overjoyed, questioned that joyful great monkey, the son of the wind-god, about what had transpired in the mission.

**5.56.3**  "How did you see the queen? And how does she fare there? And how has the cruel-actioned Daśānana behaved toward her?"

**5.56.4**  "Tell us all of this truthfully, O great monkey. Having heard the matter, we shall deliberate further upon the final determination of the course of action."

**5.56.5**  "And what is the matter that must be told by us when we go there, and what must be protected there — let your honour explain all that to us."

**5.56.6**  Assigned by him, his body hair standing on end with joy, bowing his head in reverence to the queen Sītā, he replied:

**5.56.7**  "As you yourselves witnessed, I leapt into the sky from the summit of Mahendra, composed and intent, desiring to reach the southern shore of the ocean.

**5.56.8**  "As I was going, something that seemed like a dreadful obstacle appeared: I saw a golden peak, divine and exceedingly lovely,

**5.56.9**  "standing blocking my path — I considered that mountain an obstacle.

**5.56.10**  "Approaching that divine, golden, foremost of mountains, my mind formed the thought: 'I must break through this.'

**5.56.11**  "And when struck by my tail, the peak of that great mountain, sun-like in brilliance, was shattered into a thousand fragments.

**5.56.12**  "And understanding my resolve, that great mountain spoke, as though gladdening my heart, a sweet word: 'Son!'

**5.56.13**  "'Know me also as your uncle, a friend of Mātariśvan — the one known as Maināka, dwelling in the great ocean.'

**5.56.14**  "'In former times, O son, the foremost mountains had wings and roamed the earth at will, troubling it on all sides.'

**5.56.15**  "'Hearing of the conduct of the mountains, the blessed Mahendra, the slayer of Pāka, cut off their wings with his thunderbolt by the thousands.'

**5.56.16**  "'But I was saved from that by your father, the great-souled Māruta — at that time, O child, I was hurled into the great ocean.'

**5.56.17**  "'And I must render assistance in the cause of Rāma, O subduer of foes — Rāma, the foremost of upholders of dharma, equal in valour to Mahendra.'

**5.56.18**  "Hearing this from the great-souled Maināka, having communicated my mission to the mountain, my mind was roused.

**5.56.19**  "And being given leave by the great-souled Maināka, adopting my utmost speed, I covered the remaining journey.

**5.56.20**  "Then for a long time I was advancing rapidly along the path; then I saw the goddess Surasā, mother of serpents.

**5.56.21**  "That goddess in the midst of the ocean addressed me: 'You, O foremost of monkeys, have been appointed as my prey by the immortals — therefore I shall devour you; you have been ordained for me after a long time.'

**5.56.22**  "Addressed thus by Surasā, I stood with palms joined, bowing, and with a changed countenance I spoke these words:

**5.56.23**  "'The glorious Rāma, son of Daśaratha, the scorcher of foes, has entered the Daṇḍaka forest together with his brother Lakṣmaṇa and with Sītā.'
