**5.56.24**  "'His wife Sītā has been abducted by the evil-souled Rāvaṇa — by Rāma's command I shall go to her.'

**5.56.25**  "'You who dwell in this domain, you ought to render assistance to Rāma.'

**5.56.26**  "'Or else, having seen Maithilī and Rāma the effortless worker, I shall return into your mouth — I promise you truly.'

**5.56.27**  "Addressed thus by me, that Surasā, who takes any form at will, declared: 'No one may pass beyond me — this is my boon.'

**5.56.28**  "Addressed thus by Surasā, I became ten yojanas in length; and in an instant I became half that measure in breadth.

**5.56.29**  "And her mouth was opened wide in proportion to my size. Seeing that gaping mouth, I made my body small.

**5.56.30**  "And in that moment I became thumb-sized again; swiftly plunging into that mouth, I emerged from it in an instant.

**5.56.31**  "The goddess Surasā, resuming her own form, said to me again: 'Go, gentle one, O foremost of monkeys, at your ease — may your purpose be accomplished!'

**5.56.32**  "'And bring together the Vaidehī with the great-souled Rāghava. Be happy, O mighty-armed one — I am pleased with you, monkey.'

**5.56.33**  "Then praised by all beings with 'Excellent! Excellent!', I soared up into the vast sky like Garuḍa.

**5.56.34**  "And my shadow was seized, and I could see nothing. With my speed arrested, looking in all ten directions, I could see nothing there by which my movement had been stolen.

**5.56.35**  "Then a thought arose in me: why has such an obstacle arisen in my passage — one whose form is not visible?

**5.56.36**  "With sorrowing gaze I cast my sight downward, and then I saw a dreadful rākṣasī lying in the water.

**5.56.37**  "Laughing a great laugh, I was addressed by that dreadful one — standing unperturbed she spoke these ungracious words:

**5.56.38**  "'Where are you going, O great-bodied one? You are the coveted prey of me who am hungry — satisfy my body long deprived of food.'

**5.56.39**  "With the word 'Very well!' I accepted her challenge; then I enlarged my body beyond the measure of her mouth.

**5.56.40**  "Her great and dreadful mouth grew wide in order to devour me; but she did not perceive me or the transformation I had wrought.

**5.56.41**  "Then in the twinkling of an eye I contracted my vast form and, tearing out her heart, I soared into the sky.

**5.56.42**  "That dreadful one, mountain-sized, her heart wrenched out by me, her arms released, fell into the salt ocean.

**5.56.43**  "And I heard the sky-going beings and siddhas together with cāraṇas: 'The dreadful rākṣasī Siṃhikā has been swiftly destroyed by Hanūmān.'

**5.56.44**  "Having slain her and remembering my urgent task, I traversed a great distance and saw the southern shore of the ocean adorned with mountains — where that city of Laṅkā lies.

**5.56.45**  "When the sun had set, I entered the city, the abode of the rākṣasas, unrecognised by the rākṣasas of dreadful might.

**5.56.46**  "There I searched the entire night for the daughter of Janaka, entering Rāvaṇa's inner apartments, but did not see the slender-waisted one.

**5.56.47**  "Then, not seeing Sītā in Rāvaṇa's dwelling, I reached a sea of grief and could discern no farther shore.

**5.56.48**  "And while grieving, I saw, enclosed by a wall stretched with gold, an excellent garden adjoining the palace.

**5.56.49**  "Leaping over the wall, I saw it filled with many trees.

**5.56.50**  "In the middle of the Aśoka grove stood a great śiṃśapā tree. Climbing it, I saw a golden grove of banana trees.

**5.56.51**  "Not far from the śiṃśapā tree I saw a forest-dweller — dark-complexioned, with lotus-petal eyes, her face emaciated by fasting.

**5.56.52**  "She was surrounded by misshapen and cruel rākṣasīs who subsist on flesh and blood — like a doe surrounded by tigresses.

**5.56.53**  "Seeing that woman, the blameless wife of Rāma, in such a state, I remained there on that very śiṃśapā tree, watching.

**5.56.54**  "Then I heard an extremely deep uproar of noise, mingled with the sounds of waist-ornaments and ankle-bells, in Rāvaṇa's dwelling.

**5.56.55**  "Then I, greatly agitated, contracted my form and sat in the śiṃśapā tree like a bird in the dense foliage.

**5.56.56**  "Then Rāvaṇa's wives and the mighty Rāvaṇa himself arrived at that place where Sītā stood.

**5.56.57**  "Seeing the lord of the rākṣasa hosts, the shapely Sītā drew her thighs together and clasped her full breasts with her arms.

**5.56.58**  "Daśagrīva addressed the deeply sorrowful Sītā, having prostrated with bowed head: 'Think highly of me!'

**5.56.59**  "'If in your pride and arrogance you do not accept me gladly, O Sītā, then after two months I shall drink your blood.'

**5.56.60**  "Hearing those words of the evil-souled Rāvaṇa, Sītā, most wrathful, spoke these excellent words:

**5.56.61**  "'O lowest of rākṣasas, how has your tongue not fallen out while speaking unspeakable words to the wife of Rāma of immeasurable splendour, lord of the Ikṣvāku line, and daughter-in-law of Daśaratha?'

**5.56.62**  "'What indeed is your valour, O ignoble one, O wicked one — you who abducted me in my husband's absence and came here, unseen by that great-souled one?'

**5.56.63**  "'You are not the equal of Rāma — you are not even fit to be his slave, nor mine. The Rāghava is worthy of sacrifice, truthful in speech, and renowned in battle.'

**5.56.64**  "Addressed thus with harsh words by the daughter of Janaka, Daśānana blazed up suddenly with anger, like fire blazing amid funeral-pyre bones.

**5.56.65**  "Rolling his cruel eyes and raising his right fist, he began to strike Maithilī — at which the women cried out in alarm.

**5.56.66**  "Leaping out from among the women, that wicked one's foremost wife, named Mandodarī, restrained him.

**5.56.67**  "And she, tormented by desire, spoke sweet words to him: 'What business do you have with Sītā, O one equal in valour to Mahendra? Sport with me today — Jānakī is not superior to me.'

**5.56.68**  "'Sport here, my lord, with the daughters of gods and gandharvas, with the daughters of yakṣas as well — what will you do with Sītā?'

**5.56.69**  "Then that mighty night-wanderer was suddenly lifted up and led to his own palace by those assembled women.

**5.56.70**  "When Daśagrīva had departed, the misshapen-faced rākṣasīs threatened Sītā with cruel and dreadful words.

**5.56.71**  "The daughter of Janaka counted their speech as mere straw, and their threats against Sītā were then utterly fruitless.

**5.56.72**  "Their roaring and efforts in vain, those flesh-eating rākṣasīs reported to Rāvaṇa Sītā's great resolve.

**5.56.73**  "Then all of them together, their hopes dashed and their efforts spent, having surrounded her on all sides, fell under the power of sleep.

**5.56.74**  "And when they had fallen asleep, Sītā, devoted to her husband's welfare, wailing piteously in her misery, grieved deeply, afflicted with sorrow.

**5.56.75**  "And seeing her in such a state, seeing Sītā's dreadful plight, I rested but my mind found no peace.

**5.56.76**  "And for the purpose of speaking with the daughter of Janaka, I thought about the method; then the lineage of the Ikṣvāku clan was placed by me in the foreground.

**5.56.77**  "Having heard the speech I spoke, honoured by the company of royal seers, the queen replied to me with eyes brimming with tears.

**5.56.78**  "'Who are you? By whom and how have you come here, O bull among monkeys? And what is your bond of affection with Rāma? Please tell me that.'

**5.56.79**  "Hearing those words of hers, I too spoke words: 'O queen, your husband Rāma has an ally of dreadful valour — the heroic king of monkeys of great might, named Sugrīva.'

**5.56.80**  "'Know me as his servant Hanūmān, come here — I have been sent to you by your husband, Rāma the effortless worker.'

**5.56.81**  "'And this — the glorious Rāma, that tiger among men, son of Daśaratha, has himself given this ring as a token of recognition to you, O illustrious one.'

**5.56.82**  "'That being so, I wish to be instructed by you, O queen — what shall I do? Shall I carry you to the side of Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa? Or is there some other resolution?'

**5.56.83**  "Hearing and understanding this, Sītā, the daughter of Janaka, said: 'Let the Rāghava come and, having vanquished Rāvaṇa, take me away.'

**5.56.84**  "Bowing my head in reverence to the noble, blameless queen, I asked for a token of recognition to delight Rāma's heart.

**5.56.85**  "Addressed thus, the shapely one, most greatly agitated, gave me the foremost, excellent jewel, and instructed me in words.

**5.56.86**  "Then, bowing reverently to that princess, composed in mind, I performed a clockwise circumambulation, my heart set on returning here.

**5.56.87**  "She spoke again a further word, having resolved upon it in her mind: 'Hanūmān, you must relate my situation to the Rāghava.'

**5.56.88**  "'Act in such a way that those two heroes, Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa, together with Sugrīva, come here speedily upon hearing.'

**5.56.89**  "'If this should be otherwise, my life shall be but two months. Kākutstha will not see me — I shall die here like one without a protector.'

**5.56.90**  "Hearing that pitiful speech, anger overtook me, and I looked upon the remaining task that must be done next.

**5.56.91**  "Then my body grew, becoming like a mountain; eager for battle, I began to destroy that grove.

**5.56.92**  "When the grove was broken and the animals and birds were confused and frightened, the misshapen-faced rākṣasīs woke up and looked about.

**5.56.93**  "And seeing me in that grove, gathering together from here and there, they quickly went to Rāvaṇa and reported.

**5.56.94**  "'O king, this formidable grove of yours has been destroyed by that wicked monkey, not knowing your valour, O mighty one.'

**5.56.95**  "'O lord of kings, command quickly the slaying of that evildoer of wicked intellect who has acted against your pleasure, so that he may come to destruction.'

**5.56.96**  "Hearing this, the lord of rākṣasas dispatched the rākṣasas called the Kiṃkaras, exceedingly difficult to overcome, who follow Rāvaṇa's will.

**5.56.97**  "Eighty thousand of them, bearing spears and clubs in their hands, were slain by me in that wooded place with a mace.

**5.56.98**  "Those remaining survivors among them, swift in their movement, went and reported to Rāvaṇa the army I had slain.

**5.56.99**  "Then a thought arose in me and I ascended the caitya tower.

**5.56.100**  "And having slain a hundred rākṣasas there with a pillar, I, who had become the ornament of Laṅkā, destroyed it in anger.

**5.56.101**  "Then he dispatched Jambumālin, the son of Prahasta.

**5.56.102**  "That rākṣasa, powerful and skilled in battle, together with his followers I slew with an exceedingly dreadful mace.

**5.56.103**  "Hearing this, the lord of rākṣasas Rāvaṇa sent the minister's sons, of great might, equipped with infantry forces — I sent all of them to Yama's abode with the very same mace.

**5.56.104**  "Hearing that the minister's sons, swift in battle, had been slain, Rāvaṇa sent five generals of armies, warriors — all of them together with their armies I slew entirely.

**5.56.105**  "Then again Daśagrīva sent his son Akṣa of great might together with many rākṣasas into battle.

**5.56.106**  "But that prince, son of Mandodarī, skilled in battle, who suddenly leapt into the sky — I seized him by the feet and, whirling him around a hundredfold together with his shield and sword, I crushed him.

**5.56.107**  "Hearing that Akṣa had come and been destroyed, Daśānana Rāvaṇa, most furious, directed his second son named Indrajit, the mighty one, wild with battle-frenzy.

**5.56.108**  "His entire army and he himself, that bull among rākṣasas — having rendered them powerless in battle, I experienced supreme joy.

**5.56.109**  "For it was with great confidence that this mighty-armed, mighty one had been sent by Rāvaṇa with heroes intoxicated with pride.

**5.56.110**  "But he bound me with a Brāhma weapon with great speed; then the rākṣasas there bound me with ropes.

**5.56.111**  "And taking hold of me, they led me to the presence of Rāvaṇa; being seen, I was addressed by the evil-souled Rāvaṇa.

**5.56.112**  "And questioned about my coming to Laṅkā and the slaying of those rākṣasas, I declared all of it there: 'It was for the sake of Sītā.'

**5.56.113**  "'Desirous of seeing her, I came to your abode, O lord — I am the monkey Hanūmān, the direct son of Māruta.'

**5.56.114**  "'Know me as Rāma's messenger, a monkey, the minister of Sugrīva — I have come here to your presence on Rāma's embassy.'

**5.56.115**  "'And hear also the message I convey to you — the lord of monkeys speaks to you, O lord of rākṣasas, a composed word, wholesome in dharma, artha, and kāma, beneficial as beneficial food.'

**5.56.116**  "'While I was dwelling on mount Ṛṣyamūka abounding in trees, Rāma the warrior, valiant in battle, came to friendship with me.'

**5.56.117**  "'He told me, O king: My wife has been abducted by a rākṣasa — for that reason you should make a compact with me for assistance.'

**5.56.118**  "'The Rāghava along with Lakṣmaṇa made a fire-witnessed alliance of friendship with Sugrīva who had been deprived of his kingdom by Vālin.'

**5.56.119**  "'By him, having felled Vālin in battle with a single arrow, Sugrīva was made the great king of monkeys, lord of those who leap.'

**5.56.120**  "'His assistance must be rendered by us here with our entire being — by him I have been sent rightfully to your presence here.'

**5.56.121**  "'Let Sītā be brought swiftly and given to the Rāghava, before the heroic monkeys scatter your forces.'

**5.56.122**  "'For the origin of the monkeys has not been known to anyone before — they are those who go to the presence of the gods when invited.'

**5.56.123**  "'Thus the king of monkeys speaks to you' — so I addressed him. Then he looked at me with a burning gaze, enraged, as though setting me on fire.

**5.56.124**  "By that rākṣasa of dreadful deeds I was condemned to death.

**5.56.125**  "Then his brother, named Vibhīṣaṇa, of great wisdom, petitioned that king of rākṣasas on my account.

**5.56.126**  "'The killing of a messenger is not found in the treatises on kingship, O rākṣasa; and a messenger must speak truly, as a well-wisher.'

**5.56.127**  "'Even in the case of a very great offence by a messenger, O one of incomparable might, mutilation is found — but not killing — according to the treatises.'

**5.56.128**  "Addressed thus by Vibhīṣaṇa, Rāvaṇa commanded those rākṣasas: 'Let this tail of his be set on fire today.'

**5.56.129**  "Then, hearing his word, my tail was wrapped all around with hemp bark and cotton cloth.

**5.56.130**  "Then those rākṣasas, ready-armed, of fierce valour, set my tail alight, striking it with wooden clubs.

**5.56.131**  "Bound as I was with many nooses and controlled by the rākṣasas, I felt no pain whatsoever — I who desired to see the city in daylight.

**5.56.132**  "Then those heroic rākṣasas, making loud proclamation, led me bound, enveloped in fire, along the royal road to the city gate.

**5.56.133**  "Then I contracted my very large body once more, freed myself from that bondage, and stood restored to my natural state.

**5.56.134**  "Seizing an iron club, I slew those guards; then I leapt swiftly to that city gate.

**5.56.135**  "And with my blazing tail, unperturbed, I burned that city with its towers and gateways, like the fire at the end of an age burning living beings.

**5.56.136**  "Having burned Laṅkā, again a fear overtook me: while burning Laṅkā, surely Sītā too has been burned — there is no doubt.

**5.56.137**  "Then I heard the auspicious speech of the cāraṇas who spoke a wondrous message: 'Jānakī has not been burned.'

**5.56.138**  "Then a thought arose in me after hearing that wondrous speech; the Vaidehī was seen again and I was sent off again by her.

**5.56.139**  "By the power of the Rāghava, and by your splendour, and for the purpose of Sugrīva's mission, all has been accomplished by me.

**5.56.140**  "All of this has been duly accomplished by me there — whatever remaining task has not been done here, let all of that now be carried out."

**5.57.1**  Having narrated all of that account, Hanūmān, son of the wind-god, proceeded to speak a further word:

**5.57.2**  "The effort of the Rāghava is fruitful, and the diligence of Sugrīva, having encountered the character of Sītā — as is my great leap.

**5.57.3**  "The character of the noble Sītā is worthy of her, O bulls among leapers — by her ascetic power she could sustain the worlds, or if angered she could burn them utterly.

**5.57.4**  "In every respect that Rāvaṇa, lord of rākṣasas, has grown excessively powerful — the body of one who has touched her has not been destroyed by her ascetic power.

**5.57.5**  "What a flame of fire touched by hand could not do, the daughter of Janaka, stirred to the turbulence of extreme anger, could do.

**5.57.6**  "In the middle of the Aśoka grove of the evil-souled Rāvaṇa, beneath the śiṃśapā tree, the virtuous one sits in pitiable condition.

**5.57.7**  "Surrounded by rākṣasīs, wasted by grief and anguish, she is like a crescent of the moon surrounded by streaks of cloud — shorn of its lustre.

**5.57.8**  "The Vaidehī gives no thought to Rāvaṇa who is arrogant with strength; she is devoted to her husband, lovely-hipped, and firm in resolve — Jānakī.

**5.57.9**  "For the auspicious Vaidehī is devoted to Rāma with her entire being, her mind fixed on Rāma alone — like Paulomī fixed upon Purandara.

**5.57.10**  "Clothed in a single garment, covered with dust as well, her limbs wasted by grief and anguish, Sītā is devoted to her husband's welfare.

**5.57.11**  "She was seen by me in the forest, that lady in the midst of rākṣasīs, threatened again and again by the misshapen rākṣasīs.

**5.57.12**  "Wearing her hair in a single braid, wretched, her every thought absorbed in her husband, sleeping on the ground, her limbs pale — like a lotus pool at the onset of winter.

**5.57.13**  "She who had turned away from Rāvaṇa's purpose and had resolved to die — that doe-eyed one was somehow brought to trust.

**5.57.14**  "Then she was spoken to and all the matter was revealed to her; and upon hearing of the friendship of Rāma and Sugrīva, she experienced joy.

**5.57.15**  "Her conduct is regulated and her devotion to her husband is supreme.

**5.57.16**  "That the great-souled Daśānana does not slay Daśagrīva — Rāma shall be merely the instrument in his slaying.

**5.57.17**  "Thus does the greatly blessed Sītā dwell, absorbed in grief — whatever should be done about this, let all of that now be brought to fruition."

**5.58.1**  Having heard those words, the son of Vālin addressed them all — having taken leave of Jāmbavān and the great monkeys who stood foremost among them.

**5.58.2**  "Since the mission has gone thus far and has been reported to you all, it is fitting that we now see those two princes together with Vaidehī."

**5.58.3**  "I alone am sufficient to destroy by force that city of Laṅkā with all its companies of rākṣasas, and Rāvaṇa the mighty as well."

**5.58.4**  "How much more so when accompanied by heroes — powerful, self-disciplined, skilled in weapons, capable vānara warriors such as yourselves, who are intent on victory!"

**5.58.5**  "I shall scatter Rāvaṇa in battle together with his army, his vanguard, his sons, and his brothers in arms."

**5.58.6**  "Even if Indrajit deploys his terrible divine weapons in battle — those of Brahmā, Indra, Rudra, Vāyu, and Varuṇa — I shall repel them all and slay the rākṣasas."

**5.58.7**  "With your approval, my valor shall check him."

**5.58.8**  "An incomparable and unceasing rain of mountains hurled by me could slay even the gods in battle — what then of those night-roaming demons?"

**5.58.9**  "Though the ocean should overstep its shores and Mount Mandara should be uprooted, no enemy host in battle could make Jāmbavān tremble."

**5.58.10**  "The heroic vānara, son of Vāyu — he alone is sufficient for the destruction of all the hosts of rākṣasas, and of the ancient rākṣasas as well."

**5.58.11**  "At the tremendous onslaught of Panasa and the great-souled Nīla, even Mount Mandara would be shattered to pieces — what then of the rākṣasas in battle?"

**5.58.12**  "Name a match for Mainda or Dvivida in battles involving gods, asuras, gandharvas, serpents, or birds — you cannot."

**5.58.13**  "These two best of vānaras, sons of the Aśvins, are of tremendous speed; filled with supreme pride born of the boon granted by the Grandfather."

**5.58.14**  "For the sake of honoring the Aśvins, the Grandfather of all worlds long ago bestowed upon these two an incomparable immunity from death."

**5.58.15**  "Intoxicated by the pride of that boon, these two heroic vānaras once overpowered the great army of the gods and drank the immortal nectar."

**5.58.16**  "These two alone, when roused to fury, are capable of destroying Laṅkā with all her horses, chariots, and elephants — let the other vānaras stand aside."

**5.58.17**  "Yet it would be improper for us vānaras — who have seen the goddess but not brought her — to go into the presence of the great-souled Rāghava without her."

**5.58.18**  "To report there merely that 'the goddess was seen but not brought back' — this seems to me improper for you, whose valor is renowned."

**5.58.19**  "Among you, best of vānaras, none is your equal in leaping and none is your equal in valor throughout the worlds, including among gods and asuras."

**5.58.20**  "Since the rākṣasa heroes have already been slain thus by Hanūmān, what else remains to be done here? Let us take Jānakī and depart."

**5.58.21**  Jāmbavān, best of monkeys and one who knows the meaning of things, spoke with great delight to Aṅgada who had thus resolved, speaking words full of purpose.

**5.58.22**  "This resolve is not incapable — as you see it, O prince. But let your gaze be fixed on the accomplishment of the mission in accordance with the will of Rāma."

**5.59.1**  Then the forest-dwellers — the heroes headed by Aṅgada and the great monkey Hanūmān — accepted the words of Jāmbavān.

**5.59.2**  Then all of them, filled with joy and with Vāyu's son going before them, the foremost of vānaras leaped away, departing from the summit of Mount Mahendra.

**5.59.3**  Like Meru and Mandara in their aspect, like great elephants in rut, massive in body and immense in strength, they seemed to cover the sky as they flew.

**5.59.4**  The beings of the air seemed to carry Hanūmān — honored by them, self-possessed, mighty in strength, and swift in his course — upon their very gazes.

**5.59.5**  Having secured the accomplishment of Rāghava's purpose and supreme glory for their master, they flew onward — their mission fulfilled, elevated by the success of their deeds.

**5.59.6**  All of them were eager to bring glad tidings; all delighted in the prospect of battle; all were resolute in purpose regarding vengeance for Rāma — stout-hearted warriors.

**5.59.7**  Leaping through the sky, those forest-dwellers arrived at a grove filled with trees and creepers that resembled Nandana, the garden of the gods.

**5.59.8**  This was the Madhuvana — the Honey Grove — so named, guarded and protected by Sugrīva, unapproachable by all creatures, yet delightful to the hearts of all.

**5.59.9**  It was ever guarded by the great and mighty monkey Dadhimukha — the maternal uncle of the great-souled Sugrīva, chief of the monkeys.

**5.59.10**  Arriving at that vast and most beloved grove of the lord of monkeys, the vānaras became supremely exuberant.

**5.59.11**  Then the vānaras, honey-tawny and joyful at seeing the great Madhuvana, approached the young prince Aṅgada and begged him for the honey.

**5.59.12**  Then the young prince, having obtained the consent of the elder monkeys headed by Jāmbavān, gave them freedom to partake of the honey.

**5.59.13**  And so, with permission given, all the forest-dwellers were overwhelmed with joy; delighted, they began to dance here and there.

**5.59.14**  Some sang; some bowed down; some danced; some laughed loudly; some tumbled and rolled; some wandered about; some leaped; some chattered away.

**5.59.15**  Some leaned upon one another; some boasted against each other; some leaped from tree to tree; some fell from the treetops to the ground.

**5.59.16**  Some, rising with great speed from the ground, flew up to the tops of great trees; one who was singing approached another laughing, and another laughing approached one who laughed.

**5.59.17**  One who was weeping approached another weeping; one who was shoving another came and shoved him back. The monkey army was utterly in chaos, the behavior of every creature intoxicated by drinking honey. Not one among them was not drunk; not one among them was not satiated.

**5.59.18**  Then, seeing the grove being plundered and the trees stripped of their leaves and blossoms, the monkey named Dadhimukha, in his fury, tried to restrain those monkeys.

**5.59.19**  Threatened by those bold and spirited monkeys, the grove's guardian — elder among the heroic vānaras and fierce in his energy — resolved once more to defend the forest against the monkeys.

**5.59.20**  He spoke harsh words to some boldly and without restraint; he struck others with his palms; he came to blows with still others; and to yet others he approached with conciliation.

**5.59.21**  But he was overpowered by those whose onslaught could not be checked for the force of their intoxication, and who themselves could not be stopped — fearless, they came upon him heedless of any fault, and dragged him about.

**5.59.22**  Stabbing him with their nails, biting him with their teeth, pummeling him with their palms and feet, all the monkeys together, in their drunken state, laid that guardian monkey low and left the great grove utterly ravaged.

**5.60.1**  Hanūmān, best of monkeys and foremost bull among vānaras, spoke to them: "With minds undisturbed, partake freely of the honey, O monkeys."

**5.60.2**  Hearing Hanūmān's words, Aṅgada — foremost among monkeys — replied with a clear and cheerful heart: "Let the monkeys drink honey."

**5.60.3**  "The word of Hanūmān — one who has accomplished his mission — must of necessity be honored by me. Even what might otherwise be improper must be done; how much more so a matter such as this."

**5.60.4**  Hearing those words from the mouth of Aṅgada, the bull-like vānaras responded with joyful acclamation, crying "Excellent! Excellent!" in his honor.

**5.60.5**  Having honored Aṅgada, that bull among vānaras, all the monkeys rushed toward the Madhuvana swift as the current of a river.

**5.60.6**  Elated — they who had seen and heard of Maithilī — and given free rein, those capable warriors overpowered the keepers of the Madhuvana by force.

**5.60.7**  Then they all leaped up and rushed at the grove-keepers who had assembled, striking them by the hundreds as they stood their ground in the Madhuvana.

**5.60.8**  Taking up great vessels of honey — droṇa-measures full — they struck one another; others consumed it together in great quantities.

**5.60.9**  Some honey-tawny monkeys, having drunk their fill, tossed the honey aside; others, intoxicated and unruly, pelted each other with the honeycomb leavings.

**5.60.10**  Others sat at the roots of trees clutching branches, and then, overcome to excess by the stupor of intoxication, spread leaves and lay down to sleep.

**5.60.11**  The vānaras, like mad creatures and drunk with honey, flung themselves joyfully at one another, and still others stumbled and reeled about.

**5.60.12**  Some uttered wild shrieks; some cooed in delight; the monkeys drunk on honey lay stretched out asleep on the ground.

**5.60.13**  Even the honey-keepers who were servants of Dadhimukha were driven back by those fearsome monkeys and fled in all directions.

**5.60.14**  Dragged by the knees, shown the sky, and filled with great anguish, they went and spoke these words to Dadhimukha.

**5.60.15**  "The Madhuvana has been destroyed by force by those given leave by Hanūmān. We ourselves were dragged by the knees and shown the sky."

**5.60.16**  Then Dadhimukha the grove-warden, enraged upon hearing that the Madhuvana had been laid waste, pacified those monkeys who had come to him.

**5.60.17**  "Come, let us go! We shall restrain by force those arrogant monkeys who are consuming the honey."

**5.60.18**  Hearing these words of Dadhimukha, those heroic bull-like vānaras went once more to the Madhuvana, together with him.

**5.60.19**  Dadhimukha, in the midst of them and seizing an immense tree, rushed forward swiftly — and all the other vānaras did likewise.

**5.60.20**  Those monkeys, taking up rocks, trees, and boulders in their anger, came charging toward where the great elephant-like monkeys were.

**5.60.21**  Those heroes, fixing their master's command in their hearts, rushed forward swiftly, armed with śāla trees, palmyra palms, and rocks.

**5.60.22**  The grove-guardian warriors, by the thousands, fell upon the monkeys — those perched in trees and those standing on the ground — who were arrogant in their strength.

**5.60.23**  Then, seeing Dadhimukha enraged, the bull-like vānaras led by Hanūmān charged at him with great speed.

**5.60.24**  As that great-armed and mighty one came rushing forward with his tree, the furious Aṅgada there struck that elder with both arms.

**5.60.25**  Blinded by intoxication, he did not recognize that this was his respected elder; swiftly and with great force he slammed him hard into the ground.

**5.60.26**  With his arm broken, turning away, overwhelmed and drenched in blood, that heroic elephant among monkeys instantly fell senseless for a moment.

**5.60.27**  That bull among vānaras somehow freed himself from those monkeys, withdrew to one side, and spoke to those servants who had come to him.

**5.60.28**  "Let these ones stay here; we shall go to where our master, the broad-necked vānara Sugrīva, stands together with Rāma."

**5.60.29**  "I shall report all of Aṅgada's transgressions to the king. He, the impatient one, upon hearing the words, will have the monkeys put to death."

**5.60.30**  "For this Madhuvana is the beloved grove of the great-souled Sugrīva — inherited from fathers and grandfathers, divine, and unapproachable even by the gods."

**5.60.31**  "Sugrīva will punish and put to death all these monkeys — greedy for honey, their lives now forfeit — along with their companions."

**5.60.32**  "These wicked ones who have disregarded the king's command are fit for death. The anger born of our outrage shall bear fruit."

**5.60.33**  Having spoken thus, the mighty Dadhimukha, together with the grove-keepers, leaped up at once and departed.

**5.60.34**  In no more than the twinkling of an eye, that forest-dweller arrived at the place where the wise Sugrīva — son of the Sun of a thousand rays — stood.

**5.60.35**  Seeing Rāma, Lakṣmaṇa, and Sugrīva himself, he descended from the sky and alighted on the level ground.

**5.60.36**  Having landed, that great and mighty monkey Dadhimukha, supreme lord of the grove-keepers, was surrounded by all of those keepers.

**5.60.37**  With a look of misery on his face and his joined hands raised to his head, he pressed his forehead to the auspicious feet of Sugrīva.

**5.61.1**  Then the bull among monkeys, seeing the monkey fallen at his feet with bowed head, was troubled at heart and spoke these words.

**5.61.2**  "Rise, rise! Why have you fallen at my feet? You shall have no fear, O hero — speak only the truth."

**5.61.3**  Reassured by that great-souled Sugrīva, the highly wise Dadhimukha rose up and spoke.

**5.61.4**  "O king, this grove was never granted to be enjoyed — not by Ṛkṣarajas, not by you, not by Vālin — yet those monkeys have eaten of it.

**5.61.5**  Even though the grove-wardens restrained and forbade them, these monkeys, heedless of us, ate and drank the honey.

**5.61.6**  Some threw away what remained; others ate it; and all of them, when stopped, merely raised their eyebrows in contempt.

**5.61.7**  The wardens, in trying to bar those best of monkeys from the grove, were then set upon and assaulted by those furious monkey-bulls.

**5.61.8**  Then those many heroic monkeys, eyes reddened with fury, O bull among monkeys, struck down the wardens.

**5.61.9**  Some were beaten with fists, some struck with knees; others were dragged about at will and shown the sky — hurled through the air.

**5.61.10**  Thus our brave wardens have been beaten while you, their lord, still reign; and the entire Madhuvana has been eaten by them to their fill.

**5.61.11**  While Sugrīva, bull among monkeys, was being informed thus, the highly wise Lakṣmaṇa, slayer of enemy heroes, questioned him.

**5.61.12**  "O king, why has this grove-keeper monkey come to present himself before you? Indicating what matter did he speak in distress?"

**5.61.13**  Thus addressed by the great-souled Lakṣmaṇa, Sugrīva, skilled in speech, replied to him in these words.

**5.61.14**  "Noble Lakṣmaṇa, the brave monkey Dadhimukha reports that the Madhuvana has been consumed by the heroes led by Aṅgada.

**5.61.15**  Such boldness as this would not belong to monkeys who had failed in their mission; by entering that grove they have signalled that their task is accomplished.

**5.61.16**  Without doubt the goddess Sītā has been found — and by none other than Hanūmān; for there is no other agent capable of accomplishing this deed but Hanūmān.

**5.61.17**  O best of monkeys, success in this mission rests in Hanūmān — the resolve, the valour, the learning, all are firmly established in him.

**5.61.18**  Where Jāmbavān is the guide, Aṅgada the lord of the host, and Hanūmān the presiding spirit, the outcome cannot be otherwise.

**5.61.19**  The Madhuvana has been ravaged by the valiant heroes led by Aṅgada — those best of monkeys who have returned having searched the southern quarter.

**5.61.20**  Having returned, they entered the Madhuvana and the monkeys ravaged the entire grove and enjoyed it to the full; the wardens who tried to stop them together were struck with knees.

**5.61.21**  It is for this purpose that this sweet-spoken monkey of celebrated prowess, named Dadhimukha, has come here to report."

**5.61.22**  "O great-armed Saumitri, consider the truth of it: Sītā has been found — see how all the monkeys drink honey upon arriving there!"

**5.61.23**  "O bull among men, renowned monkeys would not dare to violate the divine grove granted as a boon, without having first found the daughter of Videha."

**5.61.24**  Then Lakṣmaṇa, righteous in spirit, together with Rāghava, was filled with joy upon hearing the sweet words that flowed from Sugrīva's mouth.

**5.61.25**  Rāma rejoiced greatly, and so did the greatly glorious Lakṣmaṇa; and Sugrīva, himself overjoyed at hearing Dadhimukha's report, addressed the grove-keeper once more.

**5.61.26**  "Dear friend, I am pleased that those who have accomplished the work ate of the grove; what those who have done their duty have done is entirely fitting and must be pardoned."

**5.61.27**  "I desire at once to see those tree-leaping monkeys led by Hanūmān — bold as lions, their mission accomplished — together with the two Rāghavas, and to hear the effort made in finding Sītā."

**5.62.1**  Thus addressed by Sugrīva, the delighted monkey Dadhimukha paid obeisance to Rāghava, to Lakṣmaṇa, and to Sugrīva.

**5.62.2**  Having bowed to Sugrīva and to the two mighty Rāghavas, he leapt up into the sky together with his company of brave monkeys.

**5.62.3**  Just as he had come, so he went back swiftly; descending from the sky to the earth, he entered that grove.

**5.62.4**  Entering the Madhuvana, he found all the monkey-troop leaders, freed of their intoxication yet still elated, urinating the honey-liquor.

**5.62.5**  That hero approached them and, joining his cupped palms in añjali, spoke these gentle words in a tone of gladness to Aṅgada.

**5.62.6**  "Dear lord, let there be no anger over the fact that the wardens who stopped you did so in anger and out of ignorance — they did not know who you were."

**5.62.7**  "O greatly powerful one, you are the crown prince and lord of this forest; the offence was committed beforehand out of foolishness — it befits you to forgive it."

**5.62.8**  "Just as your father formerly was lord of the monkey host, so too is Sugrīva — no other, O best of monkeys."

**5.62.9**  "O sinless one, I have gone and reported to your paternal uncle the arrival here of all these forest-wandering monkeys."

**5.62.10**  "He, upon hearing of your coming together with these monkey-troop leaders, was overjoyed — not at all angry upon hearing that the grove was ravaged."

**5.62.11**  "Your paternal uncle Sugrīva, lord of monkeys, rejoicing, commanded me the king: 'Send them all here swiftly.'"

**5.62.12**  Hearing this gentle speech of Dadhimukha, Aṅgada, best of monkeys and skilled in speech, addressed those monkeys with words.

**5.62.13**  "I think this whole account has been heard by Rāma, O troop-leaders of the monkeys; now that our mission is done, it is not fitting for us to remain here, O afflicters of foes."

**5.62.14**  "O forest-wanderers, having drunk honey to our satisfaction and rested, what remains but to go there where my elder, Sugrīva, awaits?"

**5.62.15**  "I shall act in accordance with what all the troop-leaders say when they come together; I am dependent on you all in what must be done."

**5.62.16**  "I am not fit to issue commands, even though I am crown prince; it would be improper for me to overrule you who have accomplished the mission."

**5.62.17**  As Aṅgada spoke thus, the forest-dwellers heard his imperishable words and, their hearts filled with joy, spoke these words.

**5.62.18**  "O king, O bull among monkeys, who, being a master, would speak thus? Every man intoxicated with the pride of lordship thinks only of himself."

**5.62.19**  "These words are most fitting for you alone, not for any other; your humility proclaims the auspicious fortune that lies ahead for you."

**5.62.20**  "We are all ready and the moment is right to go there where the undying Sugrīva, lord of the valiant monkeys, abides."

**5.62.21**  "O best of monkeys, monkeys unsummoned by you cannot move a single step anywhere — this we tell you truly."

**5.62.22**  As they spoke thus, Aṅgada replied to them; saying "Very well, let us go," he leapt up from the surface of the earth.

**5.62.23**  All those monkey-troop leaders leapt up after him as he leapt, filling the sky to overflowing, like flames flung up by a sacrifice.

**5.62.24**  Those swift-moving monkeys, leaping at once into the sky, roared with great cries, like clouds driven by the wind.

**5.62.25**  While Aṅgada had not yet arrived, Sugrīva, lord of monkeys, spoke to lotus-eyed Rāma who was afflicted with grief.

**5.62.26**  "Take heart — good fortune to you! The goddess Sītā has been found, without doubt; they could not have come here if the time allotted had passed."

**5.62.27**  "The crown prince, great-armed Aṅgada, best among leapers, would not have come to me if the mission had been a failure."

**5.62.28**  "Even if the conduct of one who had not accomplished the task were such as this, his face would be downcast and his mind wandering and confused."

**5.62.29**  "This Madhuvana, guarded by our forebears and inherited from father and grandfather, no lord of monkeys bereft of joy would dare to ravage."

**5.62.30**  "O Rāma of good vows, Kausalyā is blessed in her son — take comfort! The goddess Sītā has been found, beyond doubt, and by none other than Hanūmān; no other agent of that kind could have accomplished this deed."

**5.62.31**  "O best of the wise, success, intelligence, determination, and valour are fixed in Hanūmān as surely as radiance is fixed in the sun."

**5.62.32**  "Where Jāmbavān is the guide, Aṅgada the lord of the host, and Hanūmān the presiding spirit, the outcome cannot be otherwise."

**5.62.33**  "O you of boundless valour, be not prey to anxiety any longer."

**5.62.34**  Then he heard nearby in the sky the kilakila shout of the forest-dwellers intoxicated with their achievement, roaring as they approached Kiṣkindhā, as if proclaiming their success.

**5.62.35**  Then, upon hearing that roar of the monkeys, that best of monkeys — his long tail raised and curled — became glad at heart.

**5.62.36**  Those monkeys came too, eager to see Rāma, placing Aṅgada and the monkey Hanūmān at their head.

**5.62.37**  Those heroes led by Aṅgada, filled with delight and joy, alighted near the king of monkeys and near Rāghava.

**5.62.38**  And great-armed Hanūmān, having bowed his head in reverence, announced to Rāghava that the goddess Sītā was well and unharmed.

**5.62.39**  Then, with the matter resolved in Hanūmān, the son of the Wind, Lakṣmaṇa looked upon the pleased Sugrīva with affection and great esteem.

**5.62.40**  And Rāghava, slayer of enemy heroes, delighting in his joy, looked upon Hanūmān with the greatest honour and esteem.

**5.63.1**  Then those monkeys went to Mount Prasravaṇa with its colourful forest and bowed their heads to Rāma and to the mighty Lakṣmaṇa.

**5.63.2**  Placing the crown prince Aṅgada at their head, and having saluted Sugrīva, they began to report what had happened concerning Sītā.

**5.63.3**  They told of her confinement in Rāvaṇa's inner chambers, the threats made by the rākṣasī women, her deep devotion to Rāma, and the compact that had been made.

**5.63.4**  All those monkeys reported this in Rāma's presence; and upon hearing that the daughter of Videha was unharmed, Rāma spoke in reply.

**5.63.5**  "Where does the goddess Sītā dwell, and how does she bear herself toward me? Tell me everything regarding the daughter of Videha, O monkeys."

**5.63.6**  Having heard Rāma's words, the monkeys in Rāma's presence urged Hanūmān, who was expert in the particulars of Sītā's situation.

**5.63.7**  Hearing their words, Hanūmān, the son of the Wind God, skilled in speech, spoke, recounting exactly how he had seen Sītā.

**5.63.8**  "Having leapt across the ocean stretching a hundred yojanas in breadth, I went searching with longing to see Jānakī Sītā."

**5.63.9**  "There stands Laṅkā, the city of the evil-souled Rāvaṇa, on the southern shore of the southern ocean."

**5.63.10**  "There I found Sītā, the virtuous one, in Rāvaṇa's inner chambers, living still, having set all her hope upon you, O Rāma."

**5.63.11**  "I saw her in the midst of rākṣasī women, threatened again and again, guarded by misshapen rākṣasīs in the pleasure grove."

**5.63.12**  "The goddess, she who is not accustomed to sorrow, suffers great distress, confined in Rāvaṇa's inner chambers and closely guarded by the rākṣasī women."

**5.63.13**  "She wears her hair in a single braid, wretched, absorbed in thoughts of you, sleeping on the ground, her limbs discoloured — like a lotus pond at the onset of winter."

**5.63.14**  "The goddess, her desire turned utterly away from Rāvaṇa, resolved to die — thinking of you alone, O Kākutstha, she was somehow found by me."

**5.63.15**  "O sinless tiger among men, I gradually won her trust by recounting your fame and the glory of the Ikṣvāku lineage."

**5.63.16**  "Then the goddess was spoken to and shown the full state of affairs; upon hearing of the friendship between Rāma and Sugrīva, she was filled with joy."

**5.63.17**  "Her devotion and steadfast conduct toward you are unwavering; thus was the greatly blessed daughter of Janaka seen by me, O bull among men, enduring fierce austerity sustained by her devotion to you."

**5.63.18**  "A token of recognition was given to me by her — the incident as it truly occurred between you and the crow at Citrakūṭa, O Rāghava of great wisdom."

**5.63.19**  "'The tiger among men, Rāma, should be informed by you, O son of the Wind, of everything that was seen here' — thus Jānakī told me."

**5.63.20**  "'And this token, carefully preserved, should be given to him' — speaking these words even as Sugrīva listened."

**5.63.21**  "'This glorious crest-jewel has been guarded by me with great care for you; and also the tika-mark of manaḥśilā mineral — remember that occasion,' she said."

**5.63.22**  "'This glorious jewel born of water is now rendered up by me to you; seeing this, O sinless one, I shall take joy in my affliction as if I saw you yourself,' she said."

**5.63.23**  "'I shall sustain my life for one month, O son of Daśaratha; beyond a month I cannot live, fallen under the power of the rākṣasas,' she said."

**5.63.24**  "Thus spoke Sītā to me — slender-limbed, righteous in conduct, imprisoned in Rāvaṇa's inner chambers, her wide eyes like those of a doe."

**5.63.25**  "All this I have reported to you exactly as it occurred, O Rāghava; now by every means let the crossing of the ocean's waters be arranged."

**5.63.26**  Both princes having thus found reassurance, and the token having been presented to Rāghava, the son of the Wind narrated in full with complete words, in order, everything that the goddess had related.

**5.64.1**  Thus addressed by Hanūmān, Rāma, son of Daśaratha, pressing that jewel to his heart, wept together with Lakṣmaṇa.

**5.64.2**  Beholding that finest of jewels, Rāghava, wasted by grief, his two eyes brimming with tears, spoke these words to Sugrīva.

**5.64.3**  Just as a tender cow flows with milk out of love for her calf, so too my heart melts at the sight of this precious jewel.

**5.64.4**  This precious jewel was given to Vaidehī by my father-in-law; fastened as it was at the time of her wedding, it shines most beautifully upon her head.

**5.64.5**  This jewel, born of water, is honored by the greatest; it was given by the wise Śakra, greatly pleased, at a sacrifice.

**5.64.6**  Beholding this finest of jewels, O gentle one, today I understand it as the very sight of my father, and likewise of the great king of Videha.

**5.64.7**  This jewel gleams upon the head of my beloved; today, by the sight of it, I imagine her as if she were present before me.

**5.64.8**  What did Sītā, the daughter of Videha, say? Tell me again and again, O gentle one—reviving me as if with water, one nearly dead, with the water of her words.

**5.64.9**  What grief could be greater than this—that I behold this jewel born of water, O son of Sumitrā, without Vaidehī having come with it?

**5.64.10**  Vaidehī will survive—if she can endure for a month; but I, O gentle one, could not live for even a moment without her, she of the dark eyes.

**5.64.11**  Take me also to that place where my beloved was seen; having learned of her condition, I could not remain even for a moment.

**5.64.12**  How does she, my fair-hipped one, the timid, the ever-faithful one, dwell amid the fearsome and terrible rākṣasas who inspire dread?

**5.64.13**  Surely her face, shrouded by those rākṣasas, no longer shines—like the autumnal moon whose face is veiled by clouds.

**5.64.14**  What did Sītā say, O Hanūmān? Tell me truthfully—by this I shall live, as a sick man lives by medicine.

**5.64.15**  The sweet one, sweet of speech—what did my passionate one say, she of noble hips, separated from me? Tell me, O Hanūmān: having attained sorrow upon sorrow, how does Jānakī live?

**5.65.1**  Thus addressed by the great-souled Rāghava, Hanūmān reported to Rāghava everything that Sītā had spoken.

**5.65.2**  "This is what the goddess Jānakī said, O bull among men: she gave a token from former events at Citrakūṭa, exactly as they happened."

**5.65.3**  "Jānakī, having slept pleasantly at your side, awoke before you; a crow suddenly flew up and cried out, pecking between her breasts."

**5.65.4**  "Then you slept in turn in the goddess's lap, O elder brother of Bharata; and again that bird caused pain to the goddess."

**5.65.5**  "Then coming again it cried out fiercely; thereupon you were awakened, drenched with her blood."

**5.65.6**  "Awakened by that very crow which had been tormenting the goddess without ceasing, you, O scorcher of foes, had been sleeping peacefully."

**5.65.7**  "Seeing her weeping with wounds between her breasts, O mighty-armed one, you breathed heavily like an angry serpent and addressed her."

**5.65.8**  "'Who, O timid one, has torn the space between your breasts with nail-tips? Who plays with an enraged five-headed serpent?'"

**5.65.9**  "Looking around suddenly, you saw the crow—standing before you with sharp blood-stained talons—facing you directly."

**5.65.10**  "That crow was reportedly the son of Śakra, best among birds, roaming between earth and sky, swift as the wind in its flight."

**5.65.11**  "Then, O mighty-armed one, O best among the wise, your eyes rolling in anger, you resolved upon a cruel intention against that crow."

**5.65.12**  "Taking a blade of darbha grass from the spread mat, you charged it with the Brahmāstra; blazing like the fire of dissolution, it flamed toward the bird."

**5.65.13**  "You hurled that blazing blade of grass at the crow; and then that blazing grass indeed pursued the crow."

**5.65.14**  "Abandoned by his father, by all the gods, and by the great sages, he wandered through the three worlds and found no protector."

**5.65.15**  "Him fallen to the ground, O Kākutstha, refuge of the refuge-seeking, though deserving of death, you protected out of compassion."

**5.65.16**  "The weapon could not be made to go in vain, O Rāghava—thus it destroyed the right eye of that eye-plucking crow."

**5.65.17**  "Bowing to you, O Rāma, and to king Daśaratha, that crow was then released and returned to the sky as his home."

**5.65.18**  "You who are the foremost among those who know weapons, possessed of valor, possessed of good character—why do you not employ your weapons against the rākṣasas, O Rāghava?"

**5.65.19**  "Neither nāgas, nor gandharvas, nor asuras, nor the host of Maruts are capable of standing before you in battle, O Rāma, and withstanding you."

**5.65.20**  "If you, so mighty, have any concern for me, then let Rāvaṇa be slain swiftly in battle with your well-sharpened arrows."

**5.65.21**  "And Lakṣmaṇa, the scorcher of foes, acting under his brother's command—why does he, that best of men, that Rāghava, not protect me?"

**5.65.22**  "Those two tigers among men, equal in splendor to wind and fire, unconquerable even by the gods—why do they disregard me?"

**5.65.23**  "Surely some great sin of mine exists—there is no doubt—since those two scorchers of foes, capable and united, do not regard me."

**5.65.24**  "Having heard that pitiful, tear-choked speech of Vaidehī, I again spoke these words to that noble lady."

**5.65.25**  "'Rāma is free of grief for you, O goddess—I swear this to you by truth; and when Rāma is overwhelmed by sorrow, Lakṣmaṇa also burns with pain.'"

**5.65.26**  "'You have somehow been found—it is not the time for grieving; this very moment you shall see the end of your sorrows, O passionate one.'"

**5.65.27**  "'Those two tigers among men, princes, destroyers of enemies, roused to eagerness by the prospect of seeing you, will reduce Laṅkā to ashes.'"

**5.65.28**  "'And having slain the terrible Rāvaṇa along with his kin in battle, the mighty-armed Rāghava will certainly lead you back to his own city.'"

**5.65.29**  "'Whatever token might let Rāma know—something that will generate delight in him, O blameless one—you should truly give it.'"

**5.65.30**  "She, gazing in all directions, loosened the excellent binding of her braid and, from her garment, gave me this jewel, O mighty one."

**5.65.31**  "Receiving that divine jewel for your sake, O best of the Raghus, bowing to her with my head, I hastened in my coming."

**5.65.32**  "And seeing me roused to eagerness for departure and growing in size, the daughter of Janaka, that fair-complexioned one, her face filled with tears, pitiful, her words choked with weeping, spoke to me."

**5.65.33**  "'O Hanūmān, convey my greeting of freedom from ill to both Rāma and Lakṣmaṇa, who are like lions, and to Sugrīva together with his ministers, to all of them.'"

**5.65.34**  "'And you should endeavor to arrange how that mighty-armed Rāghava may rescue me from this flood of sorrow that hems me in.'"

**5.65.35**  "'And when you reach Rāma's presence, tell him of this fierce surge of my grief and of the taunts of these rākṣasas. May your journey be auspicious, O hero among monkeys.'"

**5.65.36**  "This is the speech that your noble Sītā spoke to you, O lion among kings of kings, full of despondency. Understanding this, I have told you—believe that Sītā is well and whole."

**5.66.1**  "Then I was again addressed urgently by the goddess with a further message: 'Out of love for you, O tiger among men, and having obtained your friendship—'

**5.66.2**  "'—you must speak to Rāma, son of Daśaratha, in many such ways, so that he may quickly recover me, having slain Rāvaṇa in battle.'"

**5.66.3**  "'Or if you think it fit, O hero, O subduer of enemies, stay one day in some hidden place; rested, you shall depart tomorrow.'"

**5.66.4**  "'And by your nearness to me, O monkey—I who am so bereft of good fortune—there might be for a moment a release from the full ripening of this grief.'"

**5.66.5**  "'For once you, the mighty, have departed with regard to returning, even my very survival will be in doubt—of this there is no uncertainty.'"

**5.66.6**  "'The grief born of not seeing you would torment me further—me, overcome by sorrow upon sorrow, fallen low, a sharer in misery.'"

**5.66.7**  "'Yet this great doubt about your heroes stands as it were before me—a very great doubt, without question, concerning your allies, the monkeys and bears.'"

**5.66.8**  "'How will those armies of monkeys and bears cross the great ocean, so difficult to cross? Or how will those two sons of the best of men?'"

**5.66.9**  "'The power to leap over this ocean would belong to only three beings: Vainateya, or Vāyu, or you, O blameless monkey.'"

**5.66.10**  "'So in this undertaking of heroes, so difficult to accomplish, what solution do you see? Tell me, O best among those who know what must be done.'"

**5.66.11**  "'Granted, you alone are sufficient for the accomplishment of this task, O slayer of enemy heroes; your rise of power brings you glory.'"

**5.66.12**  "'But if Rāma, victorious, were to lead me to his own city with his full forces, having slain Rāvaṇa in battle—that would bring him glory.'"

**5.66.13**  "'Just as I was carried off from the forest by that rākṣasa through guile, Rāghava should not act out of that same fear alone—that would not befit him.'"

**5.66.14**  "'But if Kākutstha, the crusher of enemy armies, were to overrun Laṅkā with his forces and then lead me away—that would be worthy of him.'"

**5.66.15**  "'So arrange it in such a way that it may be fitting for that great-souled one's heroism, fitting for the valor of that hero in battle.'"

**5.66.16**  "Having heard that speech, laden with meaning, humble, joined with reasoning, I then spoke the following further words in reply."

**5.66.17**  "'O goddess, Sugrīva, lord of the armies of monkeys and bears, best among the leapers, endowed with valor, is firmly resolved for your sake.'"

**5.66.18**  "'The monkeys who stand under his command are endowed with heroism, possessed of valor, of great strength, swift as the speed of the mind's resolve.'"

**5.66.19**  "'Their movement is unimpeded above, below, or sideways; and they do not falter in great deeds—those of immeasurable splendor.'"

**5.66.20**  "'Not once but many times have those greatly fortunate, powerful monkeys, who travel the path of the wind, circumambulated the earth.'"

**5.66.21**  "'There are forest-dwellers there who are both superior to me and equal to me; none in Sugrīva's company is inferior to me.'"

**5.66.22**  "'I, at least, have reached here—how much more so those of great power? For it is not the foremost who are sent on missions; it is the lesser folk who are sent.'"

**5.66.23**  "'Therefore, enough of anguish, O goddess; let your anger depart. The leaders of monkey troops will come to Laṅkā in a single leap.'"

**5.66.24**  "'And those two—like the risen sun and moon—mounted upon my back, those two lion-men will come into your presence, O greatly fortunate one.'"

**5.66.25**  "'You will swiftly see Rāghava, the slayer of enemies, like a lion, and Lakṣmaṇa with bow in hand, standing at the gates of Laṅkā.'"

**5.66.26**  "'You will swiftly see the heroes assembled—armed with nails and fangs, with the stride of lions and tigers, those monkeys resembling lords of monkeys.'"

**5.66.27**  "'You will soon hear the roar of the chiefs of monkeys, resembling mountains and storm-clouds, roaring upon the slopes of Laṅkā's Malaya hills.'"

**5.66.28**  "'And you will swiftly see Rāghava—his forest exile over, the subduer of enemies—consecrated as king at Ayodhyā, together with you.'"

**5.66.29**  "Then, consoled by me with auspicious and pleasing words spoken without despondency, the daughter of the king of Mithilā attained peace—she too, so afflicted by grief on your account."
